Solid State · SOLI-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Solid State memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Solid State was written: 51,363 words, against 13,845 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 24,053 words · written 22 October 2026, 17:41 to 18:04 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Solid State plc (SOLI-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-22 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst answering the second set. Fiscal years end 31 March: FY26 is the year to March 2026 (reported 29 June 2026); FY27 is the year now running, with the H1 FY27 results due 15 December 2026 and a Capital Markets Day in November 2026. Money is sterling unless stated. "Consensus" means the FactSet median at 22 October 2026, which rests on one visible broker (Cavendish) for FY27 to FY29; Zeus Capital (joint broker from October 2026) and Berenberg also publish. The share price used is 162.5p (22 October 2026), market capitalisation about £98.5m.

A note the Consumer should carry into every model line

Three facts organise everything below. First, FY26 revenue of £154.1m contained a £23.3m NATO Support and Procurement Agency communications programme that had been deferred from FY25 and will not repeat, so the recurring FY26 base is about £131m and the FY27 consensus of £156m to £157.5m requires roughly 20% growth on that base, not the 1.5% to 3% the headline suggests. Second, the group's profit is set by the utilisation of a fixed cost base (the Ashchurch integrated systems facility, antenna and battery capacity, and a group overhead that grew £8.7m in FY26) against a revenue line that moves in programme-sized lumps: FY24 10.4% operating margin, FY25 4.8%, FY26 6.2%. Third, management's own long-term incentive plan granted in May 2026 vests between FY28 adjusted EPS of 11.5p and 16.0p, which brackets the sell-side's FY28 figure of 12.7p to 13.1p and tells the Consumer what management itself regards as the floor and the stretch for the year ending March 2028.

External change forces

C5510 questions
C-0162C-0248C-0345C-0458C-0532C-0652C-0745C-0860C-0955C-1055

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external forces are the strongest part of this case and the reason it stays in the funnel. Defence rearmament is structural at the budget level and precisely targeted at Solid State's products; the industrial destock has ended and peers are already printing order growth of 20% to 30%; AI infrastructure creates a small new demand leg. Against that, input supply has turned from tailwind to headwind in the last six months (memory inflation, lead times, unhedged dollars), competitive intensity is rising in the 39% of revenue that is distribution, and the conversion of defence budgets to orders is running late (Defence Investment Plan unpublished, United States stopgaps). The practical steer for the model: revenue growth of mid to high single digits on the recurring £131m base is well supported externally; the 20% the consensus needs for FY27 depends on dated defence awards; gross margin should carry a 100 to 200 basis point range for input inflation; and the next two reporting periods will show an optical revenue decline that the external forces do not cause and cannot cure.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and visibility

C62

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Solid State sells into three end-market clocks that run at different speeds, and the visibility is distributed in the wrong order: the largest market has the least order-level visibility.

Defence and security (47% of FY26 revenue, £72.5m, up from £57.8m and from roughly 30% of revenue in FY25) is in a structural uplift. The UK is committed to 2.5% of GDP by 2027, European NATO members are moving toward 2.5% to 3.0%, and the UK Strategic Defence Review's 20/40/40 doctrine (20% crewed, 40% reusable uncrewed, 40% expendable) points procurement precisely at tactical communications, ruggedised edge computing and battery packs for uncrewed platforms, which are Steatite's and Custom Power's products. At the budget level this has more than two years of visibility, so it qualifies as structural. At the order level the visibility is six to eighteen months at best: the UK Defence Investment Plan is still unpublished as of October 2026, over 150 United States programmes have been delayed by stopgap budgets, and the company has already lived through a deferral (November 2024) that removed £20m of expected revenue in one month. The Consumer should model defence demand as a rising trend with a wide timing band, not as a smooth line.

Industrial and medical components (Components division, 39% of revenue, £59.8m) are at the bottom of a cyclical destocking that management says ended in H2 FY26: book-to-bill stabilised at about 1:1, lead times returned to 12 to 16 weeks and then began extending again, and peers show the turn more clearly (discoverIE organic orders +31% in Q1 FY27 and +22% in H1 FY27 with book-to-bill 1.12 to 1.15; XP Power book-to-bill 1.67 in Q3 2026). The sector digest expects a six to eighteen month restocking pulse that plateaus by the fourth quarter of 2026. Visibility is twelve to sixteen weeks of orders. This is cyclical, not structural, and worth perhaps mid to high single-digit growth in Components over FY27 and FY28.

The third clock is new and positive: AI data-centre power switching (Durakool contactors, multi-million dollar United States orders), edge AI processing (the DeepX franchise), and autonomous-platform batteries (over $20m of Power orders in the last four months of FY26, $7.4m of Custom Power wins announced November 2025). These are early, unquantified at group level, and should be modelled as upside to the Power and Components lines rather than as a base-case driver.

Judgement: demand is improving across all three clocks compared with the last twelve to eighteen months (the FY25 collapse and the FY26 catch-up), but the piece that decides the operating margin (defence programme conversion) is the piece the company cannot time. Two years of visibility exists only at the budget level.

C-02

Competitive intensity: extent and direction

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Intensity is moving in opposite directions in the two halves of the business, and the net is a modest rise.

In the accredited defence and medical niches (Systems and Power, roughly 61% of revenue), intensity is stable to easing. Qualification (AS9100, ISO 13485, Official Secrets Act clearances, Secure by Design), seven to thirty year design-in lifecycles, and the new customer preference for sovereign UK and United States supply all narrow the field of credible competitors. Steatite's position as the leading UK partner for Persistent Systems' MPU5 radios and Wave Relay networks is, as far as the evidence shows, uncontested for Project CAIN.

In components distribution (Solsta, 39% of revenue) intensity is rising. Semiconductor makers such as Texas Instruments sell direct through online portals; low-cost Asian optoelectronics makers are targeting the medical laser channel where Gooch & Housego has already conceded margin pressure; Volex's consumer business lost 8% organically to Chinese pricing in Europe. The counterweight is that expert checks put over 80% of distributor-supplier relationships as stable.

Two developments in the last six months deserve the Consumer's attention. First, larger acquirers are buying into Solid State's exact niches: discoverIE paid £40m for Trival Antene (Slovenian defence antennas, a direct competitor to Q-Par) and £50m for 3Gmetalworx (electromagnetic shielding), Diploma paid $170m for CDM (defence interconnect), and TT Electronics is the subject of a collapsed Cicor bid. This raises the resources behind competitors in defence electronics, but it also prices the category and makes a £98m-capitalisation accredited supplier at 0.7x net debt to EBITDA a plausible target. Second, in battery packs the alternatives are widening (customers can source cells, packs and electronics separately), which caps pricing in standard products even where Custom Power keeps an engineering edge.

Judgement: a modest rise in intensity concentrated where Solid State's margins are already lowest. For the model this argues for holding Components operating margins near 4% to 5% rather than assuming the 7% to 9% divisional target arrives early.

C-03

Disruption force: strengthening or disproved, and direction

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

The disruption force is artificial intelligence, and over the last six months it has strengthened on the side that hurts Solid State before the side that helps it.

The hurting side is input supply: AI data-centre builds are absorbing memory and mature-node semiconductor capacity, so lead times that had normalised to 12 to 16 weeks in H1 FY26 began extending again near the FY26 year-end, memory prices are rising sharply (expert work expects DRAM and HBM prices to rise at 10% to 15% a year), and the company itself warns that some price rises could make customer programmes temporarily unviable. For an integrator of memory-dense ruggedised systems this is a direct cost and timing disruption, and it is why inventory rose to £30.5m with more strategic stock promised for FY27.

The helping side is demand: Durakool contactors specified into AI data-centre power distribution (multi-million dollar United States orders), the DeepX edge-AI franchise, and ruggedised edge computing for industrial vision. These are real but immaterial to group revenue today.

The longer-dated disruptions act in different places. Agentic design tools (Cadence ChipStack, Flux, Cofactr) threaten to commoditise the application-engineering support that is Solsta's value-add over several years. Software-defined autonomy entrants (Anduril's Lattice, Shield AI) threaten to pull value from rugged hardware toward mission software, while cheaper disposable drones shorten programme lives and erode the protection of long qualification cycles. Against this, every autonomous system needs more power conversion and more secure communications, which is where Power and Steatite sit, and the copper-to-optical transition that worries Volex is irrelevant here.

Judgement: disruption is strengthening, acts on cost first and demand second, and is two-sided over three years. For the 18-month model it is a gross-margin and working-capital risk; for the four-year model it is a modest demand tailwind in Power and a modest value-add erosion in Components.

C-04

Political or regulatory change, and how it bears on the company

C58

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

This is the strongest single external force on the company, positive in direction and negative in timing.

Direction: the Strategic Defence Review prioritises exactly Solid State's products; the UK 2.5% of GDP commitment and NATO's trajectory raise the pool; "Made in USA" procurement mandates and tariff friction favour the Q-Par USA and Custom Power Fountain Valley footprint; export controls, ITAR and security accreditations raise barriers that Solid State already clears. The appointment of Victor Chavez (former chief executive of Thales UK, who ran £1.2bn of defence programmes) as Chair from 27 June 2026 is a direct bet on converting this political tailwind into awards.

Timing: the Defence Investment Plan is still delayed, slowing the conversion of committed spending into contracts; the November 2024 deferral showed that a review can move 60% of a year's EPS between periods; United States stopgap budgets have delayed over 150 programmes; UK export-control rules changed in May and December 2025, with refusal risk on Common High Priority List items; the Autumn Budget's employer National Insurance and minimum wage changes added over £0.5m of annual cost.

Judgement: the political force is large, structural and favourable over three years; over the next six months its net effect on reported numbers is decided by the date of the Defence Investment Plan and of the Project CAIN follow-on award, neither of which the company controls. The model should treat the political tailwind as raising the probability-weighted revenue path while widening the timing variance around it.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind

❌ D32

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A headwind, and worse than twelve months ago.

Costs: memory and semiconductor prices are rising, initially in memory; cost of sales was £102.5m in FY26 (about two-thirds of revenue), so a 5% rise in bought-in component cost that is not recovered would remove roughly £5m, or more than half of adjusted operating profit. The company's mechanisms are back-to-back pricing, non-cancellable orders, customer deposits (contract liabilities rose to £6.3m from £5.8m) and milestone payments, so the base case is pass-through with a lag and some programmes deferred rather than lost. Zeus holds FY27 gross margin at 33.2% against 33.5% in FY26, which assumes the pass-through works.

Availability: power-management IC lead times have run to 40 weeks and mature-node parts to 52 weeks; Project CAIN's deliveries were exposed to memory availability and the company says supply bottlenecks will require continued inventory investment through FY27.

Concentration: single-source exposure on advanced microprocessors and specialised sensors; Taiwan acknowledged as an unalterable critical source; the exclusive Persistent Systems relationship, which is both the moat and the dependency for the tactical radio business; franchise lines (Digi, Excelitas, Silicon Labs) that can be terminated unilaterally; and no supplier-concentration disclosure to test any of it. Mitigants: safety stock, second-source qualification, a "China plus one" shift of own-brand manufacture to India.

Currency compounds the input position: about 40% of purchases are in dollars and there were zero forward contracts at 31 March 2026, so a stronger dollar raises bought-in cost with only natural matching (dollar sales, dollar invoicing where possible) as protection; the disclosed sensitivity is +£0.67m of pre-tax profit per 10% sterling strengthening and -£0.82m per 10% weakening. Broker forecasts for sterling over twelve to twenty-four months diverge (1.28 to 1.39 against the dollar).

Judgement: net headwind on both cost and availability, with pass-through that is credible but lagged, and a cash cost (inventory) in any case. The model should carry a gross-margin range of roughly 32% to 34% for FY27 and the working-capital build that protects it.

C-06

The company's own revenue and demand cycle, and whether it is turning

C52

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

The reported cycle and the underlying cycle have come apart, and the Consumer must model them separately.

Reported: revenue £163.3m FY24, £125.1m FY25 (-23%), £154.1m FY26 (+23%), consensus £156m FY27 (+1.5%), £162m FY28 (+3%), £173m FY29 (+7%, one broker). On this reading the cycle peaked and is flattening.

Underlying: strip the £23.3m deferred communications programme out of FY26 and the recurring base is about £131m, which grew roughly 4% to 7% in H1 FY26 on a constant-currency basis. FY27 consensus of £156m to £157.5m therefore requires about 20% growth on the recurring base, to be supplied by Project CAIN (£8.0m delivered in Q1 FY27), the Power order intake (over $20m in the last four months of FY26 plus $7.4m), AI data-centre and edge-AI components, and the industrial restock. The order book supports this: £102.4m at 31 May 2026, flat on the headline but £18m higher after removing the £19m NSPA order from the prior-year figure, with most of it due to ship within eighteen months.

Phasing is the insight that matters most for the next results. H1 FY26 revenue was £85.7m including the £23.3m programme; H1 FY27 will not have an equivalent, so H1 FY27 revenue is likely to be reported down year on year (a figure in the low to mid £70m range is consistent with the full-year consensus and a second-half weighting). The market has been given the comparator but may still react to the optical decline on 15 December 2026. Operating margin in H1 FY27 is the better test: the Earnings Review sets 5.5% as the level below which the thesis is at risk, against 6.5% in H1 FY26.

Judgement: the underlying cycle is in an early to middle upturn (order book up, three divisions growing, destock over); the reported cycle is flat because a one-off is washing out. The turn is real but it is being masked, and the risk is that a slip in defence timing makes the underlying upturn invisible for two more reporting periods.

C-07

Macro force: on the company, its sector and its industry, and where it bites hardest

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

The macro regime (United States ten-year yield about 5.1%, a hiking path pulled forward, a risk-off market with Fragility rated Double Underweight, and a market that "refuses re-ratings") bites hardest at the share, then at the sector, and least at the company's earnings.

At the share: a £98m AIM-listed company trading £0.3m a day, with one visible broker and a thesis that needs a multiple recovery from an 11x 24-month-forward P/E, is exactly the asset the current regime discounts. The 15% fall over three months to mid-October with no estimate cut is the regime at work. Interest-rate sensitivity of the multiple is far larger than of the profit.

At the sector: manufacturing PMIs have turned (UK 51.7, Eurozone 52.7 in August 2026), which supports the components restock, while a late-cycle rates squeeze and European consumer weakness argue the restock plateaus rather than compounds. Industrial capital expenditure caution from tariffs dampens the automation end-market.

At the company: net debt is £4.2m excluding leases, the revolving credit facility floats at SONIA, EURIBOR or SOFR plus 2.3% to 2.6%, net interest was £1.2m in FY26, so a 100 basis point move in rates is worth about £0.1m of pre-tax profit, trivial. Sterling matters more (see C-05). UK fiscal policy adds the £0.5m National Insurance cost. Defence budgets are the least rate-sensitive demand the company has.

Judgement: macro is a valuation and liquidity force for this stock, not an earnings force; the earnings model needs sterling and the industrial PMI, not the rate path, while the valuation work needs the rate path above all.

C-08

A key industry or sector driver accelerating the company's demand

C60

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Yes, two of the sector's named drivers act directly on Solid State, and a third is emerging.

The first is rearmament reaching the qualified component tier: the sector note of 13 October 2026 records that qualification of one and a half to four years keeps incumbents in place and margins hold, with Huber+Suhner's Industry segment orders up 41.7% at a 19.6% margin and TT Electronics' European book-to-bill at 135% in 2025 against a sustainable 100% to 115%. Solid State's defence revenue rose 25% in FY26 and its Power division took over $20m of autonomy orders in four months; the driver is accelerating its demand.

The second is the electronics restocking pulse: lead times back to 12 to 16 weeks and now extending, 28% of industrial distributors reporting stock too low, book-to-bill above one across the cohort. Solid State's Components division grew 8.1% in FY26 after a 14.6% fall in H1 FY25, so it is participating, but with the sector caveat that only designed-in parts reprice while standard parts pass cost on late, and Solsta's mix is weighted to franchised third-party lines with own-brand (Durakool, Gateway, Pacer) still a minority.

The third is data-centre power demand, which the industry digest treats as one exposure across nine sectors; Solid State's exposure through Durakool contactors is small but growing and comes with the same single-hyperscaler-pause risk the digest warns about.

What is weaker than at the cohort leaders is the design-in evidence: discoverIE discloses a £385m lifetime-value design-win bank; Solid State discloses none, so the Consumer cannot measure how much of the sector's "qualified design slot" driver it holds.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

C55

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Four, two positive and two negative.

Positive: (1) Capital-markets attention is broadening from a single-broker base. Berenberg initiated in December 2025 (Buy; the reports carry the target as 150p in one place and 240p in another, which the Consumer should not reconcile), Zeus was appointed joint broker alongside Cavendish in October 2026, and a Capital Markets Day is scheduled for November 2026. For a stock whose multiple has sat at a 15% to 22% discount to peers partly because nobody independent covers it, this is a dated route to re-rating. (2) Consolidation of UK and European defence electronics (discoverIE's Trival and 3Gmetalworx, Diploma's CDM, the Cicor and Volex approaches to TT Electronics) is pricing accredited niche suppliers at 9x to 11x EBIT and above, against Solid State's enterprise value of about £96m on consensus FY27 EBITDA of £14.3m (about 6.7x).

Negative: (3) The market regime pays for proven, self-funded, simple cases and refuses re-ratings; Solid State's case is a re-rating case with a demand-timing dependency, so it fights the regime until the earnings prove themselves. (4) The United States policy backdrop is paying for European capital goods faster than Europe is, which helps the Q-Par USA and Custom Power footprint but exposes the group to tariff administration (tariffs are paid at import and recovered with a lag) and to Section 899-type tax proposals management declined to discuss.

C-10

Headwinds and tailwinds by level, and the overall external picture

C55

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Value chain: tailwinds on the demand side (defence budgets, autonomy, restock, AI power and edge), headwinds on the supply side (memory inflation, lead times, single sourcing, unhedged dollar purchasing), with the route to market neutral in Systems and Power and weakening in Components. Net at the value-chain level: modestly positive, with the supply headwind the more certain of the two over six months.

Industry (industrials and capital goods): the market is paying for scarce qualified capacity and proven order conversion and not for channels or crews; Solid State owns qualified capacity (Ashchurch, accreditations, the Persistent partnership) but has not yet proven conversion at a steady cadence. Net: neutral to positive, conditional on conversion.

Sector (engineered components and electronics manufacturing): the restock turn is already in the price of the quality names (discoverIE at 18.5x), defence component shares follow award timing not budgets, and Solid State has pulled back 15% in three months on exactly that timing. Net: the sector dynamics favour the company's demand and have already punished its share for the timing, which is a better entry position than six months ago if the timing resolves.

Overall external change picture: more positive than at any point in the last eighteen months on demand, more negative than twelve months ago on inputs, and dominated for the next six months by two dates the company does not set, the Defence Investment Plan and the Project CAIN follow-on award. The tailwinds exceed the headwinds in magnitude over three years; over six months they are roughly balanced because the headwinds are certain and the tailwinds are dated but unconfirmed.

Internal change forces

C5013 questions
C-1158C-1252C-1350C-1454C-1550C-1655C-1742C-1848C-1942C-2052C-8640C-8745C-2150

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal changes are real, coherent and already paid for, and none of them moves the numbers on its own. A forced succession produced an internal chief executive and an external defence-industry chair; the structure was simplified into three accountable divisions with margin targets; the commercial model was redirected toward programmes and own-brand products; capacity, systems and people were added ahead of revenue; and the broker base and a Capital Markets Day were arranged. What did not change: no numeric guidance, a margin target quietly withdrawn, no acquisition step-up, no capital return, no real pricing, no reduction of the distribution leg. The net effect for the model is a higher fixed cost base with higher operating leverage and a flat near-term margin; the internal changes raise the payoff from the external forces without being able to time them. The one internal event that could change the case inside six months is the Capital Markets Day, which can supply the bridge the sell side has never had or reframe the 2030 ambition; the Consumer should hold both possibilities.

C-11

Changes in the chief executive, other key leaders and key actors, and what they signal

C58

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

The leadership has turned over at the top in a seven-month window, by circumstance rather than by design, and the replacements signal continuity of strategy with a deliberate upgrade in defence access.

Chief executive: Gary Marsh, chief executive for over twenty years, died in November 2025. John Macmichael, who had run the Components division (Solsta) for twenty years and sat on the board since 2010, became interim chief executive at once and permanent chief executive on 26 March 2026. His base salary moved from £200,000 to £261,000, still bottom-decile for the sector. The Leadership report calls his lack of external public-company executive experience "the single most fragile capability marker" and notes his career is UK-centred, which matters for a group whose growth plan runs through the United States.

Chair: Nigel Rogers retired after seven years; Victor Chavez CBE, former chief executive of Thales UK, joined as Deputy Chair on 1 January 2026 and became Chair on 27 June 2026. He bought 12,500 shares at 159p in December 2025, the only open-market insider purchase in the period. This is the most consequential appointment: it adds Ministry of Defence procurement relationships at exactly the point where the case turns on Project CAIN follow-ons and the Defence Investment Plan.

Below the board: Jon Baxter promoted to run Solsta; Dave Crossman promoted to Managing Director of the new Power division after restoring United States order intake at Custom Power; a Group Chief Technology Officer (Chris Beynon), a Chief Relationship Officer (Matthew Richards, who also runs Systems) and a United States Chief Sales Officer (Stephen Brown) appointed; Sam Smith took the Audit Committee chair. The finance director, Peter James, has been in post since 2017 and did not change, which protects the cash and balance-sheet discipline through the transition.

Key actors: no activist, no controlling shareholder, no insider selling; Zeus joined Cavendish as joint broker in October 2026.

What it signals: management says the strategy has not changed and the new chief executive's themes are "focus and pace"; the signal is an internal operator promoted to keep the machine running, paired with an external chair chosen to open doors. The risk the Consumer should carry is that the group is now led by long-serving insiders at low pay during a United States expansion, with the chair's attention needed for defence procurement rather than integration.

C-12

Change in strategy or strategic priorities, and how well articulated and resourced

C52

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

The strategy is unchanged in substance and sharper in execution; it is well resourced, arguably ahead of revenue, which is the margin problem of FY27.

What changed: Power was separated from Systems as a third reporting division (FY26), making the battery business's economics visible (gross margin 35.8% from 31.7%, adjusted operating margin 5.3% from 1.0%); the Components businesses were unified under the Solsta brand and a Solsta Embedded consultative unit was formed; the four strategic priorities were narrowed to delivering multi-capability programmes at scale, building longer-term revenue visibility, expanding capabilities with discipline and promoting group capabilities; the "3Cs" (cooperation, collaboration, communication) and "3Ps" (products, projects, programme partnerships) frameworks were introduced to move revenue from one-off project shipments toward repeatable products and through-life programme partnerships; a Commercial Project Office centralises tender bids; capital allocation was explicitly tilted to organic utilisation of Ashchurch and Leominster over large acquisitions.

Articulation: clear at the level of intent and structure, weak at the level of numbers. There is no disclosed share of revenue that is "programme" or "product" rather than "project", no utilisation figure for Ashchurch beyond the 50% the Risks report cites, and the annual report dropped the explicit 12% margin target in favour of "margins broadly stable in FY27 as investment continues". The Capital Markets Day in November 2026 is the first opportunity to put numbers on the framework, and the Consumer should expect it to do so.

Resourcing: capital expenditure of £3.9m in FY26 with over £7.5m planned across FY27 and FY28; adjusted selling, general and administrative costs up £8.7m to £42.0m in FY26 on compensation, incentives and Ashchurch overheads; management and administrative headcount up from 91 to 156 (while selling and manufacturing roles fell from 338 to 320); a group CRM, AI tools, apprenticeships and a profit-share scheme. The business is resourced for £250m of revenue while generating £154m, which is why the operating margin is 6.2% against a 9.2% FY21 to FY24 average.

Judgement: a sensible, coherent programme-led strategy, resourced early and articulated qualitatively. For the model, the resourcing means the fixed cost base is already largely in place and incremental revenue should drop through at a high rate, while a revenue shortfall hits the margin hard.

C-13

Guidance raised, or mid-term targets and capital-markets ambitions set or raised

C50

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Partly, and with one quiet retreat.

Set and reiterated: the 2030 ambition of at least 20p of sustainable adjusted diluted EPS (from 11.0p in FY26), a £250m revenue milestone (from £154.1m), a circa 20% total shareholder return compound ambition, and at the FY26 results new divisional medium-term operating margin targets of 12.5% to 15.0% for Systems (14.3% in FY26 with the communications programme), 10.0% to 14.0% for Power (5.3%) and 7.0% to 9.0% for Components (4.0%). The group 12% operating margin figure appears in the earlier materials and in the memo but was not repeated in the FY26 annual report, which instead guides margins broadly stable for FY27; this is the retreat, and it matters because the 20p target cannot be reached on FY28-type margins of 6.6%.

Raised: the April 2026 pre-close lifted FY26 expectations to revenue of not less than £150.0m and adjusted pre-tax profit of £7.9m (from a £145.0m and £7.2m consensus), and the outturn beat that; Zeus then raised FY27 EPS by 12.5% to 11.6p and introduced FY28 at 12.7p. The dividend was raised 10% to 2.75p.

Not raised: there is no formal numeric guidance for FY27; the company defines market expectations as the average of Cavendish, Berenberg and Zeus and speaks to them. The long-term incentive plan granted in May 2026 sets FY28 adjusted EPS of 11.5p as the threshold and 16.0p as the ceiling; the threshold is only 0.5p above the FY26 outturn, which reads as management protecting itself against another timing slip rather than as a raised ambition.

Judgement: ambitions are set and visible, the near-term guide is flat, and the explicit margin target has softened. The Capital Markets Day is where this is resolved; the Consumer should hold two cases, one where the 12% is restated with a dated bridge and one where 2030 is reframed around revenue and programmes.

C-14

Improvement in execution, operational measures, governance and cost discipline

C54

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Modest and genuine improvement in structure and reporting, offset by a cost base that is growing faster than revenue.

Execution and operational: the Power division's margin recovery (1.0% to 5.3%) after exiting low-engineering-value assembly and glass-to-metal seal work; Active Silicon consolidated to one site at Langley; the Ashchurch facility stood up and awarded its first complex cabinet contract (DSTL, £1.65m to £1.7m); Project CAIN's initial order delivered in Q1 FY27 despite memory constraints; cash conversion of 141% and net debt down to £4.2m. The revenue-recognition and working-capital flags (work in progress up 147% to £10.96m, inventories £30.5m, bill-and-hold arrangements described as judgemental) cut the other way and have not been resolved by primary disclosure.

Governance: the Audit Committee chair passed to an independent director (Sam Smith) after ISS concerns about overboarding; the ESG committee is standardising policies and risk management across subsidiaries for FY27; a Group Executive Board institutionalised succession before it was needed and worked when it was; the remuneration committee lapsed 600,375 options (and 1,027,500 in total) when targets were missed rather than resetting them, and bonuses pay only when the group beats the market expectations in place when targets were set. Still missing: an internal audit function; independent directors are only 43% of the board excluding the chair against 67% at discoverIE; the auditor has served over ten years.

Cost discipline: £1.0m of annualised savings executed in late 2024 and over £3.0m of overhead savings during FY25 show the lever exists; but FY26 added £8.7m of SG&A and 65 administrative heads, and FY27 carries a further £0.5m of National Insurance and wage cost. The Leadership report's "71% surge in administrative personnel" is the right thing to watch.

Judgement: standards are rising in governance and divisional accountability; cost discipline is being deliberately relaxed to fund growth. The margin consequence is the point for the model, not the governance.

C-15

Reducing the perimeter or composition

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Only at the edges, and the net perimeter is widening.

Reductions: Custom Power USA exited commodity assembly contracts and discontinued glass-to-metal seal production, taking a £2.7m goodwill impairment in FY25 and a short-term volume hit for a 400 basis point gross margin gain; Solsta discontinued low-margin legacy United States manufacturing lines, lifting Components gross margin from 25.0% to 26.8%; Active Silicon's facilities were consolidated into one. No disposal, demerger or exit of a business line is evidenced or mooted; the Components division, which carries the structurally weakest economics, is treated as a procurement and diagnostic funnel for the group rather than as a candidate for exit (contrast TT Electronics, which is evaluating a Components divestment).

Expansion: five acquisitions in five years, the most recent Gateway (£1.4m) and Q-Par USA (up to $2.1m) in H2 FY25, plus the Braze asset purchase; new United States production for antennas is planned.

Judgement: composition is improving by pruning low-value work inside divisions, which is the right action for margin, but there is no simplification of the group and no sign management regards distribution as non-core. For predictability, the perimeter is already narrow (see C-79); the composition change to watch is the share of own-brand and programme revenue, which is not disclosed.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Yes, in adjacent rings, with modest capital and one precedent of integration pain.

Geographic: the United States is the thrust: Q-Par USA (approved-supplier status with United States primes, a plan for onshore antenna production in Fountain Valley), Custom Power's California site as the "Made in USA" battery base with a new United States Chief Sales Officer and a vertical sales structure, and a multi-currency revolving facility with Comerica. North America was 23% of FY26 revenue (£35.5m). Supply-side geography is also moving: own-brand Durakool manufacture is shifting to India under "China plus one". United Kingdom revenue has fallen from 85% of sales in 2016 to 45%.

Product: AI data-centre power switching, edge AI (DeepX franchise), sovereign military battery systems with Volklec, through-life support and spiral upgrades under Persistent Systems deployments, integrated vision-and-computing packages for uncrewed platforms from the Active Silicon and Steatite combination, and larger Tier 2 sub-system work from Ashchurch.

How sensibly: each move shares customers, channels or skills with the core (Towson's test for an adjacency that works), the capital is small (Q-Par cost under $2.1m; Gateway £1.4m), and the Q-Par integration is reported as refuting concerns about United States execution. The counter-evidence is Custom Power: acquired in August 2022 for up to $45m, it suffered management changes, cultural mismatch and the £2.7m impairment before Dave Crossman's restructuring restored order intake; the Leadership report flags that the chief executive's UK-centred background limits hands-on familiarity with United States manufacturing regulation. The Risks report rates United States integration failure as high plausibility.

Judgement: sensible adjacencies pursued with discipline on price; the execution risk is concentrated in the United States and has already cost money once. For the model, United States growth should be a Power and Systems revenue driver with a lower margin until Fountain Valley production is proven.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Latent to nominal. There is evidence of pass-through and of mix-led margin gain, and no evidence of real price increases above cost inflation.

Evidence of pass-through: all material semiconductor and raw-material increases in the last cycle were passed on through back-to-back pricing; United States tariffs are passed through in full in most cases with administration charges, at a cost of about 10 basis points of gross margin; non-cancellable terms and deposits are demanded on sensitive orders. Evidence of mix: gross margin rose 200 basis points to 33.5% in FY26 on higher design-in content, own-brand products (Gateway at 40% to 43% gross margin, Durakool) and the exit of low-margin Power assembly.

Evidence against real pricing: the Earnings Review states plainly that the disclosures "emphasised engineering content, customer relationships and higher-value programmes rather than quantified price increases", so margin recovery rests on mix and utilisation, not price; H1 FY26 gross margin was flat at 31.0% before the second-half mix effect; Components operating margin was 4.0% against 4.1%; sector peers that have shown real pricing (Synectics gross margin 41% to 47.8%; discoverIE's customers accept 3% to 5% annual increases on parts that are 3% to 5% of system cost) have no equivalent in Solid State's record.

What is increasingly noted: Zeus observes that component inflation "could lead to price increases on customer projects", and the memory-price surge creates a window in which designed-in and allocated parts reprice (the sector's "use, refill or price" split). Solid State as a specialist distributor is positioned to pass on memory inflation with a lag; whether it keeps any of it is unproven.

Judgement: model price as cost recovery with a one to two quarter lag, not as a margin driver; the real-price opportunity is latent in memory and in through-life support pricing, and the second set should test it.

C-18

Stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how disciplined and well integrated

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Stepping down, deliberately, with a disciplined screen and a mixed integration record.

Pace: five acquisitions in five years (Willow and Active Silicon in FY22, Custom Power August 2022 for up to $45m, Gateway October 2024 for £1.4m, Q-Par USA November 2024 for up to $2.1m), none in FY26, and a stated near-term pipeline of modest antenna-related bolt-ons because organic opportunities are judged more attractive. Balance-sheet capacity exists (net debt £4.2m, a £15.0m facility with a £10.0m accordion, which the Earnings Review separately describes as a £30m multi-currency syndicated facility; the Consumer should not reconcile the two).

Discipline: targets must be strategically and culturally aligned, earnings-enhancing in year one and supported by a value-creation plan; 47 North American businesses were screened before Custom Power. Gateway lifted FY26 pre-tax profit forecasts by 1.5% and brought 40% to 43% gross-margin products; Q-Par's historical third-party revenue was only $0.4m, so its value is strategic access, not earnings.

Integration: Custom Power is the cautionary case (management changes, a £2.7m impairment, a three-year recovery); Gateway and Q-Par integrated within twelve months. By the Cockroach rule for serial acquirers the Consumer should assume a 30% to 50% conditional probability of a second integration problem within the United States expansion, though with only one deal of size in five years the base rate is loosely applicable.

Judgement: M&A is not a change force in the next eighteen months; it is an option on the balance sheet for FY28 onward and should be modelled as zero in the base case with the facility headroom noted.

C-19

Greater use of the balance sheet, or stepped-up shareholder returns, or either mooted

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Neither, and the absence is itself a signal.

Balance sheet: net debt fell to £4.2m excluding leases (£10.1m including £5.9m of lease liabilities), 0.7x EBITDA on the FactSet basis and below 0.5x on the company's; Zeus models net debt of £2.1m at March 2027 and net cash of £0.2m at March 2028. The facility was restructured with an accordion, which is capacity, not use. Capital expenditure is rising (over £7.5m over two years) and inventory will absorb cash through FY27, so the balance sheet is being used for organic capacity and strategic stock, not for acquisitions or returns.

Returns: the dividend was rebased to 2.5p in FY25 and raised 10% to 2.75p in FY26, covered 3.5x to 4.0x; consensus has 2.9p, 3.3p and 4.0p for FY27 to FY29. Treasury share purchases were £0.2m in FY26, for employee schemes only. No buyback has been announced or mooted, despite a 24-month-forward P/E at the first percentile of its five-year range and a path to net cash. Peers show the contrast: Volex launched a £40m (later £20m) buyback at 0.8x leverage.

Judgement: a conservative balance sheet held for operating flexibility, with an unexercised option to buy back stock at an unusually low multiple. The Consumer should model no buyback, a dividend growing with earnings at about 25% to 35% payout, and should note that a buyback announcement at the Capital Markets Day would be a novel positive the market is not expecting.

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces, present or plausible

C52

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Present: (1) The November 2026 Capital Markets Day, the first since the three-division structure, where divisional margin bridges, Ashchurch utilisation and the programme-revenue share could be disclosed for the first time. (2) Zeus's appointment as joint broker and Berenberg's initiation, broadening the analytical base from one house. (3) The Chair's Ministry of Defence and prime-contractor relationships, applied to Project CAIN follow-ons, NSPA framework follow-ons and Tier 1 programme bids. (4) The through-life support, training and spiral-upgrade revenue that follows the CAIN and NSPA hardware deliveries, which begins to convert one-off shipments into a recurring base. (5) A group-wide profit-share scheme, a Commercial Project Office and a Group Chief Technology Officer coordinating cross-divisional bids, which are the mechanisms behind "programmes at scale".

Plausible: (6) A move from AIM to the Main Market, which Volex used in July 2026 to widen the register; nothing is announced. (7) A bolt-on antenna acquisition using the facility headroom. (8) An approach from a consolidator, given the category pricing described in C-02 and C-09.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments, present or plausible

❌ D40

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Present: (1) The fixed cost base has been built ahead of revenue (SG&A up £8.7m, administrative headcount up 71%, over £7.5m of capital expenditure to come), so FY27 margin is guided flat and any revenue slip deleverages. (2) The explicit 12% margin target has been withdrawn from the annual report. (3) The May 2026 long-term incentive threshold of 11.5p for FY28 signals that management regards near-flat EPS over two years as a plausible outcome. (4) Working-capital quality: inventories £30.5m with more to come, work in progress up 147%, a bill-and-hold revenue-recognition policy, capitalised development of £1.11m to £1.25m, and an adjusted-to-reported pre-tax gap of £2.8m on £8.6m; the Risks report's competing-hypotheses analysis names the "false friend" profile the winner. (5) Zero currency hedging against 40% dollar purchasing. (6) A single customer at 17% of revenue in FY26 against a historical norm of none above 10%.

Plausible: (7) The Capital Markets Day resets 2030 (a revenue-led reframing without the margin). (8) A second United States integration problem. (9) Memory inflation makes a programme unviable and defers revenue into FY28. (10) The departure of the finance director, which the Leadership report rates as highly disruptive given his transactional role.

C-87

Scope for change indicated by controlling actors

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

None indicated. There is no activist, no controlling shareholder, no short interest above the disclosure threshold, no takeover approach disclosed, and no shareholder resolution contested. Directors hold roughly 1% to 2% of the equity (the chief executive's holding is recorded as both 546,207 and 150,053 shares in the sources; the Consumer should not reconcile), the register is institutional and broad, and the single-class structure carries no defences. The absence of a forcing actor means strategy and capital allocation change only when management chooses, which, given a conservative balance sheet and an unexercised buyback option at a trough multiple, is a mild negative for the pace of change; it is a mild positive for predictability, since nothing external will reshape the perimeter inside the forecast window. A consolidator approach is the only plausible external catalyst, and it is not signalled.

C-21

Strength, breadth and resourcing of the positive corporate changes

C50

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Broad, well-resourced and modest in force. The positive changes are structural and organisational (three accountable divisions, a programme-led commercial model, a defence-industry chair, a widening broker base, a dated Capital Markets Day) rather than financial (no raised guidance, no buyback, no acquisition step-up, no real pricing). They have been resourced in advance of the revenue they are meant to produce, which is precisely why the FY27 margin is flat and why the operating leverage, once revenue arrives, is high. The Consumer should carry a clear asymmetry: if the dated defence awards land, the internal changes already in place turn each incremental £10m of Systems revenue into roughly £3m of operating profit; if they slip, the same changes cost £2m to £3m a year of unabsorbed overhead with no offsetting action evidenced beyond the £1m of savings management found in late 2024.

Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4616 questions
C-2442C-2538C-2650C-2740C-2852C-2955C-3148C-8452C-3255C-3350C-3448C-3545C-3647C-3758C-3844C-3935

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry structure is supportive where Solid State earns its margin and unsupportive where it earns its volume. Qualified, design-in, sovereign defence and medical niches give Systems and Power protection on price and share, a benign competitive set, and a political tailwind, so the 60% of revenue in those divisions can be modelled with stable gross margins and an operating margin that depends on utilisation. Distribution gives Components fragmented, disintermediating economics with real Asian price competition, so the 39% of revenue there should be modelled at low single-digit operating margins rising only with restock volume and own-brand mix. The fragility sits above the company (concentrated suppliers, an exclusive radio partner, memory makers in allocation) and at the top of its customer list (one customer at 17%, a ministry as gatekeeper), not among its competitors. Solid State is a sub-tier supplier in a chain whose profit pools favour the designer and the integrator, which it partly is, and penalise the small distributor, which it also is. The company fits the sector's award-timing and fixed-cost-at-the-turn precedents, which makes it a candidate for a sharp but timing-dependent recovery, not for the compounder pattern the cohort's leaders have earned.

C-24

Rank by market share or size, and distance from the number one or two position

❌ D42

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Solid State is the smallest listed company in its cohort and holds number one or two positions only in narrow national sub-niches.

Group scale: FY26 revenue £154.1m against TT Electronics £481m, discoverIE £443m, Volex $1,243m and Diploma £1,525m; market capitalisation about £98m against Volex about $1.3bn and discoverIE over £700m. It is bottom-quartile on scale by any measure.

By activity: Steatite is the leading UK partner for Persistent Systems' tactical radios and the supplier of record for Project CAIN and the NSPA communications programme, so in the UK and NATO MPU5 channel it is number one; in ruggedised computing it is one of several accredited UK integrators behind global players such as Mercury Systems and General Micro Systems. Custom Power is a mid-sized custom battery-pack maker with a dual UK and United States footprint that single-site rivals lack, but it is far from the scale of the cell makers it partners (Saft, Molicel) or of large pack integrators. Solsta is a small specialist distributor in a market where the top twenty global players hold under 25%, so nobody is large and Solsta is not among the twenty. Q-Par is a niche antenna house; discoverIE's Trival is now a better-capitalised direct rival.

Distance from number one or two: in its chosen national defence niches it is at or near the top; in every market defined more broadly it is a long way from the leaders, and the leaders (Amphenol, TE Connectivity, Vishay) are not even its competitors because it avoids their stages.

C-25

Market concentration and the steepness of the share distribution

❌ D38

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Low concentration, flat distribution, in every market Solid State serves. Over 2,000 contract electronics manufacturers operate in Europe; the top twenty global industrial, electrical and electronic distributors hold a combined share under 25%, and a single distributor rarely exceeds low double digits in any territory; the top five automation and robotics providers hold 28%. There is no evidence that the top five hold more than 30% in Solid State's addressable niches, and no evidence of the top five holding five times the share of numbers six to ten. The exceptions are the upstream suppliers Solid State depends on, which are concentrated (foundries, memory makers, Persistent Systems as the sole source of MPU5 radios), so concentration sits where it hurts (supplier power) and not where it helps (pricing discipline among peers). The qualified defence and medical sub-niches behave more like local oligopolies because qualification limits credible bidders, which is the only structural support for pricing.

C-26

Stability of market shares and the share taken by new entrants

C50

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Stable in the qualified niches, eroding at the commodity edge, and poorly measured in both. Design-in lifecycles of seven to thirty years, requalification costs and over 80% stability in distributor-supplier relationships mean share in Systems and Power programmes moves only when programmes end or are cancelled; no source quantifies a share loss to a new entrant in those niches over three to five years. In distribution, suppliers going direct and low-cost Asian optoelectronics makers are taking share at the standard-parts end, and Volex's consumer business shows the mechanism (8% organic decline to Chinese pricing). In defence more broadly, new-model entrants (Anduril, Shield AI, Castelion) are winning United States programmes with vertically integrated, software-defined offers, but there is no evidence yet of them displacing a UK sub-tier supplier such as Steatite. The honest answer is that share data does not exist for these niches; the proxy is the order book, which rose £18m underlying in the year, and the 17% customer, which shows share can concentrate as well as fragment.

C-27

Consolidation of share toward the largest and the most advantaged, and whether the company is among them

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Share is consolidating toward scaled, well-financed acquirers, and Solid State is not one of them. The cohort's consolidators are discoverIE (£95m across three deals in six months, a database of 250 targets, a £240m revolver), Diploma (£1.6bn into 57 deals since 2019, CDM at $170m), Volex ($400m across twelve deals, Kepler SignalTek), and Cicor (which bought three TT sites and bid for the whole company). Each is buying accredited defence and medical niche businesses at 9x to 11x EBIT. The advantaged participants are those with accreditation, sovereign footprint and design-in depth; Solid State has the first two in the UK and is building them in the United States, so it is advantaged on capability and disadvantaged on scale, purchasing power and overhead absorption (£10.8m of revenue per manufacturing site at discoverIE is already bottom-decile; Solid State's thirteen locations on £154m is similar). Over three to five years the more likely direction is that Solid State is consolidated rather than consolidates, unless the Ashchurch and Power utilisation story lifts its own multiple enough to use paper.

C-28

Competitors' attitude to competition

C52

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Margin-first in the high-mix, low-volume and qualified niches; volume-first at the commodity end; and the attitude is set by the stage, not by the company.

Evidence of margin-first: XP Power exited RF power to focus on returns; Phoenix Mecano is streamlining to profitability targets; tier-two EMS specialists maintain pricing discipline in specialised programmes and avoid volume price wars; standard component makers see no advantage in low-volume high-mix work; Diploma's "elite operators refuse to compete on price". In the memory-inflation window, designed-in parts are repricing across the sector. Prices in these stages do not fall when volume falls; margins fall through under-absorption instead, which is what happened to Solid State in FY25 (gross margin held at 31.5% while operating margin halved).

Evidence of volume-first: Chinese manufacturers pricing aggressively into European consumer and optoelectronics; Volex described by a former TT Electronics director as bidding aggressively, squeezing suppliers and raising prices at renewal; second-tier cable assemblers pricing 5% to 8% below the tier-one leaders to win secondary-supplier status; semiconductor suppliers going direct. In these stages prices do fall with volume.

Solid State's exposure: Systems and Power sit in the margin-first stages; Solsta's franchised lines sit in the volume-first stage, which is why its operating margin is 4% and its target 7% to 9%.

C-29

Stability of that attitude, and the risk of a shark attack on pricing

C55

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Stable in the qualified niches over the last eighteen months to three years, with no evidence of reactive instability. The relevant competitors in defence electronics (discoverIE, TT Electronics, Diploma's CDM, Huber+Suhner) are themselves margin-expanding and acquisitive; none has an incentive to attack pricing in a market where qualification, not price, decides awards and where demand exceeds qualified capacity. The destabilising entrants (Anduril and the software-defined autonomy group) compete by vertical integration and speed rather than by cutting sub-system prices, so their threat is displacement over five years, not a price war over eighteen months. The one place a shark attack is plausible is distribution, where a large broadline distributor or a supplier going direct could underprice franchised lines, but that pressure is structural and continuous, not situational. Solid State itself shows weakness in the form of a flat H1 FY27 revenue comparison and thin coverage, but that is weakness visible to investors, not to competitors in a tender.

C-31

How supportive the industry structure is, and how fragile that support

❌ D48

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Supportive for about 60% of revenue and weak for about 40%, with the fragility sitting in the supplier tier rather than in the customer or competitor tiers.

Supportive: qualification and design-in barriers, long lifecycles, sovereign-supply preference, rising budgets, and a fragmented set of small rivals in the UK defence sub-tier give Systems and Power a structure in which margins are decided by utilisation and mix, not by price. The sector's own evidence is that qualified defence component makers keep their margins through cycles.

Weak: distribution is fragmented, disintermediating and exposed to Asian price competition; Components operating margins of 4% are the structure speaking.

Fragile: the supplier tier is concentrated (Persistent Systems exclusive, Taiwan, memory makers prioritising AI), and the customer tier is concentrated at the top (one customer at 17%, the Ministry of Defence as the gatekeeper for CAIN and the Defence Investment Plan). The structure therefore supports price and share while leaving timing and input cost outside the company's control, which is the pattern of a sub-tier supplier in a sovereign supply chain.

C-84

Historical precedents and empirical evidence on outperformance and underperformance, and the company's fit

C52

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

The sector and industry files carry six usable patterns.

(1) Qualified defence component shares follow award timing, not budgets: Solid State fell 23% in revenue and 60% in EPS expectations when a review paused awards, and the share fell 15% in three months in 2026 when the Defence Investment Plan slipped. The company fits this pattern exactly, in both directions.

(2) Fixed-cost makers see margins fall hard in a downturn and recover hardest at the turn; asset-light designers hold margins through it: discoverIE's margin rose 1.2 points while organic sales fell 7%; Renishaw's margin fell from 24.1% to 15.7%; Solid State's fell from 10.5% to 4.8%. Solid State is a fixed-cost maker in Systems and an asset-light distributor in Components, so the group geared down like a maker in FY25 and will gear up like one if volume returns; the share price outperformance in this pattern comes at the turn, which is now, if the turn is real.

(3) A restocking pulse runs six to eighteen months and only designed-in parts reprice: Solsta participates but mostly through third-party lines, so it captures the volume and little of the price.

(4) Share-price outperformance in the cohort over the last decade came from margin step-changes driven by mix and self-help (Volex 2.8% to 10.2%, discoverIE 3.4% to 13.8%, Diploma to 24.5%) combined with disciplined acquisition at under 9x EBIT; Solid State has the same stated mechanism (6.2% toward 12%) but has not delivered a step-change since FY21 and has used acquisitions sparingly.

(5) The sovereign-platform re-rating (OHB: contractor to platform, multiplicative re-rating on a programme win plus a legitimating event) is the upside archetype; a CAIN follow-on of programme scale plus a Defence Investment Plan naming tactical communications would be the signal cluster. It has not occurred.

(6) The "false friend" pattern (headline growth masking deterioration via working capital and capitalisation) is the sector's recurring underperformance mechanism (TT Electronics' 150% cash conversion on inventory unwinds, XP Power's capitalised development before its collapse); the Risks report finds the profile wins for Solid State too.

Fit: Solid State fits the award-timing and fixed-cost-at-the-turn patterns best, which argues for a volatile, timing-led outperformance if awards land in the next two periods, and fits the compounder pattern least.

C-32

Customer demand: support for the thesis, and fragility

C55

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Supportive and fragile in equal measure. Support: defence and security revenue up 25% to £72.5m; the order book £18m higher underlying with most shipping inside eighteen months; CAIN delivered in Q1 FY27 and NSPA framework inquiries continuing; Power autonomy orders over $20m in four months; medical steady with ISO 13485 expansion and 4,000 FDA-cleared medical PCs delivered; Components up 8.1% with United States design wins and internet-of-things modules. Fragility: the largest customer was 17% of FY26 revenue (£26.7m) against a prior norm of none above 10%, and a single programme was 15% of the year; government approval cadence decides the timing of the largest orders; industrial customers remain cautious and European demand has reset to a lower base; the company still needed about £7m of book-and-ship business in H2 FY26 to meet expectations, so the order book does not fully cover the year. The Consumer should model customer demand as the strongest leg of the case and concentration as its sharpest edge.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and fragility

C50

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Helping in Systems and Power, where the route is direct, design-in led and tied to primes (BAE Systems, Lockheed Martin, Saab, Siemens Healthcare, Philips) and to government end users via the NSPA framework and the Ministry of Defence; there is no intermediary to compress margin. Hindering in Components, where Solsta is itself the intermediary and faces disintermediation by direct supplier sales and self-serve catalogues; its defence is value-added programming, testing and obsolescence management, which keeps it an engineering partner rather than a logistics broker. The fragility is on the supply side of the route: Steatite's tactical radio business depends on exclusive partner status with Persistent Systems, Solsta's on franchise agreements (Digi, Excelitas, DeepX) that can be modified or withdrawn, and the Risks report names unilateral supplier termination as a counter-read. The route to market is therefore an asset for 60% of revenue and a dependency for all of it.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour, and fragility against them

❌ D48

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Peers are recovering simultaneously, so Solid State's growth is not evidence of share gain, and its position against them is strong in the niche and weak on scale. XP Power's H1 2026 gross margin was 45.9% and operating margin 7.9% with book-to-bill 1.67; discoverIE's organic orders rose 22% to 31% with a 13.8% margin; TT Electronics' European defence runs at 15.3% margins and its H1 2026 margin expanded 230 basis points. None of these behaves aggressively toward Solid State; they compete for acquisition targets more than for the same orders. Against Volex, described as a well-run aggressive competitor without proprietary intellectual property, Solid State's battery and systems work overlaps only at the edges. Fragility: Solid State earns 6.2% where the peers earn 8% to 14%, so in any tender where a larger peer chooses to bid, Solid State has less margin to give; and its accreditation moat protects price only on programmes already won, not on new ones where a discoverIE or Diploma subsidiary is also qualified.

C-35

New entrants by type, and the threat of each

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Adjacent entrants (medium threat): discoverIE through Trival (antennas, directly against Q-Par) and 3Gmetalworx (shielding), Diploma through CDM (defence interconnect), Volex in defence harness and battery-adjacent work, Cicor in high-reliability EMS. They bring capital and customer access, but they enter by acquisition, so they mostly buy existing competitors rather than add capacity.

Standard entrants (medium threat in Components, low in Systems): low-cost Asian optoelectronics and sub-assembly makers into medical lasers and consumer lines; generic contract manufacturers are blocked from defence and medical by AS9100 and ISO 13485.

Orthogonal entrants (low near term, rising): software-defined autonomy companies (Anduril, Shield AI) that vertically integrate hardware and could internalise communications, power and computing sub-systems; AI design-automation platforms that reduce the need for application engineering; and defence customers themselves insourcing sub-systems for supply resilience. The last is rated low plausibility in the TT Electronics work because primes tie 95% of engineering to sustaining work, and that reasoning transfers.

Overall: the entry threat is moderate, slow, and concentrated in Components and in the long-dated software-defined shift; it does not change the eighteen-month model.

C-36

Substitutes and alternatives, and the trend

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

A moderate and slowly rising threat. Substitutes named in the evidence: consumer tablets tested against rugged devices (lifecycle costs converge, so the risk is contained); customers buying cells, packs and electronics separately or through contract manufacturers instead of a custom pack; direct purchase from semiconductor makers instead of through a distributor; AI design tools and configurators instead of application-engineering support; open-architecture modular sub-systems that make component substitution easier; and in-house prime capability. Absent: optical substitution (relevant to Volex, not here), and any substitute for qualified secure tactical radio hardware in a NATO programme. The trend is rising at the commodity and software ends and flat in the qualified core; the practical effect over four years is pressure on Components' value-add and on standard battery products, not on the defence programme revenue that drives the margin.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, and fragility

C58

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Helping on direction, hurting on timing, fragile on both. Help: the Strategic Defence Review's priorities, the 2.5% of GDP commitment, NATO budgets, sovereign-supply mandates in the UK and United States, the DSTL award and the Project CAIN initial order, accreditation regimes that keep unqualified rivals out, and a Chair with Ministry of Defence relationships. Hurt: the Defence Investment Plan delay, United States stopgap budgets, export-control tightening with refusal risk, tariffs paid at import and recovered later, the Autumn Budget cost. Fragility: a single political decision (a review pause) has already moved 60% of a year's EPS; the UK government changed leadership in 2026 (Burnham replacing Starmer) with fiscal rules under scrutiny; and United States procurement rules (Executive Order 14415 on critical minerals waivers from January 2027, indentured bills of materials, NIST SP 800-171 scoring) add compliance obligations for a supplier with United States ambitions. Direction is the most reliable positive in the case; timing is the least reliable.

C-38

On whom disruption forces act, how, and how fragile the company's position is left

❌ D44

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Suppliers: AI capacity absorption and memory inflation act on the semiconductor and memory makers, who pass scarcity and price down; Solid State's position is fragile here because it is a price-taker with single-source dependencies and no hedging.

Customers: the autonomy and edge-AI shifts act on defence and industrial customers by changing what they buy (more batteries, more secure communications, more edge computing) and how (sovereign, software-defined, faster cycles); Solid State's position is favourable on product and fragile on cycle length if programme lives shorten.

Competitors: vertical integration by new defence entrants and acquisition by scaled peers act on the competitive set; Solid State's position is defensible on accreditation and weak on scale.

Regulators and political actors: rearmament and sovereign mandates act in Solid State's favour; procurement delay and export control act against it.

Other actors (the sell side, the market): the AI-financing crack and the risk-off regime act on the share price through liquidity and the discount rate; the position is fragile because the stock is small and thinly covered.

Net: disruption leaves the company's product position stronger and its cost, timing and valuation position weaker.

C-39

Suppliers: a support or a source of risk

❌ D35

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A source of risk, and the least-disclosed part of the case. Risks: Persistent Systems is the exclusive source of the MPU5 radios and Wave Relay networks that underpin CAIN, NSPA and the high-margin end of Systems; a change in distribution terms or a direct relationship between Persistent and a NATO ministry would remove the moat; advanced microprocessors and specialised sensors are single-sourced; Taiwan is critical and unalterable; memory makers are prioritising AI customers and raising prices; franchise suppliers can terminate; the company discloses no supplier concentration, no supplier financial-health measures and no defect or delivery metrics. Supports: supplier trust (Saft, Electrochem and Molicel cell partnerships; world-class suppliers relying on Solsta's engineers to create demand), prompt payment preserving allocation priority (trade payables stable at £17.0m), cross-divisional early warning of shortages from the distribution business, scheduled orders, buffer stock and second-source qualification, and the "China plus one" programme. On balance suppliers hold more power over Solid State than its customers do, and the second set's gross-margin line should be built with that in mind.
