Company - Competitive advantage

❌ D457 questions
C-4152C-4232C-4362C-4452C-4542C-4640C-4738

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Solid State holds a narrow, genuine moat of accreditation, design-in and sovereign capacity that protects price and share on programmes it has already won in defence and medical niches, and holds almost no advantage in the 39% of revenue that is distribution. It is small on every scale measure, which costs it purchasing power, overhead absorption and a liquidity premium, but the niches are small enough that accreditation rather than scale decides them. The addressable market is large and growing faster than the company's conversion capacity, customer utility is demonstrably not inferior in Systems and Power, and pricing power is cost recovery rather than price leadership. Most important for the model: the advantage does not compound mechanically; outputs rise when programmes are won and Ashchurch fills, and fall when they are not, so the second set should build the forecasts from programme timing and utilisation rather than from a steady-state growth and margin algorithm.

C-41

Sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats, by type, strength and direction

C52

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Customer-related (medium, stable to widening in defence, narrowing in distribution): design-in irreversibility, where a Steatite computer or Custom Power pack specified into a military platform or medical imaging system cannot be swapped without recertification, giving seven to thirty year revenue lives; over 1,500 active accounts and relationships with BAE Systems, Lockheed Martin, Saab, Siemens Healthcare and Philips; through-life support, training and spiral upgrades under Persistent Systems deployments. The 17% customer and the £23.3m single programme show the relationships are deep but concentrated. In distribution the customer advantage is the engineer-to-engineer sales model, which AI design tools and direct supplier portals erode over time.

Production-related (medium, widening): a sovereign dual UK and United States footprint (Redditch and Crewkerne; Fountain Valley and Indiana), the Ashchurch integrated systems facility with £15m of headroom, automated battery test rooms, cell-sorting and welding upgrades, and ISO 13485 cleanroom capability at Weymouth. This is new capacity that competitors without a sovereign footprint cannot replicate quickly; it is widening in capability and currently under-utilised.

Scale-based (weak, static): none at group level; £154m of revenue confers little purchasing power. The only scale effect is internal, where the distribution business gives the systems businesses early warning of shortages.

Regulatory or political (strong, stable): AS9100, AS9120, SC21, ISO 13485, FDA clearances, Official Secrets Act and facility security accreditations, Secure by Design, ITAR and EAR compliance systems, approved-supplier status with United States primes through Q-Par. These take one and a half to four years to earn and are the reason the qualified niches behave like local oligopolies.

Culture or knowledge (medium, at risk): forty years of an engineering family culture, application knowledge in harsh-environment electronics, the exclusive Persistent Systems partnership and the Saft, Electrochem and Molicel cell relationships. The founder-era chief executive is gone, administrative headcount has surged, and the Persistent relationship is a dependency as much as a moat.

Net: a real but narrow moat built on accreditation and design-in, widening on sovereign capacity and narrowing on the distribution side; it protects price and share within programmes won, not the timing of new programmes.

C-42

Scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what it confers

❌ D32

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Small on every axis. Revenue £154.1m against discoverIE £443m (35% of it), TT Electronics £481m, Volex $1.24bn and Diploma £1.52bn (9%); 476 average employees against Volex's 12,500; thirteen locations; market capitalisation about £98m with £0.3m traded daily; one visible broker. Against customers it is a sub-tier supplier to primes with budgets of billions; against suppliers it is a small account for foundries and memory makers and a valued but replaceable partner for Persistent Systems.

What scale confers here is mostly negative: limited purchasing power, a fixed overhead that is large relative to revenue (adjusted SG&A of £42.0m is 27% of sales), bottom-quartile revenue per site, thin research intensity, and a valuation discount tied to illiquidity. What it does not prevent is niche leadership, because the niches are small enough that accreditation rather than volume decides them. The one scale advantage is agility: a £1.65m DSTL cabinet contract and an $10.8m radio order are material wins for Solid State and would be rounding errors for a prime.

C-43

Realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and the growth it allows

C62

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Large relative to the company, growing at 4% to 6% in the core and faster in defence, and bounded in practice by qualification and sales capacity rather than by market size.

The custom industrial electronics serviceable market is about $30bn growing 5% to 6% (discoverIE's framing); professional and industrial electronics grow 4% to 5% globally; the European high-mix low-volume EMS sub-segment is about $21bn growing 6.8% with outsourcing at 40% against 50% globally; the global EMS market grows 5.6% to 2030; defence electronics within NATO budgets moving toward 2.5% to 3.0% of GDP grows faster, and the UK 20/40/40 doctrine redirects it toward Solid State's products; data-centre power demand grows at 17% a year to 2030. Solid State's share of any of these is well under 1%.

Growth allowed: management's £250m by 2030 implies about 10% compound from FY26, which the market size permits several times over; the Zeus base case of 2.6% revenue growth in FY28 is a statement about conversion capacity, not about the market. The binding constraints are the number of programmes the group can qualify for and deliver at once, the engineering headcount (stable at about 80 technical specialists), the Ashchurch and antenna capacity now being added, and the customer's procurement cadence. The Consumer can model double-digit organic growth in Systems and Power without a share-of-market objection; the objection is operational.

C-44

Surplus customer utility against alternatives: leading, or demonstrably not inferior

C52

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Not inferior is demonstrated; leading is not.

Evidence against inferiority: repeat awards from the Ministry of Defence (NSPA £23.3m, CAIN $10.8m), DSTL choosing Ashchurch for complex cabinets, Custom Power's $7.4m of wins across Tier 1 UK and United States primes in air, maritime and subsea autonomy after it deliberately shed low-value accounts, Siemens Healthcare and Philips design-in relationships, 4,000 FDA-cleared custom medical PCs delivered, tariffs passed through with 10 basis points of margin erosion (customers accepted the cost because the engineered products matter to them), and suppliers such as Digi, Excelitas and DeepX choosing Solsta to create design-in demand. Gross margin rising 200 basis points in a year of memory inflation is consistent with customers paying for engineering content.

Evidence that limits the claim: no disclosed churn, win rate, net promoter or retention figure (contrast TT Electronics' churn falling from 30% to 10% and Windy City Wire's 99% retention); no expert-call coverage exists for the company at all, so the public narrative cannot be triangulated against customers; Components operating margin of 4% says the distribution customer does not pay much for the service; Custom Power's goodwill impairment followed the loss of churned industrial customers; and the Wistron-type evidence that exists for Volex (secondary supplier, chosen for 5% to 8% lower pricing) has no equivalent here either way.

Judgement: the Sophos and Bytes test (disprove inferiority) is passed for Systems and Power on the pattern of repeat sovereign awards; it is not passed for Components, where the economics say the utility is modest and substitutable.

C-45

Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of surplus utility, shown by application

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Limited. The applied evidence is cost recovery, not price leadership: full pass-through of semiconductor and material inflation in the last cycle; tariff pass-through with administration charges; back-to-back pricing, non-cancellable orders and deposits that protect margin on long-lead programmes; and the Rothschild & Co Redburn observation that specialist distributors keep pricing power during supply disruption because customers prioritise availability. The group gross margin held in a 31% to 33.5% band through a 23% revenue fall and a 23% rise, which shows pricing is stable rather than strong.

What is absent: any disclosed price increase above cost; any statement that customers accept annual increases (discoverIE's 3% to 5%); any gross-margin expansion attributed to price rather than mix; and in Components a margin so thin that pricing power is evidently not being exercised. The sector's current memory-inflation window gives a test: if FY27 gross margin holds at or above 33% while memory costs rise and lead times extend, the company is recovering cost with a short lag; if it rises above 34%, it is keeping some of the price. Zeus holds 33.2%, which is the cost-recovery case.

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers, stated or de facto

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Not a feature of the model. Solid State is a specialist whose proposition is availability, engineering support, accreditation and sovereign supply, for which customers pay a premium over commodity sources; there is no stated or de facto policy of passing scale gains to customers, no scale to pass, and no evidence of a Costco-type pricing culture. The closest analogue is the distribution business supplying obsolescence management and small-batch sourcing that saves customers' engineering time, which is value sharing rather than scale sharing. For an investment case this is neutral: the absence of scale economies shared is consistent with a niche premium model, and it means growth does not come from a price-led volume flywheel.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical forces improving the advantage and the financial outputs each period, rather than heroic dynamism

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Weak. The Business Foundations report's own verdict is that "improvement is driven by heroic contract wins and execution rather than mechanical forces", and the record supports it: three divisions, each needing its own programme wins, a group margin that swings 10.4% to 4.8% to 6.2% with the timing of one customer's orders, and no disclosed design-win bank, recurring-revenue share or installed base from which growth mechanically compounds.

The mechanical elements that do exist are three. First, Ashchurch absorption: £15m of capacity headroom against £2m to £3m of unabsorbed overhead means each £10m of additional Systems revenue lifts group operating margin by roughly 150 to 200 basis points without further investment; this is gravitational in the sense that it needs orders, not new capability. Second, the through-life support tail on deployed tactical radio networks (training, support, spiral upgrades, replacement) builds a recurring layer on top of each hardware programme, and management is explicitly steering toward "programme partnerships" to deepen it; it is nascent and unquantified. Third, the maturation of acquired assets and own-brand lines (Gateway at 40% to 43% gross margin, Durakool, Pacer) lifts mix without heroics.

Against these, the balancing forces are equally mechanical: fixed overhead that grows with ambition, memory inflation that needs active repricing, and a customer cadence set by ministries. On balance the company's outputs improve when management wins and delivers, not because the model compounds on its own; this is the single most important difference between Solid State and discoverIE or Diploma for the four-year model.

Back to contents

Company - Economic strength

C5011 questions
C-4942C-5055C-5145C-5248C-5345C-5448C-7545C-7670C-7750C-7862C-7958

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Solid State's economics are those of a small, asset-light, accredited integrator with a fixed overhead sized for a larger business: gross margin is reliable and improving (33.5%), operating margin is cyclically depressed (6.2%) and mechanically recoverable through utilisation, returns on capital are middling (14% on capital employed) and would be high on tangible capital, cash conversion is strong on average and erratic by period because programme billing and inventory move in lumps, the balance sheet is a buffer with unused capacity, and the earnings base is moderately clean with working capital rather than adjustments as the quality signal. The defining property is operating leverage: a 10% revenue surprise either way moves EPS by roughly a third, so the Consumer's model should be built on a volume and utilisation bridge with a wide cone rather than on margin assumptions, and the EPS growth that consensus shows (5%, 13%, 22%) is a margin-recovery forecast on near-flat revenue that is as sensitive to programme timing as the FY25 collapse was. The scope to improve outputs is large and congruent with the strategy; the durability of any given year's output is low.

C-49

EPS growth, revenue growth, and durability

❌ D42

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

The record is a saw-tooth and the forecast is a gentle ramp with a one-broker hockey stick at the end.

History, adjusted diluted EPS (company and brokers' basis): 14.1p FY22, about 13p FY23, 20.0p FY24, 6.2p FY25, 11.0p FY26; the four-year compound rate from FY22 to FY26 is negative (about -6%) despite revenue rising from £85.0m to £154.1m (16% compound, of which a large part was acquired: Custom Power, Active Silicon, Willow, Gateway, Q-Par). Revenue growth by year: +28% FY22, +49% FY23, +29% FY24, -23% FY25, +23% FY26; organic constant-currency growth in FY26 was 25.3% but only about 4% to 7% excluding the deferred £23.3m programme.

Forecast, brokers' basis: FY27 11.6p (+5%), FY28 13.1p (+13%; Zeus 12.7p, Cavendish 12.7p), FY29 16.0p (+22%, Cavendish alone); revenue +1.5%, +3.2%, +6.9%. The EPS growth comes from margin (EBIT margin 6.3% to 6.6% to 7.5%) on near-flat revenue, which is the operating-leverage mechanism of C-75 applied to a recurring base growing mid-single digits while a £23.3m one-off washes out.

Durability: low at the annual level because a single programme can move EPS by half; moderate at the three-year level because defence budgets are rising, Ashchurch is built and the recurring base is growing; management's own long-term incentive range for FY28 (11.5p to 16.0p) spans the whole consensus cone and its 2030 ambition of 20p requires about 16% compound from FY26. The peer table shows the contrast: Volex 22%, 11%, 11%; discoverIE 11%, 7%, 6%; Diploma 45%, 4%, 4%; TT Electronics 75%, 34%, 12% from a trough. Solid State's forecast growth is the lowest in the cohort in the first year and the most back-end loaded.

C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return for three to five years, and the implication for the fair multiple

C55

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

High scope and low capital need, which is the attractive feature of the economics if orders arrive.

The highest-return deployment needs almost no capital: filling the Ashchurch facility's £15m of revenue headroom at Systems gross margins (the sub-assembly and integration stage earns 35% to 39% gross margins in the value-chain map) would add roughly £5m of gross profit against perhaps £1m of incremental variable overhead, so about £4m of operating profit on an asset already paid for, or a 40% to 50% return on the £7.5m of planned capital expenditure even if all of it were attributed to the ramp. Antenna manufacturing and test capability (United States onshore production), Power's automated test rooms and the India transfer of own-brand lines are the next uses, each measured in low single-digit millions. Bolt-on acquisitions at the Gateway type of multiple (£1.4m for a business with 40% to 43% gross margins) are available but small. Working capital is the one large use: strategic inventory for memory and programmes absorbs cash in FY27 at a return that is defensive rather than high.

Mid- and long-term implication: return on capital employed has already moved from 8.7% to 14.0% on utilisation alone; the 2030 plan implies 20% or more if margins reach 10% to 12% on the existing capital base. A business that can earn mid-teens to 20% returns on capital with 1% to 2% capital intensity deserves a mid-teens earnings multiple in its sector (the five-year median for this stock is 15.7x; the cohort's quality names trade at 17x to 29x), against 11x today. The fair multiple therefore depends almost entirely on whether the deployment is demand-constrained, which it is, rather than capital-constrained, which it is not.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital

❌ D45

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Middle to low of the cohort and recovering. Company-defined return on capital employed (adjusted operating profit over average capital employed): 15.5% FY21, 8.7% FY25, 14.0% FY26. Post-tax return on invested capital on a FactSet basis is roughly 9% to 11% (adjusted EBIT £7.5m to £9.6m depending on basis, taxed at 24%, on shareholders' equity of about £63m plus net debt of £10m including leases); on tangible capital, after removing goodwill and acquired intangibles that account for a large share of the £63m of net assets, the return is materially higher, probably in the high teens to twenties, which reflects an asset-light model whose invested capital is mostly acquisition goodwill and working capital. Return on new invested capital in the last three years has been poor on Custom Power (impaired) and good on Gateway and on organic capacity. Peers: Volex 21%, Diploma 22.7%, discoverIE 15.2% (45% on tangible capital), TT Electronics 13.3%, Gooch & Housego 9.6%. The lever is the same as everywhere in this case: utilisation; each point of operating margin is worth roughly two points of return on capital employed.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow, and the behaviour of working capital

❌ D48

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Conversion is strong on average and erratic by year, because working capital is where the programme lumpiness shows up.

Company basis: adjusted operating cash conversion 84% FY24, 173% FY25, 141% FY26, 102% to 103% in H1 FY26; cash from operations £13.5m, free cash flow £11.2m in FY26. FactSet basis: free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure) £5.8m FY24, £2.5m FY25, £6.4m FY26, or 66%, 494% and 156% of reported net income; adding intangible capital expenditure (£1.3m) and lease payments reduces FY26 free cash flow to about £4m to £5m. Consensus has free cash flow of £5.7m FY27, £6.5m FY28 and £9.7m FY29 on net operating cash flow of £11.8m, £12.5m and £14.3m with capital expenditure of £2.8m, £2.8m and £1.7m, which is lighter than the company's "over £7.5m across two years" and should be corrected upward by the Consumer.

Working capital: net working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables) has run between 19% and 24% of trailing revenue since FY23 (£30.6m at March 2026, 19.8%), with swings of £4m to £7m between half-years driven by milestone billing (receivables £27.9m at the H1 FY26 peak, £17.1m to £20.2m at year-end) and programme inventory (£30.5m, 76 days, including a 147% rise in work in progress to £10.96m ahead of CAIN). Contract liabilities of £6.3m show customers pre-fund long-lead programmes, which is the mitigating mechanism. FY27 will add strategic memory inventory, so the Consumer should model working capital absorbing £2m to £4m in FY27 and releasing in FY28 as CAIN and the memory buffer unwind, giving free-cash conversion of about 80% to 90% of adjusted net income in FY27 and above 100% in FY28, consistent with the peer table's 0.9x, 0.9x and 1.1x.

C-53

Gross and operating margins, and their trend

❌ D45

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Gross margin, company basis: 31.7% FY24, 31.4% to 31.5% FY25, 33.5% FY26 (above a 31% floor since FY22); FactSet's standardised line is lower (28.8%, 27.3%, 29.8%) because it classifies some costs differently, and the Consumer must not mix the two. Divisional: Systems about 35% to 39% implied, Power 35.8% (from 31.7%), Components 26.8% (from 25.0%). Trend: up, on mix (own brand, design-in content, exit of low-value assembly), with memory inflation the near-term threat and Zeus holding 33.2% for FY27.

Operating margin, adjusted, company basis: 10.4% to 10.5% FY24, 4.8% FY25, 6.2% FY26 (7.0% in H1 FY26 before second-half overhead), against a 9.2% average for FY21 to FY24; FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin is 8.8%, 3.4% and 4.8% on a stricter definition. Divisional FY26: Systems 14.3% (lifted by the communications programme), Power 5.3%, Components 4.0%; targets 12.5% to 15.0%, 10.0% to 14.0% and 7.0% to 9.0%. Consensus: 6.3% FY27, 6.6% FY28, 7.5% FY29 (one broker), against peers at 8% to 10% (TT Electronics), 11% (Volex), 14% to 15% (discoverIE) and 24% to 26% (Diploma).

Trend: gross margin structurally improving by one to two points per cycle on mix; operating margin cyclically depressed by under-absorption and growth overhead, with a three-point recovery available from utilisation alone and a further two to three points from the divisional targets. The Consumer should model gross margin as the reliable line and operating margin as the output of volume over a largely fixed £42m overhead.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base

❌ D48

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Moderately clean, with three items that need watching and one structural leakage.

The adjusted-to-reported gap: adjusted pre-tax profit £8.6m against reported £5.8m in FY26, a gap of £2.8m (33%), made up of £1.76m amortisation of acquired intangibles, about £0.68m of reorganisation costs, share-option charges and £0.09m of imputed interest on deferred consideration; FY25 carried a £2.7m Custom Power impairment and £2.73m of adjustments. Amortisation of acquired intangibles is the usual and acceptable adjustment; reorganisation costs have recurred for three years (£0.5m FY24, £0.91m H1 FY25, £0.68m FY26) and the Consumer should treat about £0.5m a year as a real cost.

Capitalised costs: development and software additions of £1.11m in FY26 (£0.85m FY25), £1.25m including purchased intangibles, net book value £1.66m; small against £154m of revenue but rising, and the Risks report ranks it the top monitoring item because XP Power's collapse followed a similar pattern. Against discoverIE, which expenses all research, this is a modest flattering of operating profit of under 1% of revenue.

Revenue recognition: £153.6m recognised at a point in time and £0.5m over time, with bill-and-hold arrangements described as judgemental and work in progress up 147%; the Earnings Review carries this as a false-friend candidate, and the memo asked for the annual report note to be read. Nothing in the audited accounts has been qualified.

Tax and other leakage: the adjusted effective tax rate is 24.1% (flat), the reported rate 30.1%; the UK rate is 25% and United States goodwill deductions help cash tax, so there is no structural tax drag. Net interest of £1.2m on about £10m of average debt including leases is the only below-the-line item of note. Share count is stable at 57.1m with a 10% dilution cap and £0.2m of treasury purchases, so EPS is not diluted.

Judgement: the earnings base is cleaner than TT Electronics' (adjustments 138% of statutory losses) and less clean than discoverIE's; the Consumer should model adjusted EPS with a recurring £0.5m reorganisation drag and should treat work in progress, not the adjustments, as the quality signal.

C-75

Materiality of fixed-cost operating leverage, and the range of outcomes it creates

❌ D45

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Very material, and the single largest source of forecast variance.

Measured from the record: FY25 revenue fell 23% and adjusted operating profit fell about 65% (£17.6m to £6.0m); H1 FY25 revenue fell 30% and FactSet EBIT fell about 74%; FY26 revenue rose 23% and adjusted operating profit rose 60%. The implied incremental drop-through is 25% to 30% of revenue change at the group level, and higher in Systems where the communications programme carried most of the FY26 margin recovery. Company disclosure: its own severe downside scenario (revenue 6% below base over eighteen months) cuts EBITDA by 22%, a 3.7x elasticity. Zeus's sensitivities: a 50 basis point margin change moves profit by £0.77m and EPS by 0.9p (8.2%); a 2% volume shift moves revenue by £3m and EPS by 0.24p (2.2%); a 1% revenue change moves EPS by 1.1%, which is Zeus's volume-only elasticity and understates the under-absorption effect the FY25 record shows.

Cost structure: cost of sales £102.5m (about two-thirds of revenue, mostly bought-in materials and therefore variable), adjusted SG&A £42.0m (27% of revenue, mostly fixed within a year; staff costs £33.1m across 476 people, facility costs for thirteen sites including Ashchurch's £2m to £3m of unabsorbed overhead). On these numbers, incremental revenue at a 33% gross margin with 5% to 10% variable overhead drops through at 25% to 30%, and a shortfall is lost at the same rate.

Range of outcomes: on the FY27 consensus base (revenue £157.5m, operating profit £9.9m), revenue 10% above the base lifts operating profit to about £14m and EPS to 15p to 16p; revenue 10% below cuts it to about £5.5m and EPS to 7p; revenue 20% below (an FY25-type deferral) takes operating profit toward £2m. The cone for FY27 EPS is therefore roughly 6p to 16p around an 11.6p centre, with the skew set by programme timing rather than by demand level.

C-76

Materiality of financial leverage, its amplification of EPS and risk, and its comfort

C70

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Immaterial and comfortable. Net debt excluding leases was £4.2m at March 2026 (£7.4m FY25, £3.1m FY24), £10.1m including £5.9m of lease liabilities; FactSet's net debt to EBITDA is 0.7x on the lease-inclusive definition and the company's covenant basis (pre-IFRS 16) is under 0.5x. Facilities: a committed revolving facility of £15.0m with a £10.0m accordion on the Business Foundations account, or £30m multi-currency with Lloyds and Comerica on the Earnings Review account, floating at SONIA, EURIBOR or SOFR plus 2.3% to 2.6% depending on leverage; cash of £6.6m. Net interest expense was £1.2m in FY26 (£1.1m FY25, £1.5m FY24), about 14% of adjusted pre-tax profit, falling as debt falls; consensus implies about £1.0m in FY27 and £0.9m to £1.0m in FY28, and Zeus has net cash by March 2028. A 100 basis point rate move is worth about £0.1m of pre-tax profit, or 0.1p of EPS.

Amplification: at these levels gearing adds almost nothing to EPS growth or to risk; the risk that financial structure poses is liquidity in a working-capital spike (FY25 net debt reached £13.1m at the half-year, 2.0x trailing EBITDA on the FactSet calculation, as a large receivable sat unpaid), which the facility headroom covers. Comfort: high; the balance sheet is a buffer, not a lever, and the unused capacity is an option for acquisitions or buybacks.

C-77

How much of each major cost part is cuttable or reducible

C50

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Cost of sales (£102.5m, 67% of revenue): largely variable bought-in components, cells, modules and radios; reducible only through mix (own brand at 40% plus gross margins), sourcing (India transfer, second sourcing) and pricing; not cuttable as such.

Staff costs (£33.1m, 476 people): the discretionary part is the administrative layer that grew from 91 to 156 heads in FY26 and the £2.1m of performance-related and variable costs in H1 FY26; a downturn response of the FY25 type (£1.0m of annualised savings executed within months, over £3.0m of overhead savings during FY25, termination costs £0.43m) shows £2m to £3m is reachable quickly, with the 80 technical engineers and the manufacturing workforce protected.

Facility and overhead (within the £42.0m SG&A): thirteen locations with the Ashchurch and antenna sites recently added; Active Silicon's consolidation shows site rationalisation is possible, but the new capacity is strategic and would be kept under-utilised rather than closed, so the £2m to £3m of unabsorbed overhead is not cuttable without abandoning the thesis.

Growth investment (£2.5m in H1 FY26 alone): deferrable, as it was in FY25.

Capital expenditure (£3.9m FY26, over £7.5m planned across FY27 and FY28): deferrable at the cost of the United States antenna plan.

Judgement: roughly £4m to £6m of annual cost is reducible within six to twelve months in a downturn (about 3% to 4% of revenue), enough to hold operating margin above 4% in an FY25-type year but not enough to protect it; the business is built to grow into its cost base, not to flex it.

C-78

Scope to improve the financial outputs beyond cost cutting, precedents, concentration of sources, and congruence with strategy

C62

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Large scope, concentrated in a few areas, supported by peer precedents of a different kind, and fully congruent with the stated strategy.

The sources: (1) Utilisation of Ashchurch and the antenna and Power capacity, worth 150 to 250 basis points of group margin on £10m to £15m of incremental Systems revenue, the largest and most mechanical lever. (2) Power division from 5.3% toward its 10% to 14% target as low-value work is gone and autonomy programmes scale, worth about 100 to 150 basis points of group margin on a £32m base. (3) Components from 4.0% toward 7% to 9% through own-brand mix, Solsta Embedded and restock volume, worth about 100 to 150 basis points on a £60m base. (4) Through-life support and programme partnerships raising the recurring share and smoothing utilisation. (5) Mix toward design-in content lifting gross margin by a further 100 basis points. Together these bridge 6.2% to roughly 10% to 12%, which is the 2030 ambition, and it comes from three or four big areas rather than many small ones.

Precedents: Volex (2.8% to 10.2% by exiting 400 negative-margin part numbers, vertical integration and site consolidation), discoverIE (3.4% to 13.8% by selling distribution and buying design-in businesses), Diploma (high-teens to 24.5% by mix and acquisition), TT Electronics' European defence at 15.3%. Each shows the destination is reachable for an accredited niche electronics business; none shows it being reached primarily by filling a new facility with government programme volume, which is Solid State's route, so the precedents support the level and not the path.

Congruence: the strategy (programmes at scale, utilisation before acquisition, own brand, three accountable divisions with margin targets) is the improvement plan; nothing in the plan requires a change of direction. The weakness is the missing numeric bridge, which the Capital Markets Day should supply.

C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, its change, and what it means for predictability

C58

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Narrow and slightly widening. Three divisions (Systems about 40% of revenue, Components 39%, Power 21%), two operating countries (the United Kingdom with eleven sites and the United States with two), thirteen locations, 476 people, over 2,000 customers in 60 countries but 45% of revenue in the United Kingdom, 28% in Europe, 23% in North America. Peers run 23 to 41 sites across 18 to 25 countries. The perimeter is widening through United States production (antennas), India sourcing and new product lines (edge AI, data-centre power), each adjacent and small.

For predictability the narrowness cuts both ways. Few divisions and two countries make the model simple to build and the drivers few, which is good. But the narrowness concentrates the outcome on a handful of programmes, one ministry and one customer, so the few drivers are each large and binary, which is bad. The unpredictability in this case comes from lumpiness inside a narrow perimeter, not from breadth; a wider perimeter (more programmes, more countries) would in fact make earnings smoother. The second set should treat the perimeter as fixed for eighteen months and as modestly wider (United States, programmes) by year four.

Company - Management

C5011 questions
C-5558C-5650C-5748C-57b52C-5860C-5950C-6050C-6150C-6238C-6358C-6442

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Management is a competent, honest, low-paid engineering team that executes well within its niches, communicates the mechanism clearly, sets conservative expectations and beats them, and has just been reinforced by a chair with the defence-procurement access the case needs. It is not an outlier: margins and returns sit at the bottom of the cohort, the standards of disclosure (utilisation, programme share, churn, suppliers) are below what the thesis requires, capital allocation is passive, and the new chief executive is an insider seven months into the role with a United States expansion to run from Worcestershire. The ambition is large and the strategy is sensible and well resourced, which means the next two years test execution rather than intent. For the model: trust the gross margin, the cash discipline and the conservative expectation-setting; do not assume management can influence the timing that decides operating margin; and watch the Capital Markets Day for the first numeric bridge and for any reframing of the 2030 ambition.

C-55

Size of the company's ambition or vision, particularly against peers

C58

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Large relative to the company's size, ordinary relative to the cohort's leaders. The stated ambition is at least 20p of sustainable adjusted EPS by 2030 (from 11.0p), £250m of revenue (from £154.1m, about 10% compound), a 20% compound total shareholder return, divisional margin targets of 12.5% to 15%, 10% to 14% and 7% to 9%, and a chief executive who says the group should "not turn away challenging prospects that it can win and execute by combining its capabilities" and wants "accelerated but sustainable growth". The deliberate build of capacity ahead of demand (Ashchurch, antennas, Power test rooms, a 71% rise in administrative headcount) is ambition expressed in cost. Against peers: Volex targets $2bn and 12% margins having beaten its last plan a year early; discoverIE targets 17% margins after ten consecutive upgrades; Diploma compounds EPS at 18% to 26%; TT Electronics targets 12% after missing 10% for years. Solid State's ambition is of the Volex and discoverIE kind in shape and smaller in evidence: the last step-change in its margins was FY21 to FY24 and it was reversed. The "focus and pace" framing and the explicit willingness to take harder programmes signal a push, not a steady state, which for a 6% margin business is the right force provided execution keeps up.

C-56

Quality and boldness of the strategy as an enabler

C50

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Smart within its means, not bold, and dynamic across a plausible range of environments.

Widening the moat over the mid term: sovereign dual-footprint manufacturing, Tier 2 sub-system capability at Ashchurch, programme partnerships with through-life support, own-brand products and the Volklec sovereign battery alliance all deepen the accreditation and design-in moat where it exists; nothing in the strategy addresses the structural weakness of the distribution leg beyond moving it toward consultative embedded solutions (Solsta Embedded), which is sensible but slow.

Growth as a priority: yes, with capacity built first and acquisitions subordinated to organic utilisation; the £250m milestone is explicit.

Dynamism across environments: the three-division mix (defence and security 47%, medical and industrial, AI data-centre and edge) and the United Kingdom, European and United States spread give the strategy more than one environment in which to work; defence budgets are counter-cyclical to industrial capital expenditure, and the restock and AI legs do not depend on the Ministry of Defence. The weakness is that the environment that matters most, sovereign procurement cadence, is the one no strategy can hedge, and the group's response (build capacity and wait) is the correct long-run posture and the costly short-run one.

Boldness: the 2030 numbers are bold for the base; the actions are incremental (no transformational acquisition, no exit of distribution, no capital return). The strategy is a good enabler of the operating-leverage case; it is not a strategy that creates a new kind of business.

C-57

Quality of the chief executive, judged by actions

❌ D48

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

An experienced internal operator, seven months into the permanent role, with a record of competent divisional management and no evidence yet of transformational capability.

Actions on the record: twenty years running the Components division, which he unified under the Solsta brand in 2023 and where he executed the Gateway acquisition (integrated within a year, 40% to 43% gross margins added) and the exit of low-margin United States lines (gross margin 25.0% to 26.8%); in his first months as chief executive he separated Power from Systems, created the Commercial Project Office, Chief Technology Officer and Chief Relationship Officer roles, introduced the 3Cs and 3Ps frameworks, a profit-share scheme and structured performance reviews, and delivered the FY26 outturn ahead of expectations with Project CAIN shipped on time against memory constraints. He screened 47 North American businesses before the Custom Power acquisition under the previous chief executive.

Limits: the Components division he ran grew 8% in FY26 and earns a 4.0% operating margin, which is the weakest in the group; his career is UK-centred while the growth plan is American; he has no external public-company executive experience; management deflected on Custom Power's impairment and on funding of UK defence commitments; the explicit 12% margin target disappeared from the annual report on his watch. Pay is bottom-decile (£261,000 base), which keeps fixed cost down and limits the pool from which a successor could be hired.

Judgement: an enabler rather than an ossifier, with clear communication and operational grip, whose test is the next two reporting periods and the Capital Markets Day. Not yet an Animal CEO by the Slootman standard; the Leadership report's "partial match" is fair.

C-57b

Priority on hiring well, and the quality of the leadership team built

C52

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Hiring is treated as a priority and done largely from within, which is both the strength and the limit.

Evidence: 78% of FY26 hires sourced internally without agency fees; nearly half of vacancies filled by promotion; a Group Executive Board formed in 2024 that enabled a seamless succession when the chief executive died; new General Manager roles in Systems; appointments of a Group Chief Technology Officer, a United States Chief Sales Officer (Stephen Brown), a Head of Operations for Custom Power USA (Steve Soto), a Business Development Director for Integrated Computing Systems (Peter Molyneux); Jon Baxter (30 years in distribution, ex-Arrow) promoted to run Solsta; Dave Crossman promoted to run Power after restoring United States order intake; a long-serving finance director (since 2017, ex-PwC and IQE) and a KPMG-trained financial controller; an HR director with twenty years in engineering; the chair recruited externally from Thales UK.

Limits: bottom-decile executive pay (chief executive £261,000, finance director £227,000 against £480,000 to £564,000 at peers) restricts external recruitment of senior talent; the team is long-tenured and insider-heavy; management and administrative headcount rose 71% while technical engineers stayed at 80, which is where the growth plan needs people; employee turnover rose modestly in FY26 and no turnover, engagement or net-promoter figure is disclosed, so bench quality cannot be independently verified; the Leadership report rates key-person dependency on the chief executive and finance director as high.

Judgement: a team deep enough for a £150m business and probably for £200m; whether it is deep enough for £250m with a United States manufacturing leg is the open question, and the evidence that would answer it (retention, external hires at senior level in the United States) is thin.

C-58

How well resourced and invested the whole business is

C60

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Well invested, to the point of being over-invested relative to current revenue, which is the correct posture for an ant colony and the cause of the present margin.

Evidence: the Ashchurch integrated systems facility built and operational with £15m of revenue headroom and a flagship DSTL contract; Active Silicon consolidated into an expanded Langley site; Custom Power's facilities upgraded with automated test rooms, ESD flooring, welding and cell-sorting; ISO 13485 added at Weymouth; a group CRM, AI tools, ERP standardisation and a Commercial Project Office; capital expenditure of £3.9m in FY26 and over £7.5m planned across FY27 and FY28 for antennas and United States production; strategic inventory of £30.5m with more promised to protect delivery; a £15m to £30m facility with headroom; five engineering heads added, apprenticeships and development programmes; second sourcing, "China plus one" and a Supply Chain Council.

Costs of the posture: £2m to £3m of unabsorbed overhead, £8.7m of added SG&A, cash tied up in stock, a flat FY27 margin guide. Peers that are asset-light (discoverIE at 1.5% capital intensity and 72% local-for-local production) hold margins through downturns; Solid State's maker-side investment makes it more resilient operationally and less resilient financially.

Judgement: the buffer exists and is paid for; the model should treat the FY27 cost base as largely in place and the FY27 to FY29 capital expenditure as above consensus.

C-59

Holistic and congruent culture, with alignment across the total system

C50

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Partly. The pieces that are congruent: a forty-year engineering culture ("engineers that talk to engineers") that matches a design-in proposition; a three-division structure with named managing directors and divisional margin targets that matches the programme-led strategy; incentives that pay the annual bonus only when the group beats the market expectations in place when targets were set and a long-term plan that vests on real EPS growth with options lapsing when missed; low executive pay and no insider selling that match a prudent, shareholder-first posture; the 3Cs framework that asks divisions to bid together, which is the behavioural change the strategy needs; a group-wide profit share.

The pieces that are not: the 71% rise in administrative headcount sits oddly with a lean engineering culture; the Risks report records management using digital platforms that allow filtering of difficult live questions and glossing over inventory write-offs, which is not the Avanza or Ryanair standard of transparency; the Persistent Systems dependency and the memory-inflation exposure are managed by stock rather than by contract, which is a culture of buffering rather than of structural fix; the culture's keeper for four decades has died and the successor's "focus and pace" is a statement of intent rather than a demonstrated system. There is no Glassdoor, engagement or turnover disclosure to test any of it.

Judgement: congruent in design, unproven in operation under the new leadership, and well short of the Lifco standard of a decentralised system that runs itself.

C-60

Clarity and helpfulness of communication, including on strategy and the driver transmission mechanism

C50

Question asked How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Clear on the mechanism, thin on the numbers. Management explains the transmission (programme wins fill Ashchurch, utilisation lifts Systems margin, own brand and Power mix lift gross margin, three divisions report their own margins against targets) plainly and consistently, defines market expectations transparently as the average of three named brokers, publishes order-book figures at each half with the one-off programme separated out, and gave an underlying growth figure (about 4%) that was less flattering than the headline. The pre-close trading update in April is a reliable annual anchor.

What is missing or unhelpful: no formal numeric guidance for revenue, margin or profit; the group 12% margin target quietly dropped from the annual report; no Ashchurch utilisation figure, no programme-revenue share, no design-win bank, no customer churn, no supplier concentration, no segment forecasts in FactSet; deflection on Custom Power's impairment and on how UK defence commitments would be funded; a share-ownership disclosure for the chief executive that appears as both 546,207 and 150,053 shares; and a sell-side base so thin that nobody independent restates the story. The Capital Markets Day is the chance to fix most of this, and the Consumer should read it as the first proper disclosure of the driver bridge.

C-61

Record of acting, not just talking: recent delivery and mid-term track record

C50

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Recent (last twelve months): strong. The deferred NATO programme was delivered in full in H1 FY26; Project CAIN was won (October 2025) and the initial $10.8m shipped in Q1 FY27 against memory constraints; Power was separated, restructured in the United States and returned to 5.3% margins with over $20m of new orders; Ashchurch opened and won DSTL; Active Silicon was consolidated; net debt fell £3.2m; the FY26 outturn beat the April upgrade; the succession worked; a Capital Markets Day and a joint broker were arranged.

Mid-term (three to five years): mixed. Revenue grew from £85.0m in FY22 to £163.3m in FY24 and EPS from 14.1p to 20.0p, a 30.3% five-year total shareholder return compound to March 2024; then FY25 missed by 60% at the EPS line for an external reason (the review pause), with management responding with £1m of savings within weeks and guiding honestly through it; the FY26 recovery restored revenue but not margin (6.2% against 10.4%). Custom Power's integration took three years and an impairment. Across the period the company met or beat the expectations it set in every year except FY25, and in FY25 it beat its rebased number. The pattern is delivery against conservative expectations, not outperformance in a tough environment; when the environment turned, the numbers broke.

Judgement: a record of action that is better than the record of outcomes, because the outcomes have been set by a customer's calendar; the Consumer should give management credit for execution and none for timing.

C-62

Outlier standards: higher, faster, better, with a record of out-executing competitors

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

No. The evidence of standards is sound rather than exceptional: accreditations across sites, Secure by Design, a clean audit record over ten years, 141% cash conversion, prompt supplier payment, a quick cost response in 2024. The evidence of out-execution is absent: operating margin of 6.2% against 8% to 15% at every listed peer; Components at 4% against discoverIE's 13.8% in a comparable design-in model; return on capital employed of 14% against 21% to 23% at Volex and Diploma; no disclosed productivity, scrap, on-time delivery or utilisation metric; administrative headcount growing faster than revenue. The group executes competently within its niches and has not shown that it does anything better or faster than the cohort's leaders.

C-63

Shareholders first, and prudence of governance: what the evidence shows, and why

C58

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

The evidence points to a management that puts shareholders ahead of itself in pay and incentives, and to governance that is adequate rather than scaffolded beyond need.

Shareholders first: executive pay in the bottom decile of the sector; the annual bonus pays only when the group beats the market expectations that stood when targets were set, and is deferred for post-year-end validation; the long-term plan vests on real EPS growth (30% plus inflation over three years for the FY24 grant; 11.5p to 16.0p of FY28 EPS for the May 2026 grant) with 1,027,500 and 600,375 options lapsed when targets were missed rather than reset; no director has sold a share on the open market in the period while peers' executives have (TT Electronics' former chair, Spectris' chief executive); the chair bought shares on joining; related-party transactions are £0.05m and at arm's length; a single share class with one vote each, full takeover code, no defences; the FY25 dividend was cut to preserve growth capital and then restored with 3.5x to 4.0x cover rather than paid out of a weak year.

Governance prudence: a Group Executive Board built before succession was needed and used when it was; Audit, Remuneration and Nomination committees staffed only by independent directors; the audit chair moved to an independent director after ISS concerns; an ESG committee standardising subsidiary governance; a Minimum Controls Framework style of oversight through the board's reserved matters; whistleblowing channels. Against that: only two to four independent directors (43% of seats excluding the chair against 67% at discoverIE); no independent internal audit function, with peer reviews instead, during a United States expansion; the auditor has served over ten years with a lead partner change in 2024; executive shareholding guidelines of 150% of salary against 250% at discoverIE; inconsistent disclosure of the chief executive's shareholding; the ability to filter live questions at results.

Why: a founder-era, engineering-led, low-pay culture that does not extract value from shareholders and has not yet built the institutional scaffolding that a £250m, two-country group will need. Prudent in alignment, light in structure.

C-64

Enlightened capital allocation and dynamic share repurchase, with a record of high returns on new capital

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

An operator and generator rather than an allocator. The positive evidence: a strict acquisition screen (strategic and cultural fit, earnings-enhancing in year one, 47 targets reviewed for one deal), small bolt-ons bought cheaply (Gateway £1.4m for 40% to 43% gross margins), capital directed at organic capacity where the return is highest (Ashchurch), debt reduced rather than distributed when the balance sheet needed it, the dividend cut when earnings fell and restored when they recovered. The negative evidence: Custom Power at up to $45m was the one large allocation of the last five years and was impaired; the dominant cash use in FY27 is inventory; no buyback has been considered at a 24-month-forward multiple at the first percentile of its five-year range and with net cash in sight, even though the long-term plan is EPS-based and a buyback would serve it; £0.2m of treasury purchases for employee schemes is the whole repurchase record; dividend growth is modest and no capital-allocation framework (hurdle rates, leverage range, return priorities) is disclosed. Returns on new invested capital have been high on organic capacity and own-brand bolt-ons and poor on the one large acquisition. The Consumer should not model repurchases and should treat a buyback announcement as a positive surprise.

Back to contents

Risks

❌ D384 questions
C-8040C-8135C-8238C-8340

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The risk profile is concentrated, correlated and timing-driven rather than diversified and structural. The core engine has slowed in the reported and the underlying numbers because a one-off is washing out and defence awards are late, while the input force itself has strengthened. The fundamental risks cluster around one mechanism (a deferral) that cascades into under-absorption, inventory and accounting concerns, with medium plausibility and a 30% to 60% hit to EPS; the sentiment risks are those of an illiquid AIM share in a risk-off regime with a down-year-on-year interim print due in December. The multiple is already at a trough, so the downside from here is roughly 20% on a bad half and 40% to 50% on a repeat of November 2024. For the second set, the model's downside case should be the FY25 template (revenue down 15% to 20%, operating margin 4% to 5%, EPS 6p to 8p) with a probability well above the base rate for an ordinary industrial, and the base case should carry the H1 FY27 optical decline explicitly so that the Consumer is not surprised by it.

C-80

Deceleration of the core growth engine over the last twelve months

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Reported growth is at its peak and underlying growth is below the company's normal rate; the input force is not slowing but its conversion is.

Reported revenue growth of 23% in FY26 overstates the engine: excluding the £23.3m deferred programme and currency, underlying growth was about 4% in H1 FY26 and 5% to 7% for the year. The company's normal rate is hard to define because of acquisitions, but revenue compounded about 15% from FY20 to FY26 with roughly half organic; the recurring base is therefore growing below the company's historical organic rate of high single digits, and the consensus for FY27 (+1.5% to +3% reported, which is about +20% on the recurring base if achieved) depends on dated awards. Divisionally the engine is uneven: Systems ex-programme roughly flat, Power +15.6%, Components +8.1% off a destocked base. The input change force (defence budgets, autonomy doctrine, restock) has accelerated over twelve months, so this is not a case of the driver fading; it is a case of the driver being converted on a government timetable that has slipped, which is what the 15% share-price fall over three months is pricing. The honest reading is that the engine decelerated in the reported numbers because a one-off is washing out, decelerated in the underlying numbers because UK industrial demand is flat and defence awards are late, and will re-accelerate only on the CAIN follow-on and the Defence Investment Plan.

C-81

Fundamental risks that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, with plausibility and size

❌ D35

Question asked What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

(1) A second Ministry of Defence deferral (Project CAIN follow-ons, Defence Investment Plan slipping into FY28). Plausibility: medium, the most likely of the set; the precedent is November 2024 and the plan is already late. Size: 30% to 60% of forecast EPS, since the fixed base would be unabsorbed; the company's own severe case is 22% of EBITDA on a 6% revenue shortfall.

(2) Memory and semiconductor shortage making programmes temporarily unviable or deferring shipments. Plausibility: medium and rising. Size: 10% to 25% of EPS through deferred revenue and margin dilution, plus working-capital absorption.

(3) Loss or alteration of the exclusive Persistent Systems relationship, or Persistent selling direct to ministries. Plausibility: low. Size: large, since the high-margin end of Systems and the recurring communications run-rate (£23.3m in one year) would go.

(4) Loss of the 17% customer or cancellation of its programme. Plausibility: low to medium (it is a government). Size: up to 17% of revenue and a larger share of profit.

(5) A Taiwan supply disruption. Plausibility: low. Size: severe and sector-wide.

(6) A second United States integration failure or impairment at Custom Power or the antenna onshoring. Plausibility: medium. Size: small to moderate in cash, larger in credibility.

(7) An accounting event: revenue-recognition or work-in-progress adjustment, or capitalised development write-off. Plausibility: low to medium (the "false friend" profile wins the competing-hypotheses analysis but the audit is clean). Size: moderate; the damage would be to the multiple more than to the cash.

(8) Export-licence refusal, a cyber breach or a product-liability event in a defence programme. Plausibility: low. Size: moderate.

The risks cluster: a deferral triggers inventory obsolescence and unabsorbed overhead, which triggers the working-capital and accounting concerns, so the joint probability of experiencing none of them over two years is low and the correlated downside is larger than any single item.

C-82

Perception- or sentiment-driven risks that are sudden and high-magnitude

❌ D38

Question asked What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

(1) UK defence procurement headlines: any further Defence Investment Plan delay, budget reallocation or United States stopgap news hits every UK defence component share regardless of the company's order book; the sector digest records this as the mechanism of the recent fall.

(2) Small-cap AIM liquidity: £0.3m of daily value traded, one visible broker, a Fragility rating of Double Underweight in a regime that is selling small, levered or illiquid names first; a 10% to 20% move on no news is normal for this register.

(3) Peer read-across: discoverIE, TT Electronics and XP Power all report in December; a weak print or a "restock has plateaued" message would be applied to Solid State's Components leg immediately, and Volex's data-centre customer concentration story would be read across if the AI-financing crack spreads.

(4) Memory-price headlines: each report of DRAM inflation or allocation is read as a margin and delivery risk for a memory-dense integrator.

(5) Results optics: H1 FY27 revenue will be down year on year because of the comparator; a market that has not done the arithmetic can sell the headline on 15 December.

(6) A disappointing Capital Markets Day (no numeric bridge, a reframed 2030) would be read as a retreat.

(7) A collapse in a peer takeover or an activist-driven sale in the cohort changes the perceived floor for small accredited suppliers.

The share has fallen from 199p at the FY26 results to 162.5p with estimates flat, so a large part of the sentiment risk has already been realised; what remains is the half of the fall that a further deferral would add.

C-83

How far the shares could fall to a fair trough multiple

❌ D40

Question asked How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Base trough: 120p to 135p, 17% to 26% below 162.5p. Deferral trough: 85p to 105p, 35% to 48% below.

Method: the ten-year 24-month-forward P/E distribution puts the tenth percentile ("crash") at 10.9x and the current reading at 11.1x on a 24-month-forward EPS of about 14.7p that blends FY28 (13.1p) and FY29 (16.0p, one broker). On the FY27 consensus of 11.6p a 10.9x trough gives 126p; on FY28's 13.1p it gives 143p; the December 2024 low was 123p and the 52-week low 135p, both printed with lower forward estimates than today. In a repeat-deferral case EPS falls toward 7p to 8p (operating margin 4% to 5%) and the multiple, applied to depressed earnings, historically expands rather than contracts (the Risks report's bear case is 15.5x on a £5m pre-tax profit, about 95p; a 12x to 14x range on 7p gives 85p to 100p). The enterprise-value cross-check gives the same picture: at £96m of enterprise value and consensus FY27 EBITDA of £14.3m the stock is on 6.7x; at 5x trough EBITDA of £10m (an FY25-type year) the equity is worth about £46m plus net cash, or roughly 85p.

The asymmetry to carry: the current multiple is already at the first percentile of five years, so the downside from multiple compression alone is limited to about 20%; the larger downside comes from the earnings line, and only a deferral produces it.

Guidance

C526 questions
C-6852C-7055C-7158C-7245C-8554C-7348

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Guidance is conservative, honest and under-specified. The company issues no numbers of its own, adopts the thin broker average as its expectation, moves it only at the April pre-close and on firm orders, and beats it by small margins; its qualitative commentary runs ahead of the numbers on demand and behind them on timing. The medium-term ambition (20p, £250m, divisional margins of 7% to 15%) is explicit while the group margin target has been quietly dropped and the LTIP threshold for FY28 is barely above FY26, so management is signalling a wide range for the next two years rather than a path. For the model: use consensus FY27 as management's cautious case, place the central case modestly above it on FY28 and FY29 revenue, hold margin at consensus until the Capital Markets Day provides a bridge, and treat the December interim as a phasing event rather than a trend. The quality of the guidance process is good; the quantity of guidance is the problem, and it is about to be tested on a stage the company has never used.

C-68

Company guidance against sell-side consensus for this year and next, and why

C52

Question asked To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

The company gives no formal numeric guidance; it defines "market expectations" as the average of its covering brokers (Cavendish, Berenberg and Zeus) and speaks to that average, so guidance and consensus are the same number by construction. The useful comparison is between the company's qualitative steer and the consensus it has adopted.

This year (FY27, to March 2027): consensus revenue £156m to £157.5m, adjusted pre-tax profit £8.8m to £9.0m, EPS 11.6p, on an operating margin of 6.3% and a gross margin of 33.2%. The company's steer is "margins broadly stable in FY27 as investment continues ahead of revenue" (in line with the 6.3%), an order book that covers most of the year, and the chief executive's expressed "high confidence to exceed next-year market expectations" at the FY26 results (above). On revenue the steer sits at or slightly above consensus; on margin it sits at consensus; on timing it carries the Defence Investment Plan caveat that consensus does not model.

Next year (FY28): consensus EPS 12.7p (Zeus and Cavendish) to 13.1p (FactSet median), operating margin 6.6%, revenue £159m to £162m. The company's only numeric marker is the long-term incentive range of 11.5p to 16.0p for FY28, whose midpoint of 13.75p sits above consensus and whose threshold sits below it; the divisional margin targets, if reached by 2030, imply a group margin of 10% or more, well above the 6.6% consensus for FY28, but no date is attached.

Why the alignment: a single-broker history (Zeus as corporate broker) in which the company's view and the broker's model were effectively the same document; the April pre-close is the point at which the company moves the number, and it moved FY26 up by 3% on revenue and 10% on profit. The Consumer should treat consensus as management's cautious case, with the chief executive's commentary and the LTIP midpoint as the signal that management's own central expectation is modestly higher.

C-70

Record, nature and behaviour in upgrading guidance, beyond meeting or beating

C55

Question asked Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

The behaviour is that of an honest tracker that sandbags modestly and upgrades through the broker at two fixed points a year rather than through its own guidance.

Upgrade events: March 2025, the reinstated $25m order lifted FY26 EPS estimates by 20%; April 2026, the pre-close lifted FY26 revenue to not less than £150m and profit to £7.9m (consensus had £145m and £7.2m), after which the outturn beat again (£154.1m, £8.6m); June 2026, the results lifted FY27 EPS by 12.5% and introduced FY28; the interim dividend was raised 11% and the broker's dividend forecasts 6%. Before FY25 the group "achieved earnings upgrades exceeding 20% in multiple historical periods" during the FY22 to FY24 run.

Downgrade events: November 2024, FY25 and FY26 EPS cut 60% and 46% in one note after the review pause; the dividend forecast cut from 4.3p to 2.5p. Management did not kitchen-sink; it rebased to the order book it could see and then beat the rebased number (£5.0m against £4.2m).

Nature: upgrades are modest (3% to 12% at a time, apart from the one-off order reinstatement), evidence-based (an order, a pre-close), and come after the fact rather than as a forward raise; management has never raised a numeric forward guide because it does not issue one. The size of beats (1% to 2% on revenue, 3% to 9% on profit) is consistent with a deliberately low bar rather than with an outperformance culture. The one behaviour the Consumer should not expect is a pre-emptive upgrade on pipeline; this management waits for the order.

C-71

Conservatism of guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five, adjusted for the change in input forces

C58

Question asked How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Conservative on revenue, reasonable on margin, and dependent on one broker beyond FY28.

Revenue: consensus +1.5%, +3.2%, +6.9% for FY27 to FY29 against +28%, +49%, +29%, -23%, +23% for FY22 to FY26 (about 15% compound, roughly half acquired). On the recurring base the FY27 number is demanding (about 20%), but as a reported figure it is below every year of the last five except FY25, in a period when the input forces (defence budgets, autonomy doctrine, restock, AI power) are stronger than at any point in those five years. Zeus describes its own FY28 revenue growth of 2.6% as prudently modelled with scope for upgrade if awards accelerate.

Margin: consensus 6.3%, 6.6%, 7.5% against 5.0%, 7.8%, 8.8%, 3.4%, 4.8% on FactSet's basis (7.7%, 10.4%, 4.8%, 6.2% on the company's) and a 9.2% average for FY21 to FY24; the brokers model a slower recovery than the company achieved in FY21 to FY24 and well below the divisional targets, which is conservative if utilisation rises and realistic if the overhead keeps growing.

EPS: +5%, +13%, +22% from 11.0p; the FY29 figure is a single broker's and embeds a 7.5% margin, which is why the "cheap" 24-month-forward multiple partly rests on it.

Adjusted for change forces: with defence budgets up, a facility built, an order book £18m higher underlying and peers printing 20% to 30% order growth, a flat revenue forecast is conservative; with memory inflation, a 17% customer, a delayed plan and a fixed cost base, a flat margin forecast is prudent. The Consumer's base case can sit modestly above consensus on revenue for FY28 and FY29 and at consensus on FY27 margin, with the deferral case as the counterweight.

C-72

Specificity of the guidance: building blocks or vagueness

❌ D45

Question asked How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

The building blocks are named but not quantified, so the guidance is specific in structure and vague in arithmetic. Named: order-book cover (£102.4m, most shipping within eighteen months), the non-repeat of the £19m to £23.3m programme, the Project CAIN initial order (£8.0m in Q1 FY27), Power order intake (over $20m plus $7.4m), the divisional margin targets, Ashchurch utilisation as the margin lever, memory lead times and the Defence Investment Plan as the risks, capital expenditure of over £7.5m across two years, and the natural currency hedge. Not quantified: the revenue split of the order book by division and half, the utilisation rate, the share of recurring and programme revenue, the expected gross-margin effect of memory inflation, the overhead run-rate, the tax rate, the interest cost, and any bridge from 6.2% to the divisional targets. FactSet carries no segment forecasts. By the standard of the question, guidance is closer to "vague about how results come about" than to "built from stated building blocks", and the November 2026 Capital Markets Day is the first scheduled opportunity for the company to supply the blocks.

C-85

Informal and qualitative commentary against formal guidance and consensus for this year and next, and why

C54

Question asked To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Above on revenue direction, in line on margin, and below on timing certainty.

Above: the chief executive's "high confidence to exceed next-year market expectations" at the FY26 results; "accelerated but sustainable growth"; the Power division's over $20m of order intake in four months described as a primary engine of organic growth; multi-million dollar Durakool orders into AI data centres; an order book that is £18m higher underlying; the chair's defence pipeline framing; Zeus's observation that management's "strong H2 momentum in Power and Components justified lifting FY27 EPS by 12.5%" (the broker heard more than it modelled).

In line: "margins should remain broadly stable in FY27 as investment continues"; the explicit statement that the group does not expect a rapid return to historic margins; capital expenditure and inventory investment to continue.

Below: "delays to the UK Defence Investment Plan may affect the pace of order-book growth"; "component shortages may affect project timing and viability"; "further strategic inventory investment may be needed"; an acknowledgement that the European electronics market has reset to a lower base and that domestic transport is mixed.

Why: management's commentary describes a business whose demand is running ahead of the consensus and whose conversion is running behind it; the formal number splits the difference, and the broker has written down the conservative side. The Consumer should read the informal commentary as pointing to upside in the FY28 revenue line and to risk in the FY27 phasing.

C-73

Other material or uncommon features of the guidance or investor relations

❌ D48

Question asked What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

(1) No formal numeric guidance at all; expectations are the broker average, which the company names and publishes, an unusual degree of transparency about an unusually thin consensus. (2) Coverage: Zeus was the only active broker on the AlphaSense pool through FY26 with a corporate-sponsored 100% Buy; Cavendish is the only broker visible on FactSet; Berenberg initiated in December 2025; Zeus became joint broker in October 2026; the FactSet revision data for this stock is therefore a one-broker series and the dashboard's "sell-side momentum D" reflects an absence of movement, not a wave of cuts. (3) A first Capital Markets Day in November 2026 under the new chief executive and chair. (4) The H1 and H2 phasing: FY26 was 56% first-half weighted because of the programme; FY27 will be second-half weighted, so the interim print on 15 December will show revenue down year on year. (5) The April pre-close trading update is the reliable upgrade point; order-book figures are given at the interim and final results with the one-off separated. (6) The LTIP range (FY28 EPS 11.5p to 16.0p) is a disclosed management expectation band that the sell side has not commented on. (7) The FactSet revision table labels fiscal years one year behind the company's (its "FY2027" row of 13.1p is the company's FY28), which the Consumer must correct for. (8) Disclosure gaps: no utilisation, no churn, no design-win bank, no supplier concentration, no expert-call coverage of the company anywhere in the research pool. (9) Results can be delivered through platforms that allow filtering of live questions.
