Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 13,190 words · written 23 October 2026, 02:11 to 02:30 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Solid State plc (SOLI-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Written 2026-10-22 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Fiscal years end 31 March (FY27 is the year to March 2027, now running; H1 FY27 ended 30 September 2026 and reports on 15 December 2026; a Capital Markets Day is scheduled for November 2026). Money is sterling. Unless stated, profit figures are on the company's adjusted basis, which is also the brokers' basis that FactSet consensus uses: adjusted operating profit is before amortisation of acquired intangibles, share-based payments and reorganisation costs; adjusted EBITDA adds back depreciation of owned and right-of-use assets and amortisation of capitalised development. FactSet's own standardised history lines (gross margin 29.8%, adjusted EBIT margin 4.8% for FY26) sit on a different basis and are not used here except where named. "Consensus" is the FactSet median at 22 October 2026, which rests on one visible broker (Cavendish, 8 September 2026) for FY27 and FY28 and one for FY29; Zeus Capital's published figures (29 June 2026) are quoted where they differ.

How to read this set

The first set's closing steer was to build the model from programme timing and utilisation, not from a growth rate and a margin. I have done that. The base case below rests on five numbers the Consumer should hold in mind throughout: a recurring revenue base of about £131m in FY26 (reported £154.1m less the £23.3m non-repeating NATO Support and Procurement Agency programme); an adjusted overhead of £42.0m of which roughly £36m to £38m is fixed within a year; a gross margin of 33.5% that is reliable to within a point; a diluted share count of about 58m that nothing changes; and a set of defence award dates (the UK Defence Investment Plan, Project CAIN follow-on tranches, NATO framework follow-ons) that the company does not set. Everything in the P&L below operating profit is small and predictable. Everything above it is decided by the fifth number.

Where I disagree with the first set I say so in the answer concerned. The main disagreements are three: the first set's 25% to 30% incremental drop-through is right at the gross-profit line but overstates what reaches operating profit, because management has shown it reinvests most of the gain (P-01); the first set's steer to hold FY27 at consensus understates the timing risk in the second half of FY27, so my base case sits modestly below consensus for FY27 and above it for FY28 (P-06, P-10); and the first set's working-capital steer of a FY27 absorption and a FY28 release is right in percentage terms but wrong in cash terms, because growth keeps absorbing cash in FY28 (P-08).

Financial forecasts

C539 questions
P-0156P-0262P-0346P-0445P-0554P-0650P-0858P-0954P-1054

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts for the next 18 months to four years are a story about one mechanism with one dependency. The mechanism is fixed-cost operating leverage on a £42m overhead that management built ahead of revenue and will keep growing at 4% to 5% a year: each £10m of revenue above about £115m is worth £2.5m to £3m of operating profit before reinvestment and about £2m after it, and the same arithmetic removes £2.5m to £3m for every £10m that slips. The dependency is the date on which the UK Ministry of Defence and NATO procurement convert a budget that is rising 8% to 9% a year into tranches that ship from Ashchurch. Nothing else in the set moves the numbers by more than a point of margin: pricing is cost recovery, efficiency lands as 20 basis points a year of gross margin, financial leverage is immaterial and improving, below-the-line items help slightly, the feedback loops add two or three points of growth over four years, and management's record is of beating a low bar when the calendar holds and missing by half when it moves. The base case is therefore a flat FY27 (EPS about 10.8p, below consensus) and a staircase to about 14p, 17.7p, 19.8p and 21.8p (above consensus), with the operating margin at 8.6% by FY31, still short of the FY21 to FY24 average and of management's ambition, and with free cash flow and net cash rising faster than earnings. The cone is wide (FY27 EPS 7p to 12p, FY28 11p to 17.5p) and skewed down in FY27 and up in FY28. For the third set the questions that matter are whether the next two events (the November Capital Markets Day and the 15 December interim) resolve the timing of the first tranche, and how far the market has already priced the deferral case into an 11x multiple against unrebased estimates; for the fourth set the question is that consensus has the right four-year direction and the wrong shape, and that the first revision after the interim is more likely to be down for FY27 and up for FY28 than the reverse.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C56

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

The judgement in one paragraph. Solid State's operating leverage is the largest in its cohort and it has grown since FY24: the fixed overhead is now about £4m higher than at the FY24 peak, so the revenue needed to earn a 10% operating margin has risen from about £163m to about £200m. Over the next four years the leverage works in the company's favour in the base case, lifting the adjusted operating margin from 6.2% (FY26) to about 6.0% in FY27, 7.0% in FY28, 8.0% in FY29 and 8.5% to 9% by FY31, with EPS growing roughly twice as fast as revenue from FY28. But the leverage reaches the operating line only at about 22% to 25% of incremental revenue, not the 40% the gross margin would allow, because management reinvests; and in any year in which a programme slips, the same mechanism removes 25% to 30% of the shortfall from profit with no offset. The cone around FY27 operating margin is therefore 4.0% to 7.0% around a 6.0% centre, and around FY28 it is 5.5% to 8.5% around 7.0%.

What the cost base is, measured. Cost of sales was £102.5m in FY26 (66.5% of revenue), almost all bought-in components, cells, radios and modules, and therefore variable. Adjusted selling, general and administrative cost was £42.0m (27.3% of revenue), up £8.7m in one year. Inside it: staff costs of £33.1m across 476 average employees, of which the management and administrative headcount rose from 91 to 156 while selling and manufacturing roles fell from 338 to 320; about £3.3m of variable performance-related pay (nil in FY25); about £2.5m of growth investment disclosed for H1 FY26 alone; facility costs for thirteen sites, with the Ashchurch integrated systems facility adding about £3.0m a year of overhead (the AlphaSense operating-leverage report, citing the FY25 results and the FY26 annual report) against a revenue capacity of about £15m that is still largely unused. Treating the variable pay and the deferrable growth investment as flexible, the fixed element of overhead is about £36m to £38m, or 24% of FY26 revenue. On a 33.5% gross margin that puts the operating break-even at about £110m to £115m of revenue, and each £10m of revenue above it adds about £3.4m of gross profit less £0.5m to £1.0m of volume-related overhead, so £2.5m to £3.0m of operating profit before any decision to reinvest.

How the leverage has behaved, and the insight the record gives. The FY24 to FY25 decline is the clean measurement: revenue fell £38.2m (23%) and adjusted operating profit fell £11.0m, a decremental drop-through of 28.8% (AlphaSense's calculation from the FY25 results). The FY25 to FY26 recovery is the instructive one: revenue rose £29.1m and gross profit rose about £12.2m (gross margin 31.5% to 33.5%, a gross drop-through of 42%), but adjusted operating profit rose only £3.6m, an incremental drop-through of 12.3%, because management added £8.7m of overhead in the same year. Half-year data say the same: H1 FY26 revenue rose 38.7% and the incremental drop-through was about 10%. This is the point the first set's 25% to 30% figure misses. The arithmetic leverage at the gross line is about 40%; the leverage that reaches the operating line is whatever management leaves after choosing how much to reinvest, and in the last up-year it left 30% of the gross gain. The FY25 down-year shows the asymmetry: in a decline there is no reinvestment to withdraw quickly (£1.0m of annualised savings was found within months, against a £12m fall in gross profit), so the decremental rate approaches the full gross rate net of variable pay.

What I assume for reinvestment, and why. The FY26 step was exceptional (a chief executive succession, the stand-up of Ashchurch, the separation of Power, a 71% rise in administrative heads); management says the investment phase continues but at a slower pace ("margins broadly stable in FY27 as investment continues"). I assume adjusted overhead grows about 4% to 5% a year from FY27 (£42.2m FY27, £44.4m FY28, £46.4m FY29, £48.7m FY30, £51.0m FY31), made up of about 3% wage inflation on £33m of staff cost, the £0.5m Autumn Budget employer National Insurance and minimum wage effect in FY27, variable pay that rises with profit, and modest engineering hiring, offset by the small efficiencies described in P-03. Against revenue growing 6% to 8% a year from FY28, that leaves a net drop-through of 21% to 25% of incremental revenue to operating profit. If management holds overhead flat in real terms instead, the drop-through rises to 30% and FY29 EPS is about 2p higher; if it repeats a FY26-type step, the margin stalls at 6% to 7% for another year.

How it shapes each line item, over the next 18 months. Revenue: the leverage does not shape revenue, but revenue phasing shapes everything else, and the next 18 months have a hole in them. H1 FY27 (to September 2026) has no equivalent of the £23.3m programme that sat in H1 FY26, so it will report revenue down about 10% to 14% (my estimate £75.5m against £85.7m) with Project CAIN's £8.0m initial order, Power's order intake and the components restock filling only part of the gap. Gross margin: slightly up on mix (the programme was dilutive to gross margin at about 39% of Systems' 38.7% average, and Power and own-brand lines are rising), so about 33.6% for FY27, with memory inflation worth 30 to 50 basis points of lag against it. Operating margin: this is where the leverage bites. Losing £23.3m of programme revenue removes about £9m of gross profit and roughly £7m of contribution; replacing it with about £22m of other revenue at a similar gross margin restores most of it; but the full-year overhead run-rate is about £1m higher than FY26 and the H1 FY27 utilisation of Ashchurch is low. My base case is an operating margin of about 5.6% in H1 FY27 (£4.2m) and 6.4% in H2 FY27 (£5.0m), 6.0% for the year (£9.2m), against consensus 6.3% (£9.8m) and the company's "broadly stable" steer of about 6.2%. The first set's test, that an H1 FY27 operating margin below 5.5% puts the thesis at risk, is the right test and my base case passes it narrowly. EBITDA follows operating profit with a £4.6m depreciation and development amortisation add-back: about £13.8m (9.0%) for FY27 against consensus £14.3m (9.1%). EPS: about 10.8p for FY27 against consensus 11.6p and FY26's 11.0p.

Over four years. The leverage is the mechanism by which the margin recovers, and the base case has it working at a measured pace. Each year of 7% to 8% revenue growth (£12m to £14m) adds £4.5m to £5m of gross profit and £2m of overhead, so about £2.7m to £3.1m of operating profit, which is 150 to 180 basis points of group margin a year gross of other effects. That takes the group from 6.0% in FY27 to 7.0% in FY28, 8.0% in FY29, 8.3% in FY30 and 8.6% in FY31. The divisional view says the same thing by a different route: Systems carries nearly all the fixed-cost leverage (Ashchurch, Redditch, the antenna chamber; gross margin 38.7%, operating margin 14.3% in FY26 and a 12.5% to 15.0% target), so each £10m of additional Systems revenue is worth about £3.9m of gross profit and roughly £3m of operating profit, or about 190 basis points of group margin; Power has moderate leverage (battery test rooms and cell-sorting lines; 5.3% margin against a 10% to 14% target) and needs volume to reach its target; Components has almost none (distribution; 4.0% margin), and its margin moves on mix and restock volume, not on fixed-cost absorption. My base case has Systems reaching the top of its target range (15%) by FY29 on £65m of revenue, Power reaching the bottom of its range (10%) by FY30 on £49m, and Components at 6% by FY30, below its 7% to 9% target.

Against the last four to six years. FY21 to FY24 produced an average adjusted operating margin of 9.2% and a peak of 10.4% on £163.3m of revenue with £38.1m of overhead; FY25 produced 4.8% on £125.1m; FY26 6.2% on £154.1m with £42.0m. The next four years differ in three ways. First, the fixed base is larger, so the same revenue earns less: FY24's revenue would earn about 8% today. Second, the capacity is larger and newer, so the ceiling is higher: Ashchurch's headroom alone could add about 190 basis points without capital. Third, the revenue that fills it is programme-shaped, so the path is a staircase, not a slope: the record is +29%, -23%, +23% in revenue and 10.4%, 4.8%, 6.2% in margin, and the model should expect the same shape at a smaller amplitude, with FY27 the flat step. Consensus (6.3%, 6.6%, 7.5%) models a slope; my base case (6.0%, 7.0%, 8.0%) models a staircase with FY27 the tread and FY28 and FY29 the risers.

The width and skew of the cone. On the FY27 base case (revenue £153m, operating profit £9.2m), revenue £10m higher (a follow-on tranche shipped in H2) adds about £2.5m to £3m of operating profit (margin 7.2% to 7.5%, EPS 13p); revenue £10m lower (the tranche slips into FY28) removes the same (margin 4.3%, EPS 7.5p); a £20m shortfall (the FY25 template) takes operating profit toward £3m and EPS toward 4p. For FY27 the skew is negative because the half that decides it, H2 FY27, depends on an award that the Defence Investment Plan delay has already pushed back once; for FY28 the skew is positive because what slips out of FY27 lands in FY28 on top of a year of normal growth, exactly as FY26 recovered from FY25.

Peers, and why Solid State differs. discoverIE raised its margin 1.2 points in a year of 7% organic decline because capital spending is 1.5% of sales and manufacturing is local-for-local and variable; XP Power's margin went from 4.3% to 7.9% in a year as its Vietnam site loaded; TT Electronics gained 230 basis points in H1 2026 from Cleveland's turnaround and Plano's closure; Volex reached 10.2% through vertical integration and site consolidation. Solid State's leverage is higher than discoverIE's because its overhead-to-revenue ratio is higher and its capacity newer, and it has no self-help programme of the TT or Volex kind, so its margin is more purely a function of volume. The sector note of 6 October 2026 states the rule: own the asset-light designers through the cycle and buy the fixed-cost makers at the turn; Solid State is the fixed-cost maker, and the turn is now if the awards land.

Indicators. H1 FY27 operating margin against 5.5% (15 December 2026); the Capital Markets Day's utilisation figure for Ashchurch and any overhead run-rate guidance (November 2026); the FY27 SG&A outturn against £42.2m (June 2027); the order book at 30 November 2026 and the split between programme and recurring revenue.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years

C62

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

The judgement. Financial leverage is immaterial to EPS and will become more so: net debt of £4.2m excluding leases (£10.1m including £5.9m of lease liabilities) at March 2026 falls to about £3.7m at March 2027 and the group is at or near net cash by March 2028 in my base case, accumulating about £12m of net cash by March 2030 and about £19m by March 2031 if nothing is spent on acquisitions or buybacks. The balance sheet contributes about 0.1p to 0.2p of EPS growth a year from falling interest and otherwise neither amplifies nor dampens the operating line. Its importance for the model is twofold: it removes liquidity from the list of risks even in an FY25-type deferral, and it creates an unused option (a buyback or a bolt-on acquisition) worth 8% to 12% of EPS if exercised, which the base case does not assume.

The measurement. Net interest expense was £1.2m in FY26 on FactSet's reported basis (£1.1m FY25, £1.5m FY24); on the adjusted basis the gap between adjusted operating profit (£9.6m) and adjusted pre-tax profit (£8.6m) is £1.0m, which includes about £0.3m of lease interest and excludes £0.09m of imputed interest on deferred consideration. The committed facility is a £15.0m multi-currency revolving credit facility with Lloyds and Comerica with a £10.0m uncommitted accordion, priced at 2.3% to 2.6% over SONIA, EURIBOR or SOFR depending on leverage (the Earnings Review's separate description of a £30m facility is not reconciled, as the first set noted; the £15m plus £10m figure is the annual report's). Cash was £6.6m. Net debt to EBITDA was 0.7x on FactSet's lease-inclusive definition and below 0.5x on the company's covenant basis. A 100 basis point move in rates is worth about £0.1m of pre-tax profit, or 0.1p of EPS.

The path. Base case free cash flow after tax, interest, lease payments and all capital spending (the strict definition used in the cross-check) is about £2.4m in FY27, £5.9m in FY28, £8.0m in FY29, £9.1m in FY30 and £10.3m in FY31, against dividends of £1.7m rising to £2.7m and about £0.2m a year of treasury purchases for employee schemes. Net debt therefore runs £3.7m (March 2027), about nil (March 2028), net cash £5.6m (March 2029), £12.1m (March 2030), £19.4m (March 2031). Zeus has net debt of £2.1m at March 2027 and net cash of £0.2m at March 2028, so the paths agree to within £1.5m; mine is slightly more conservative in FY27 because I carry a larger strategic inventory build and higher capital spending. Net financial items in the model fall from about £0.9m in FY27 to £0.7m, £0.5m, £0.3m and £0.2m, with lease interest of about £0.3m a year the floor until the cash balance earns interest.

What it does to EPS. Falling interest adds about 0.2p to EPS between FY26 and FY29; nothing else. The share count is unaffected (no buyback, no cash issuance). The sensitivity the Consumer should note is the one that runs the other way: in a working-capital spike of the H1 FY25 kind (net debt reached £13.1m, 2.0x trailing EBITDA on FactSet's calculation, when a large receivable sat unpaid), interest would rise by perhaps £0.3m for a half-year, which is 0.4p of EPS, and the covenant basis would still be comfortable. In the FY25-template deferral case (operating profit about £3m), the group would still generate cash because working capital unwinds in a downturn (FY25 cash conversion was 173%), so the balance sheet does not become a constraint even in the bad case.

The balance sheet over four years, and the unused option. By FY29 the base case has net cash of about £5.6m and by FY30 about £12m, against a market capitalisation of about £98m. A business with 14% return on capital employed (rising toward 20% on my margin path), 2% capital intensity and no acquisition pipeline of size will have a lazy balance sheet by FY29. A £10m buyback at about 165p would retire about 6m shares (10.5% of the count) and lift EPS by about 10%; a Gateway-type bolt-on (£1.4m for 40%-plus gross margin products) is earnings-enhancing from year one by management's own screen. Neither is in the base case because management has shown no inclination to use either (the first set's C-19 and C-64), and the capital-allocation framework (hurdle rates, leverage range, return priorities) is undisclosed. The Capital Markets Day is the point at which a framework could appear; if it names a leverage range of, say, 1x to 1.5x EBITDA, the implied capacity is £20m to £30m of deployable capital, which is the single largest unmodelled positive for FY29 to FY31 EPS. The Consumer should carry this as upside to the base case rather than in it.

Peers. Volex ran 0.8x leverage and launched a £40m (later £20m) buyback; discoverIE carries a £240m revolver for acquisitions; TT Electronics is deleveraging after restructuring. Solid State is the least levered and the least active allocator in the cohort, which is a comfort for predictability and a cost for EPS growth.

Indicators. Net debt at 30 September 2026 (expected to be modestly above March on inventory and the interim dividend); any capital-allocation framework at the Capital Markets Day; the FY27 year-end net debt against about £3.7m.

P-03

Scope for cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the main line items, and when

❌ D46

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

The judgement. The scope for cost elimination is small and the scope for efficiency is real but reaches the gross-margin line rather than the overhead line. Over the next 18 months no net cost reduction reaches operating profit: about £0.5m to £1.0m a year of identifiable efficiency (back-office alignment in Power, in-house recruitment, the India transfer of own-brand manufacture, the end of Active Silicon's dual-site costs) is more than offset by the £0.5m Autumn Budget cost, wage inflation of about £1.0m and the full-year run-rate of FY26's hires. Over four years, efficiency shows up as 30 to 50 basis points a year of gross-margin improvement from mix and the exit of low-value work, worth about £0.5m to £0.8m of operating profit a year, and as overhead growing one to two points slower than revenue, which is the whole of the operating-leverage story in P-01 restated. In a downturn, management can reach £2m to £3m of annualised savings within six months (the FY25 precedent) and £4m to £6m within twelve, enough to hold the operating margin at about 4% in an FY25-type year but not to protect it. The business is built to grow into its cost base, not to cut it, and the Consumer should not model a cost programme.

What is cuttable, by line. Cost of sales (£102.5m): not cuttable, reducible only through mix and sourcing. Staff costs (£33.1m, 476 people): the discretionary layer is the administrative headcount that grew by 65 in FY26 and the £3.3m of variable pay; the 80 technical engineers and the manufacturing workforce are protected by the strategy. The FY25 response shows the speed and the limit: £1.0m of annualised savings executed within months of the November 2024 deferral, over £3.0m of overhead savings during FY25 in total, £2.2m of variable costs and site-closure overheads that did not recur (the finance director, December 2024), termination costs of £0.43m, and adjusted overhead falling only from £38.1m to £33.3m (£4.8m, 13%) while revenue fell 23%. Facility costs: thirteen sites, with Ashchurch and the antenna chamber newly added and strategic; Active Silicon's consolidation to Langley shows site rationalisation is possible at the margins, but the £2m to £3m of unabsorbed overhead at Ashchurch is the thesis and would be kept. Growth investment (£2.5m in H1 FY26): deferrable, and deferred in FY25. Capital expenditure (over £7.5m across FY27 and FY28): deferrable at the cost of the United States antenna plan.

What efficiency is actually being done, and what it is worth. The evidence lists four programmes, none with a disclosed saving. (1) The final phase of aligning support structures in Power (the FY26 annual report), following the Custom Power USA restructuring that "substantially outperformed previous projections" without further impairment; worth perhaps £0.3m to £0.5m a year. (2) In-house recruitment that sourced 78% of FY26 hires without agency fees; worth perhaps £0.2m to £0.3m a year against the prior pattern. (3) The "China plus one" transfer of Durakool manufacture to India; a gross-margin item in Components, worth perhaps 20 to 40 basis points of divisional gross margin over two years. (4) Group systems (CRM, ERP standardisation, AI tools) and the Commercial Project Office; a productivity item whose value is in bid throughput, not cost. Set against them: the Autumn Budget's employer National Insurance and minimum wage changes (over £0.5m a year from April 2026), wage inflation on £33m of staff cost (about £1.0m at 3%), the full-year cost of the senior appointments made during FY26 (Chief Technology Officer, Chief Relationship Officer, United States Chief Sales Officer, Head of Operations at Custom Power USA), and the antenna facility's overhead. Net, overhead rises about £0.2m in FY27 before any volume-related or variable-pay effect, which is what my £42.2m assumes.

The efficiency that does reach the P&L: gross margin. The record is clear that Solid State's efficiency gains land in cost of sales, not overhead. Components gross margin rose from 25.0% to 26.8% in FY26 by discontinuing low-margin legacy United States manufacturing lines; Power's rose from 31.7% to 35.8% by exiting commodity assembly and glass-to-metal seal work (a 400 basis point gain for a short-term volume loss); group gross margin rose from 31.5% to 33.5%, of which perhaps half was mix within divisions and half the shift toward Systems and Power. The remaining levers of this kind are own-brand mix in Components (Gateway at 40% to 43% gross margin, Durakool, Pacer; own-brand share undisclosed but a minority), design-in content, through-life support in Systems, and the antenna business whose product margins the finance director put at 60% to over 70% (December 2024 call). I assume these add 20 basis points a year to group gross margin from FY28 (33.6% FY27, 33.8% FY28, 34.0% FY29, 34.1% FY30, 34.2% FY31), net of memory and semiconductor inflation passed through at a lag. Each 20 basis points is worth about £0.35m of operating profit on £170m of revenue, or 0.5p of EPS.

Timing. FY27: nothing net; the Autumn Budget cost and wage inflation absorb the identifiable savings, and the gross-margin gain is offset by the memory pass-through lag. FY28: the Power back-office alignment and India transfer are complete, variable pay rises with profit, so overhead grows about 5% and gross margin gains 20 basis points; net efficiency contribution about £0.5m. FY29 to FY31: the same pattern, about £0.5m to £0.8m a year.

Peers, and the contrast. TT Electronics' Project Dynamo delivers £3m in 2026 and over £6m annualised from 2027 on a £481m revenue base, with £15.2m of restructuring cost in 2025 and £1.4m in H1 2026; Volex exited 400 negative-margin part numbers and consolidated sites to go from 2.8% to 10.2%; XP Power closed China and moved to Vietnam for a 450 basis point gross-margin gain. Each had a structural problem to fix. Solid State has none of that scale of inefficiency to remove, which is to its credit as an operator and its disadvantage as a self-help story: its margin recovery has to come from volume. The one structural inefficiency it does have, thirteen locations on £154m of revenue (about £12m per site, bottom-decile in the cohort), is a consequence of the acquisition history and is not being addressed, and I would not expect it to be while the group is in a capacity-building phase.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-77) put £4m to £6m of annual cost as reducible within six to twelve months in a downturn. I agree with the figure for twelve months and would put six months at £2m to £3m, the FY25 pace. More important, I would add that the number is a downturn measure only; in the base case the cuttable cost is never cut, and the Consumer should treat the P-03 contribution to the forecasts as a gross-margin item of about 20 basis points a year and nothing at the overhead line.

Indicators. FY27 adjusted overhead against £42.2m; Components and Power gross margins at the interim; any disclosed own-brand revenue share at the Capital Markets Day; headcount in the FY27 annual report (administrative heads against 156).

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years

❌ D45

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

The judgement. Price contributes to revenue and not to margin. Over the next 18 months pass-through of memory and semiconductor inflation adds about 1% to 2% to reported revenue (£2m to £3m a year) at a constant pound margin, which arithmetically lowers the gross margin percentage by 20 to 40 basis points while leaving gross profit unchanged, with a one to two quarter lag during which the margin dips before it recovers. Over four years there is no evidence that supports modelling real price increases above cost inflation anywhere in the group, so the gross-margin improvement in the model (33.5% to 34.2%) is entirely mix and utilisation, as P-03 describes. The one place real price could appear is the through-life support and spiral-upgrade tail on deployed tactical radio networks and the antenna business, both high-margin and both small; I treat them as mix. The practical risk is not that price is lost but that price is refused: management itself says some component price rises could make programmes temporarily unviable, so the cost of pricing power in this cycle is deferred revenue, not lower margin. Sterling matters more than price: a 10% move is worth £0.7m to £0.8m of pre-tax profit, about 9% of FY27 profit, with no hedge.

The evidence on price, condition by condition. The AlphaSense pricing report (22 October 2026, 115 sources) and the first set agree on the character of the record. (1) In the 2021 to 2023 shortage, suppliers raised prices 7% to 25% and Solsta passed them to customers in full; gross margin stayed in a 29% to 31.7% band; franchised distributors kept none of the lift (expert call, October 2022). (2) In the FY25 downturn, gross margin held at 31.5% while revenue fell 23%: prices did not fall, margins fell through under-absorption. (3) On United States tariffs, management passed them on with administration charges at a cost of about 10 basis points of gross margin and some order deferral as customers shortened schedules (April 2025, interim report December 2025). (4) On the NSPA and CAIN programmes, pricing is back-to-back with suppliers, non-cancellable, with deposits (contract liabilities £6.3m) and milestone billing; the margin is protected before delivery but the channel margin on Persistent Systems hardware is set by the supplier and not disclosed. (5) On the current memory squeeze, the company says it is "pro-actively collaborating with customers" and holds strategic buffer stock; Zeus holds FY27 gross margin at 33.2% against 33.5%, the cost-recovery case. (6) The FY26 gross-margin rise of 200 basis points was mix and execution: Systems fell from 39.7% to 38.7% because the high-volume programme was dilutive, Power rose from 31.7% to 35.8% on the exit of low-margin work, Components rose from 25.0% to 26.8% on the exit of legacy United States lines. No disclosure in five years attributes any gross-margin movement to price.

What that means line by line. Revenue: in FY27 and FY28 I include about £2m to £3m a year of inflation pass-through within the growth rates (that is, the volume growth is about one point lower than the reported growth). Gross margin: the pass-through lag costs about 30 basis points in FY27 (the memory price rises began near the FY26 year-end and the contracts reprice at order or delivery), recovered in FY28; the model's 33.6% for FY27 is mix of +40 basis points less lag of -30 basis points. Operating margin and EPS: no contribution from price; the memory lag is worth about £0.4m, or 0.5p of EPS, in FY27. Working capital: pass-through pricing at higher input costs raises inventory and receivables in pounds, which is one reason FY27 working capital rises to about 21.5% of revenue.

The condition that matters most: memory inflation and programme viability. Memory prices are rising sharply (expert work expects DRAM and HBM prices to rise 10% to 15% a year; one hardware maker reported critical memory component prices up to 115% higher in March 2026; power discretes on 40 to 52 week lead times). For an integrator of memory-dense ruggedised computers the exposure is direct. Three outcomes are possible for a given programme: the customer accepts the repriced bill of materials (the defence and medical pattern, where the component is a small share of platform cost and qualification prevents switching); the customer defers until prices settle (the risk management names); or Solid State absorbs the difference to hold the order (the risk the sell side names). The evidence favours the first for Systems and Power (sovereign customers, non-cancellable terms, deposits) and the second for Components (price-sensitive industrial customers; Foxx Development's customers refused memory-driven price rises in September 2026 and volumes fell). My base case therefore carries the memory effect as a 30 basis point gross-margin lag in FY27 and as a small revenue deferral risk inside the FY27 revenue cone, not as a margin loss.

The latent real price. Three places could produce real price over four years, and none is in the base case. (1) Through-life support, training and spiral upgrades on the installed base of MPU5 and Wave Relay networks (over 2,000 Royal Marines radios, the NSPA deliveries, CAIN): the AlphaSense feedback-loop report infers 15% to 20% of hardware value over five years; support pricing is set by Solid State's own engineering, not by a supplier price list, and would carry a higher margin than hardware. (2) The antenna business, with product margins of 60% to over 70%, where United States onshore production and approved-supplier status give some pricing freedom. (3) Allocation pricing in the Components distribution business during the memory squeeze, where a specialist with stock can price availability; the sector digest's "use, refill or price" split applies, but Solsta's franchised lines pass through rather than keep. If the first two grow as the base case assumes, they lift Systems' gross margin by mix; if management prices them aggressively, there is 50 to 100 basis points of group gross margin above the base case by FY30. The test the first set set (C-45) stands: FY27 gross margin at or above 33% is cost recovery; above 34% is price retention.

Customer concentration and the ceiling. One customer was 17% of FY26 revenue (£26.7m) and a single programme 15%; the Ministry of Defence is the gatekeeper for CAIN and the Defence Investment Plan. That customer's bargaining power caps price on new tranches, and follow-on orders are typically priced at or below the initial order. The base case therefore assumes follow-on CAIN and NSPA tranches at the same margin as the initial orders, not higher.

Peers, for calibration. discoverIE's customers accept 3% to 5% annual increases on parts that are 3% to 5% of system cost, and its gross margin is 40.5%; Diploma's Peerless aerospace business ran gross margins above 50% on spot pricing and is moderating them for volume; XP Power attributed part of its H1 2026 gross-margin rise to pricing on five to six month lead times; TT Electronics lost 120 basis points in Power in H1 2026 while customer pricing agreements were finalised; Volex passes tariffs through in 100% of cases and indexes copper. Solid State sits at the defensive end of this range with Volex and TT, not at the design-in end with discoverIE and Diploma, because 39% of its revenue is franchised distribution and its largest programmes resell a sole-source radio. The gross-margin gap to discoverIE (about seven points) is structural, not a pricing opportunity.

Sterling. About 40% of purchases are in dollars with no forward contracts at 31 March 2026 and only natural matching as protection; the disclosed sensitivity is +£0.67m of pre-tax profit per 10% sterling strengthening (lower dollar costs) and -£0.82m per 10% weakening. Broker forecasts for sterling over 12 to 24 months range from 1.28 to 1.39 against the dollar, which spans about ±4% from the current rate and therefore about ±£0.3m of pre-tax profit, or ±0.4p of EPS. The model carries no currency effect in the base case but the Consumer should treat sterling as a separate driver of the same size as the memory lag.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-17, C-45) is right that price should be modelled as cost recovery with a one to two quarter lag. I would add two points. First, cost recovery itself inflates reported revenue in an inflation year, so the FY27 reported growth rate flatters volume by about a point. Second, the real cost of pricing power in this cycle is timing, not margin: the model's revenue cone should be wider for memory, and its margin cone need not be.

Indicators. H1 FY27 gross margin against 33.0% (below says the lag is biting, above 34% says price is being kept); any statement at the interim or the Capital Markets Day about programmes deferred on component cost; the Components division's gross margin against 26.8%; sterling against the dollar at the March 2027 balance-sheet date.

P-05

Reinforcing or balancing feedback loops: how they shape the main line items over the next 18 months to four years

C54

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

The judgement. Four reinforcing loops and four balancing loops are at work, and over the next 18 months the balancing loops win on points; over four years the reinforcing loops are strong enough to change the shape of the revenue line (smoother, with a growing recurring layer) and to add perhaps two to three points of annual growth, but not strong enough to make defence revenue compounding in the way discoverIE's design-win bank or Diploma's service-proximity loop compounds. The practical consequence for the model is that I treat the recurring base (£131m in FY26) as growing 5% to 6% a year with the reinforcing loops contributing about half of that, and treat programme revenue (£20m to £30m a year) as a separate, lumpy block whose timing the loops do not control. The loops also tell the Consumer which lines to watch: the installed-base loop shows up first in Systems gross margin and order-book quality, the capacity loop in Systems revenue per site, and the balancing loops in inventory days and overhead growth.

The reinforcing loops, each with its evidence, its size and what stops it.

(1) The tactical-radio installed-base loop. Each deployment of Persistent Systems MPU5 radios and Wave Relay networks through Steatite (over 2,000 Royal Marines radios fully fielded by October 2025, the £23.3m NSPA delivery in H1 FY26, the £8.0m CAIN initial order delivered in Q1 FY27) creates a base that needs training, through-life support, radio-over-IP gateways, situational-awareness software integration and spiral upgrades, and raises the switching cost for the customer because the software ecosystem would have to be reconfigured. The evidence it is operating: management's description (November and December 2025) of large communications contracts as "irregular initial revenues followed by recurring revenue from after-sale services"; Steatite's development of spiral upgrades and mobile command systems; the Rheinmetall Canada (March 2024) and United States Navy (five years of littoral testing) precedents for ecosystem expansion. The size: the AlphaSense report's inference is 15% to 20% of initial hardware value over five years; on roughly £50m of hardware deployed through Steatite since FY23 that is £1.5m to £2.0m a year of support revenue by FY28, growing with each tranche, at a gross margin above the hardware's. What stops it: a budget freeze that extends the life of existing gear (Radiocoms Systems' February 2026 accounts warn of UK departments delaying capital projects); Persistent selling direct or changing channel terms; the Ministry of Defence choosing a different waveform for the wider Army beyond 16 Air Assault Brigade. Confidence that the loop exists: high; that it is material inside four years: medium; the proportion and the period are inference.

(2) The sovereign-capacity-to-programme loop. Accredited dual UK and United States capacity (Ashchurch, Fountain Valley, Q-Par USA's approved-supplier status with Lockheed Martin and Thales) qualifies the group for larger Tier 2 sub-system bids, which load the capacity, which earns further accreditation and references. Evidence: the DSTL cabinet contract (£1.65m to £1.7m) at Ashchurch; the annual report's account of a cross-divisional integrated power-and-computing solution for a next-generation defence platform under the "3Cs" model, described as creating "repeatable opportunities across similar platforms"; the UK Segmented Acquisition Model (April 2026) and the 10% novel-technology procurement mandate (June 2025), which favour qualified suppliers of uncrewed-platform electronics. Size: this is the loop that fills Ashchurch's £15m headroom and is worth up to 190 basis points of group margin; its pace is set by the Defence Investment Plan. What stops it: a second deferral, which leaves the capacity idle and the loop running backward (unabsorbed overhead reduces the profit available to fund bids). Precedent: TT Electronics' Boxer position grew from a battery control unit award in 2021 to £16m of content by 2022 through cross-selling, then its North American sites failed under the complexity of rapid growth in 2024. Confidence: medium.

(3) The Power autonomy design-in loop. Custom battery packs designed into uncrewed air, maritime and subsea platforms (over $20m of orders in four months, $7.4m of wins across Tier 1 UK and United States primes) give multi-year platform revenue as the platforms scale, and the Volklec sovereign cell collaboration (September 2025) adds a UK end-to-end offer aligned with the Strategic Defence Review. Evidence: Power revenue up 15.6% and margin from 1.0% to 5.3% in FY26; the 20/40/40 doctrine. Size: my base case has Power growing 11% to 15% a year to about £52m by FY31. What stops it: heavy-vehicle electrification remains experimental (a former L3Harris chief engineer, June 2025); Volklec's cells rest on technology licensed from a Chinese maker, which could limit security-conscious buyers; a shortening of programme lives as disposable platforms proliferate. Confidence: medium.

(4) The own-brand and acquired-asset maturation loop. Gateway (40% to 43% gross margin), Durakool (AI data-centre contactors), Pacer and Solsta Embedded lift Components' mix and gross margin each year without new capital; discoverIE's record (design-win bank of about £385m lifetime value, margin up ten points since FY14) is the sector's proof that this compounds. Size at Solid State: about 20 basis points a year of group gross margin. What stops it: supplier franchise termination; AI design tools eroding the application-engineering value-add. Confidence: medium-high that it runs, low that it is material inside four years.

The balancing loops.

(1) Working capital. Growth absorbs cash: net working capital has run 19% to 24% of revenue since FY23 (£30.6m, 19.8%, at March 2026) with swings of £4m to £7m between half-years on milestone billing and programme inventory (work in progress up 147% to £10.96m ahead of CAIN; inventory provisions £5.3m). Each £10m of revenue growth absorbs about £2m of cash, and memory inflation raises the pound value of the same stock. The finance director says working capital has never constrained taking on a programme and the facility covers spikes; the Volex precedent (cash conversion 64% in FY26 as data-centre growth required $55m of working capital) shows what the loop does at scale. In the model: working capital at 21.5% of revenue in FY27 (strategic memory stock), easing to 20% by FY29, which still absorbs £1m to £2m a year. This is the loop that keeps free cash flow at 60% to 80% of adjusted net income until FY29.

(2) Overhead ahead of revenue. Investing ahead (£8.7m of SG&A in FY26, Ashchurch's £3m, the antenna chamber, over £7.5m of capital spending to come) compresses the margin, which limits the profit available for engineering investment (technical headcount about 80, flat, while administrative headcount rose 71%). discoverIE's deliberate £4.4m reinvestment took its margin down 0.4 points and its chief executive says new engineering resource takes over a year to pay back; the former Variohm director's claim that half of discoverIE's mix is commoditised with engineering flat is the sector's warning that this loop can hollow out the value-add. At Solid State the loop shows as a flat FY27 margin and a risk that the engineering bench does not scale with the ambition.

(3) Supplier concentration. The exclusive Persistent Systems relationship, single-sourced microprocessors and sensors, Taiwan, and memory makers prioritising AI mean that the more Steatite's radio business grows, the more its margin and cadence depend on one supplier's pricing and allocation; growth strengthens the dependency rather than the moat. Nothing in the evidence shows the terms changing, but nothing shows them either.

(4) The ministry cadence. Programme revenue arrives on a government timetable; the larger the programme share of revenue becomes, the more the group's reported results swing with it (one customer 17% of revenue, one programme 15%). The reinforcing loops above each increase the programme share; the balancing loop is that they increase the lumpiness too, until the through-life tail is large enough to smooth it, which on the sizes above is beyond four years.

How the loops shape the line items. Revenue: the reinforcing loops contribute about half of the 5% to 6% recurring-base growth in the base case (about £3m to £4m a year), and they raise the probability that each programme is followed by another, which is why my FY28 and FY29 revenue sits above consensus; they do not set the dates. Gross margin: the support tail, own-brand mix and antenna growth add the 20 basis points a year the model carries. Operating margin: the capacity loop is the leverage of P-01. Working capital and free cash flow: the balancing loops hold conversion below 100% of adjusted net income through FY28. Share count and balance sheet: unaffected.

Net. The business model is still a lumpy programme business with a recurring layer forming beneath it; the reinforcing loops are real, dated by evidence from 2023 to 2026, and slow; the balancing loops are mechanical and immediate. The answer to the question the feedback-loop report was asked (whether the installed base and sovereign capacity make defence revenue recurring and compounding over four years) is: partly, with the recurring share of Systems revenue rising from perhaps 5% to 10% to 15% by FY30, which is enough to narrow the cone from FY29 but not to remove the programme dependency from FY27 and FY28.

Indicators. Any disclosure of through-life support or programme-partnership revenue at the Capital Markets Day; the NSPA framework's first non-UK follow-on; Power order intake at the interim; inventory days and work in progress at 30 September 2026; the ratio of engineering to administrative headcount in the FY27 annual report.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction they should be leaned

C50

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

The judgement. The record supports a bimodal weighting, not a single discount. In years when the customer's calendar held, management set a low bar and beat it by 3% to 9% at the profit line (FY22, FY23, FY24, FY26) and upgraded through the broker at two fixed points (the April pre-close and the June results); in the one year the calendar moved (FY25), expectations were cut 60% in a month. So the forecasts should carry moderate weight as the modal outcome and be leaned in two directions at once: down for FY27, where the probability-weighted EPS (about 10.3p) sits below the modal outcome (about 11.5p to 12p) and below consensus (11.6p) because the second half depends on a dated award that has already slipped once; up for FY28 and FY29, where consensus revenue growth of 3% and 7% is below the record, below the input forces and below management's own long-term incentive midpoint (13.75p for FY28 against consensus 12.7p to 13.1p), and where anything that slips out of FY27 lands. For the four-year horizon, management's 2030 ambition (£250m of revenue, 20p of EPS) should carry low weight as a dated target and moderate weight as a direction: my base case reaches about £190m and 20p a year late, in FY31.

The record, measured. One year: FY26 beat the original consensus by 6% on revenue (£154.1m against £145.0m) and 19% on adjusted pre-tax profit (£8.6m against £7.2m), and beat the raised April bar by 1% and 9%. Three years: FY24 met a record year; FY25 missed the original expectation by 67% at the pre-tax line (£5.0m against £15.0m) for an external reason and then beat the rebased figure by 19% (£5.0m against £4.2m); FY26 beat. Five years: FY22 and FY23 brought upgrades of over 20% in several periods; the dividend was cut once (FY25) and restored. Across FY22 to FY26 the company met or beat the expectation it had set in every year except one, and the one miss was larger than all the beats combined. Upgrades have been evidence-based and modest (3% to 12.5%) apart from the one-off order reinstatement (+20% in March 2025), and management has never issued a numeric forward guide, so "guidance" and consensus are one number by construction.

What the record says about the direction of the lean, line by line. Revenue: management's qualitative commentary has run ahead of the number (the chief executive's "high confidence to exceed next-year market expectations" in June 2026; Power's order intake "a primary engine"; the order book £18m higher underlying), and the broker has written down the cautious side (Zeus calls its FY28 growth of 2.6% "prudently modelled with scope for upgrade"). Lean FY28 and FY29 revenue up. But for FY27 the revenue depends on H2 shipments against a Defence Investment Plan that is still unpublished and a memory squeeze that management says could defer programmes; lean FY27 down modestly. Gross margin: the record is a 31% to 33.5% band and Zeus's 33.2% is at the cautious end; hold. Operating margin: the record says the margin follows volume with a drop-through of 25% to 30% gross and about 22% net of reinvestment (P-01); consensus 6.3%, 6.6%, 7.5% is a slope; lean FY27 slightly down and FY28 up. Below the line: interest, tax and share count have been forecast accurately and should carry full weight. Cash: consensus capital expenditure (£2.8m, £2.8m, £1.7m) is below the company's stated plan (over £7.5m across FY27 and FY28) and should be corrected up; consensus free cash flow is on FactSet's pre-lease, pre-intangible definition and is about £2m a year too high on a strict definition.

The weight to give each year. FY27: the modal outcome is at or slightly above consensus (the company has not warned, the order book covers most of the year, Q1 included CAIN's £8m), but the weight on the mode should be no more than 55%, with 30% on a partial slip (EPS about 9.5p to 10p) and 15% on an FY25-type deferral (EPS about 7p). The probability-weighted FY27 EPS is about 10.3p; my base path uses 10.8p, which is the mode after a timing haircut. FY28: the probability-weighted figure (about 14.2p) is above the base path (14.0p) and above consensus (13.1p), because a deferral in FY27 raises FY28 as FY26 was raised by FY25's deferral; weight the consensus at 40% and lean up. FY29: the consensus is one broker (Cavendish; 16.0p) and should be read as a scenario; my base case of about 17.7p is above it on revenue and margin; weight low, lean up. FY30 and FY31: no consensus; the company's 20p ambition and divisional margin targets are the only external markers; my base reaches 19.8p in FY30 and 21.8p in FY31.

The signal in management's own numbers. Two markers deserve more weight than the sell side gives them. The long-term incentive plan granted in May 2026 vests between FY28 adjusted EPS of 11.5p and 16.0p: the threshold is only 0.5p above FY26's outturn, which says management regards a near-flat two years as possible; the midpoint of 13.75p sits above consensus, which says management's central expectation is modestly above the broker's; the ceiling of 16.0p is the stretch and matches Cavendish's FY29 figure a year early. The second marker is the quiet withdrawal of the group 12% operating margin target from the FY26 annual report in favour of "margins broadly stable in FY27": management is managing the near-term expectation down and the long-term ambition up, which is consistent with a staircase path and with my lean.

Peers, for the base rate. TT Electronics missed its 10% margin target for years before a turnaround; discoverIE delivered ten consecutive upgrades on a design-in model; Volex beat its FY27 targets a year early on data-centre demand; Gooch & Housego needed 60% of its profit in H2. Solid State's record sits between TT (missed a dated target) and discoverIE (compounded quietly): it has met undated expectations and missed when a date moved. The base rate for a fixed-cost defence electronics supplier loading a new facility with government volume (the AlphaSense precedents: Astra Microwave's multi-year ramp; United States obligation failures; Duratec's missed revenue targets on defence delays) is that loading takes longer than planned and slips at least once. The Consumer should assume one slip in four years and that it has a 30% to 40% chance of being FY27.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (Guidance and Sell-side group judgements) said to use FY27 consensus as management's cautious case and to place the central case modestly above it for FY28 and FY29. I keep the second half and change the first: FY27 consensus is management's cautious case for the demand level but not for the timing, and the probability-weighted centre is below it. The difference is about 0.8p of EPS, within the noise of the result, but it matters for the next two events: the H1 FY27 print and the H2 phasing are where a miss would come from, and the sell side has not modelled the deferral case at all.

Indicators. The November trading update (whether it carries an H2-weighted phrase or a follow-on order); the H1 FY27 order book against £102.4m; whether the April 2027 pre-close moves the FY27 number up (the habitual pattern) or down (the FY25 pattern); the Capital Markets Day's handling of the 12% target.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C58

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

The judgement. Everything below operating profit is small, stable and improving, and the Consumer can model it with more confidence than any line above it. Net financial items fall from about £1.0m to £0.2m as the group moves to net cash; the adjusted tax rate holds at about 25%; the diluted share count rises about 0.2m to 0.3m a year (0.4% to 0.5%) from option exercises and the May 2026 LTIP, with no buyback and no cash issuance; the dividend grows with earnings at a payout of 22% to 28%; and the gap between adjusted and reported profit narrows from £2.8m to about £2.1m as acquired-intangible amortisation runs off. The net effect is that adjusted EPS grows about 1% a year faster than adjusted operating profit, reported EPS grows faster still (about 21% a year from a low base against 15% for adjusted EPS), and free cash flow on a strict definition rises from about £2.4m in FY27 to about £10m by FY31, roughly double the £4.4m FactSet-definition average of FY22 to FY26. The three things that are different from the last four to six years are the absence of acquisition spending (nil against £33m over FY22 to FY25), the absence of deal-related interest and imputed interest, and the shift from net debt to net cash; the thing that is the same is that the share count does nothing.

Line by line, with the FY26 base and the forecast.

Amortisation of acquired intangibles: £1.76m in FY26, stable for three years, classified in head-office overhead and added back in adjusted profit (the brokers follow the company). It runs off as the Custom Power (August 2022), Active Silicon and Willow (FY22) intangibles age: about £1.7m FY27, £1.6m FY28, £1.4m FY29, £1.2m FY30, £1.0m FY31. It is excluded from the model's depreciation and amortisation line, as the form requires, and from adjusted EPS; it reduces reported EPS by about 2.3p a year falling to 1.3p.

Reorganisation and deal costs: £0.68m in FY26, £0.43m FY25 (plus £0.91m in H1 FY25 on another count), £0.5m FY24; headcount rationalisation, terminal payments and earn-out adjustments. These have recurred for three years and the first set was right to call about £0.5m a year a real cost. I carry £0.5m a year as a cash cost below adjusted EBITDA (it reduces free cash flow in the cross-check by £0.5m a year) and exclude it from adjusted EPS, following the company's and the brokers' convention, but the Consumer should know that adjusted EPS overstates economic EPS by about 0.6p a year on this item.

Share-based payments: about £0.4m a year, rising modestly with the May 2026 grant; excluded from adjusted profit; non-cash.

Impairment: £2.7m on Custom Power goodwill in FY25; none in FY26 and Custom Power USA is now outperforming its projections; none assumed. The plausible impairment event over four years is a United States onshoring problem at the antenna business, small in cash.

Net financial items: £1.0m on the adjusted basis in FY26 (bank interest on an average of about £8m of drawn debt at about 7%, lease interest of about £0.3m, commitment fees), £1.2m reported including £0.09m of imputed interest on deferred consideration (£0.37m due within a year, £0.27m after). Forecast: £0.9m FY27, £0.7m FY28, £0.5m FY29, £0.3m FY30, £0.2m FY31, the floor set by lease interest and fees. Consensus implies about £1.0m in FY27 and FY28 (pre-tax profit £8.8m and £9.7m against EBIT £9.8m and £10.7m); mine is slightly lower because net debt falls faster from FY28.

Tax: adjusted effective rate 24.1% in FY26 (management's "underlying" 26%; reported 30.1%; the UK rate 25%; United States entities recognised previously unrecognised losses, which reduced the current charge). Forecast 25% on adjusted profit, with cash tax modestly below the charge on accelerated capital allowances during the FY27 to FY28 capital-spending step. Risks: Section 899-type United States proposals (declined by management), Pillar Two is irrelevant at this size. Deferred tax is sensitive to the share price through share-based payment deductions (£0.21m in FY26); immaterial.

Share count: 57.1m issued, 282,008 in treasury, 56.8m voting rights; FactSet diluted 57.6m; outstanding options 1.27m; 158,500 exercised in September 2026; the May 2026 grant of up to 730,000 options vests between FY28 EPS of 11.5p and 16.0p; a 10% dilution cap applies; treasury purchases of £0.2m in FY26 for employee schemes. Forecast diluted count 57.8m FY27, 58.1m FY28, 58.3m FY29, 58.5m FY30, 58.7m FY31, which costs about 0.1p of EPS a year. No buyback is modelled (P-02 sets out the unexercised option).

Dividend: 2.75p for FY26 (raised 10%, covered 4.0x); consensus 2.9p, 3.3p, 4.0p. Forecast 3.0p, 3.4p, 3.9p, 4.4p, 4.9p, a payout of 28% in FY27 falling to about 22% as earnings grow faster than the dividend, consistent with the FY24 payout of 21.5% at the last earnings peak and with a board that cut the dividend in FY25 rather than pay it from a weak year. Cash cost £1.7m rising to £2.9m.

Minority interests, associates, pensions: none.

EPS: adjusted and reported. Adjusted diluted EPS in the base case: 10.8p FY27, 14.0p FY28, 17.7p FY29, 19.8p FY30, 21.8p FY31 (a compound rate of about 15% from FY26's 11.0p, after a 2% fall in FY27). The bridge from operating profit to EPS adds about one point a year of growth from falling interest and subtracts about half a point from dilution. Reported EPS: adjusted pre-tax profit less about £2.6m of adjustments in FY27 (£1.7m amortisation, £0.5m reorganisation, £0.4m share-based payments) falling to about £2.1m by FY31, taxed at a higher effective rate because the amortisation is largely non-deductible: about 7.2p FY27, 10.7p FY28, 14.6p FY29, 16.9p FY30, 18.8p FY31, against 7.2p reported basic in FY26 (FactSet). The gap between the two bases is 3.6p in FY27 narrowing to 3.0p by FY31; the Consumer should quote the adjusted figure, which is the brokers' basis, and note the gap when the multiple is discussed.

Net income. Adjusted net income about £6.2m FY27 (consensus £6.6m), £8.1m FY28 (consensus £7.5m), £10.3m FY29 (one broker £9.1m), £11.6m FY30, £12.8m FY31. Reported net income about £4.2m FY27 rising to about £11m by FY31, against £4.1m in FY26 and £0.5m in FY25.

Free cash flow, and where the last four to six years mislead. On FactSet's definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure, before intangibles and lease payments), free cash flow was £2.3m, £4.8m, £5.8m, £2.5m and £6.4m for FY22 to FY26, an average of £4.4m and 66% to 494% of reported net income; consensus has £5.7m, £6.5m and £9.7m for FY27 to FY29 on the same definition. On the strict definition I use in the cross-check (adjusted EBITDA less the working-capital movement, cash tax, cash interest, lease payments, all capital spending including intangibles, and reorganisation costs), free cash flow is about £2.4m FY27, £5.9m FY28, £8.0m FY29, £9.1m FY30 and £10.3m FY31, which is 40%, 73%, 78%, 78% and 80% of adjusted net income. The gap to FactSet's definition is about £1.8m of lease payments and £1.0m of intangible spending a year. Three features of the path matter. First, FY27 is the trough, because capital spending steps up to about £3.8m (the company's "over £7.5m across FY27 and FY28" against consensus £2.8m), strategic memory stock adds about £2m to inventory, and profit is flat. Second, conversion stays below 100% through FY31 because growth of 6% to 8% a year absorbs £1m to £2m of working capital each year at 20% of revenue; the first set's steer of a FY28 "release" is right as a percentage (21.5% to 20.5%) and wrong in cash. Third, from FY29 free cash flow exceeds dividends by £5m to £7m a year, which is the cash that accumulates to the lazy balance sheet of P-02. In the deferral case, free cash flow would actually rise in the year of the deferral (working capital unwinds, as FY25's 173% conversion showed) and fall the year after.

Versus the last four to six years, in one view. FY22 to FY26: adjusted EPS fell at about 6% a year compound (14.1p to 11.0p) while revenue rose 16% a year, because the margin halved and £33m of acquisitions were funded with £26.8m of equity (FY23) and debt; interest rose from £0.2m to £1.5m; the dividend was rebased; free cash flow averaged £4.4m. FY27 to FY31: adjusted EPS rises at about 15% a year on revenue growth of about 5% to 6%, with no acquisitions, interest falling to near zero, the dividend compounding at about 12%, free cash flow doubling, and the share count flat. The below-the-line items turn from a drag of about two points a year of EPS growth into a help of about one point.

Indicators. The FY27 interim's net finance cost (about £0.5m for the half); the tax rate in the interim; any treasury purchase or option exercise announcements; the capital-expenditure outturn against the plan at the FY27 results; and whether the dividend increase in December 2026 is in the 8% to 12% range (confidence) or held (caution).

P-09

The net likely impact of the other items in the set on the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C54

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

The judgement. Taking the items above together with the external and internal forces of the first set, the net effect on the main line items over the next four years, against the last four to six, is: revenue growth slower in headline terms (about 5% to 6% a year compound to FY31 against about 15% for FY20 to FY26, roughly half of which was acquired) and more organic; gross margin higher and more stable (33.5% to 34.2% against a 29% to 33.5% range); operating margin recovering from 6.2% to about 8.6% by FY31, which still leaves it below the FY21 to FY24 average of 9.2% until the final year; adjusted EPS compounding at about 15% from a trough against a negative compound rate over FY22 to FY26; free cash flow roughly doubling; and the balance sheet moving from net debt to net cash. The shape is the point: the last six years were an acquisition-led expansion with one programme spike and one programme hole; the next four are an organic, utilisation-led recovery with a flat first year, a staircase after it, and one slip to be expected somewhere in the path. The net impact is positive for the investment case and smaller than management's ambition.

The items, each with its sign, size and timing.

Sterling and the unhedged dollar purchasing (negative to neutral; up to ±£0.8m of pre-tax profit per 10%; continuous): not in the base case; a separate driver of the same size as the memory lag.

The Autumn Budget's employer National Insurance and minimum wage changes (negative; over £0.5m a year; from April 2026): in the FY27 overhead; permanent.

Memory and semiconductor inflation and lead times (negative on timing and working capital, neutral on margin after the lag; 30 basis points of gross margin in FY27, £2m of inventory; one to two quarters): in the base case for FY27, fading in FY28 as capacity added by Schneider, Eaton and the memory makers arrives.

The industrial restock (positive; worth about 6% Components growth in FY27 and 5% in FY28, plateauing by the fourth quarter of 2026 as the sector digest expects; then mid-single digits): in the base case. The risk is a weak December print from discoverIE, TT Electronics or XP Power being read across.

AI data-centre and edge-AI demand (positive; small; Durakool multi-million dollar orders and the DeepX franchise; growing from FY28): inside Components' growth rate; the digest's single-hyperscaler-pause risk applies.

The Defence Investment Plan and the CAIN and NSPA follow-ons (the decisive item; positive in level, negative in timing; worth £10m to £20m of revenue in any given year; dated by government): the base case probability-weights a follow-on into H2 FY27 and assumes the plan is published and converting by FY28.

United States expansion costs and tariffs (negative in FY27 to FY28, positive from FY29; the Fountain Valley antenna production investment is in the over £7.5m of capital spending; tariffs passed through at 10 basis points of gross margin with a lag): in the base case at a lower Power and antenna margin until FY29.

The 17% customer and the Ministry of Defence as gatekeeper (negative for predictability, neutral for level): the reason the revenue cone is wide and the pricing on follow-ons is held flat.

The Capital Markets Day in November 2026 (positive for disclosure, unknown for numbers): not in the model; if it restates a dated margin bridge or a capital-allocation framework, the FY29 to FY31 figures move up; if it reframes 2030 around revenue without a margin, the sell side's FY29 figure is at risk.

The widening broker base (Zeus as joint broker from October 2026, Berenberg from December 2025): no effect on the model, a modest effect on how quickly consensus moves after each event.

Peer takeover activity in accredited defence electronics (discoverIE's Trival and 3Gmetalworx, Diploma's CDM, Gooch & Housego's recommended cash acquisition in July 2026, the collapsed Cicor bid for TT): no effect on the model; a floor under the multiple.

The net by line item, versus the last four to six years. Revenue: £153m FY27 (flat), £165.5m FY28, £178.5m FY29, £189m FY30, £199.5m FY31; history £67m FY20 to £154m FY26. Gross margin: 33.6% rising to 34.2%; history 27.4% to 33.5% (company basis 30.2% FY21 to 33.5% FY26). Adjusted overhead: £42.2m rising to £51.0m at 4% to 5% a year; history £15.6m FY21 to £42.0m FY26. Adjusted operating profit: £9.2m, £11.5m, £14.3m, £15.7m, £17.2m; margin 6.0%, 7.0%, 8.0%, 8.3%, 8.6%; history £17.6m (10.4%) FY24, £6.0m (4.8%) FY25, £9.6m (6.2%) FY26. Adjusted EBITDA: £13.8m (9.0%), £16.5m (10.0%), £19.6m (11.0%), £21.2m (11.2%), £22.9m (11.5%); history £20.1m FY24, £10.3m FY25, £13.9m FY26 on the brokers' basis. Adjusted EPS: 10.8p, 14.0p, 17.7p, 19.8p, 21.8p; history 14.1p, about 13p, 20.0p, 6.2p, 11.0p. Free cash flow (strict): £2.4m, £5.9m, £8.0m, £9.1m, £10.3m. Net cash (debt) excluding leases: £(3.7)m, about nil, £5.6m, £12.1m, £19.4m; history £(3.1)m FY24, £(7.4)m FY25, £(4.2)m FY26.

Where the net is most and least certain. Most certain: gross margin (a one-point band), below-the-line items (P-08), the share count, the dividend, and the direction of overhead. Least certain: the FY27 second half, the FY28 revenue level, and therefore the FY27 and FY28 operating margins; the cone on each is set out in P-01 and P-10. Over four years the uncertainty narrows in percentage terms as the recurring layer grows, but each year's absolute cone stays about £15m of revenue and 2 to 3 points of margin wide because the programme block stays lumpy.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years

C54

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

The base case in one paragraph. A flat FY27 (revenue £153m, down 0.7%, as the £23.3m programme washes out and is replaced by CAIN's initial order, Power's order book and the components restock; operating margin 6.0%; EPS 10.8p, down 2%), followed by a staircase: FY28 £165.5m (+8%), 7.0%, 14.0p; FY29 £178.5m (+8%), 8.0%, 17.7p; FY30 £189m (+6%), 8.3%, 19.8p; FY31 £199.5m (+6%), 8.6%, 21.8p. Management's 2030 ambition of £250m and 20p is reached on EPS a year late and not reached on revenue; the divisional margin targets are reached at the top of the range in Systems (by FY29), at the bottom in Power (by FY30) and not in Components. The probability I attach to the base path is about 55%, with 25% on an award-led path that is one year ahead of it and 20% on a deferral path that is one year behind it and starts with an FY25-type year.

The operational drivers behind the base case, by division.

Systems (FY26 revenue £62.5m including £23.3m of programme; recurring about £39m; operating margin 14.3%, gross margin 38.7%): FY27 £54m, FY28 £59m, FY29 £65m, FY30 £70m, FY31 £74.5m. Built as a recurring base (ruggedised computing, Active Silicon imaging, Q-Par antennas, the DSTL cabinet work, through-life support) growing 4% to 6% a year from about £39m, plus a programme block: CAIN's £8.0m initial order in FY27, a probability-weighted follow-on tranche of about £5m in H2 FY27 (55% of about £9m), and £15m to £20m a year of CAIN follow-ons, NSPA framework follow-ons from other NATO members and Tier 2 sub-system work from FY28, rising as the Defence Investment Plan converts. Operating margin 13.2% FY27 (CAIN at programme economics, Ashchurch covering its standing costs), 14% FY28, 15% FY29, 15% FY30, 15.3% FY31 as Ashchurch loads toward its £15m headroom. The driver to watch is Systems revenue per half against £20m to £22m of recurring run-rate; anything above £30m in a half means a tranche shipped.

Power (FY26 £31.8m, +15.6%; operating margin 5.3%, gross margin 35.8%): FY27 £36.5m (+15%), FY28 £40.5m (+11%), FY29 £45m (+11%), FY30 £49m (+9%), FY31 £52.5m (+7%). Driven by the over $20m of autonomy orders taken in the last four months of FY26 and $7.4m of Tier 1 wins, the United States vertical sales structure under the new Chief Sales Officer, "Made in USA" procurement preference, and the Volklec sovereign offer from FY28. Operating margin 7% FY27, 8% FY28, 9.5% FY29, 10% FY30, 10.5% FY31: the bottom of the 10% to 14% target reached by FY30 on the automated test rooms and cell-sorting capacity. The driver to watch is United States order intake and the margin at the interim against 5.3%.

Components (FY26 £59.8m, +8.1%; operating margin 4.0%, gross margin 26.8%): FY27 £62.5m (+4.5%, of which about one point is pass-through inflation), FY28 £66m (+5.5%), FY29 £68.5m (+4%), FY30 £70m (+2%), FY31 £72.5m (+3.5%). Driven by the restock pulse through calendar 2026, AI data-centre contactor orders, internet-of-things modules and own-brand lines; held back by disintermediation and Asian price competition in standard parts. Operating margin 4.5% FY27, 5% FY28, 5.5% FY29, 5.8% FY30, 6% FY31: below the 7% to 9% target throughout, on the first set's judgement (C-02) that the structure does not support the target early. The driver to watch is book-to-bill against 1.0 and the gross margin against 26.8%.

Central costs (head office, before acquired-intangible amortisation): about £3.3m FY27 rising to £3.9m FY31.
