Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 14,120 words · written 23 October 2026, 13:49 to 14:05 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Solid State plc (SOLI-GB): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and what guidance, the sell side and the market already reflect

Written 2026-10-23 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst who writes the investment memo. Fiscal years end 31 March: FY27 is the year now running; H1 FY27 (to 30 September 2026) reports on 15 December 2026, after a trading update expected in early November and the first Capital Markets Day on 25 November 2026. Money is sterling. Profit figures are on the company's adjusted basis, which is the brokers' basis and the basis of FactSet consensus (adjusted operating profit before amortisation of acquired intangibles, share-based payments and reorganisation costs). "Consensus" is the FactSet median at 22 October 2026 (one visible broker, Cavendish, 8 September 2026, for FY27 and FY28; one for FY29); Zeus Capital's 29 June 2026 figures are quoted where they differ. "The base case" means the second set's forecast inputs as the Financial Analyst's model (update 1) calculated them: revenue £153.1m, £165.6m, £178.7m for FY27 to FY29; adjusted EPS 10.8p, 14.0p, 17.7p; against consensus 11.6p, 13.1p, 16.0p. The share price is 162.5p (22 October 2026; the model used 171p), market capitalisation about £98.5m.

What the reader should carry through this set

Three findings organise the answers below. First, the earlier sets' analysis is congruent on the one mechanism (fixed-cost operating leverage on a £42m overhead) and the one dependency (the date of the next defence tranche), but one fact they both carry is wrong: the UK Defence Investment Plan was published on 30 June 2026, so the document the first set was waiting for has already arrived and the share has fallen 18% since; the catalyst is now a contract, not a plan (D-01). Second, the base case's growth is more diffuse than the narrative suggests (Power and Components supply two-thirds of the FY26 to FY29 profit increase) while the variance is concentrated almost entirely in one line, Systems programme revenue; so the level of the forecast rests on three divisions and the predictability of the forecast rests on one (D-03). Third, the share price is 99% idiosyncratic over the last six months and the multiple sits at the first percentile of its own five-year range while every peer's rating has risen (D-07), which means the forecasts translate straight into the price with no sector noise in between: the case is as predictable as the next tranche and no more.

I change none of the second set's forecast inputs. The model summary's apparent gaps (EPS growth of +45.3% in FY27, free cash flow £0.5m a year above the Analyst's, net debt £3m to £5m worse than the Analyst's) are all basis effects and are explained in D-01; none shows an input to be wrong.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

❌ D477 questions
D-0164D-0255D-0342D-0438D-0550D-0652D-0760

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The predictability of Solid State's forecasts over the next 18 months is low period by period and moderate in total, and the reason is the same at every question in this group. The earlier sets are congruent on a case with one moving part, and the one fact they got wrong (the Defence Investment Plan is published) replaces a document with a contract as the next catalyst without changing the inputs. The 18-month numbers rest on strengths the company has shown (orders held, pass-through discipline, a restructured Power business, an honest expectation-setting habit) and on a customer's date that no strength can bring forward; the four-year numbers rest half on capabilities not yet shown. The growth in the base case is diffuse across three divisions, which defends its level, while the variance sits in one line, Systems programme revenue, which sets its cone; the cone is about ±35% on FY27 EPS, skewed down in FY27 and up in FY28, and the share-price cone is bimodal with a favourable expectation and a one-in-five chance of a 40% fall. Disclosure is adequate to monitor outcomes at four dated points and inadequate to monitor the mechanism until the Capital Markets Day supplies a utilisation figure and a bridge; the transmission from words to EPS is traceable at every link but two, the Ministry's date and the partner's margin; and the share price is 99% its own, so whatever the forecasts turn out to be, the price will follow them without sector help or hindrance. For the memo's topic #9, the case is simple (one driver), fragile (a £20m cushion of recurring revenue above break-even, one customer, one partner, no hedge), sudden in its risks (a review can remove 60% of a year in a month) and trackable only after the fact; for topic #10, the stock is at the bottoming-to-turning-up boundary, where the multiple has already priced the fear and the earnings have not yet proved the turn. The Consumer should present the forecasts as a probability-weighted staircase and not as a path, should put the H1 FY27 optical decline in the memo in advance, and should treat the first contract let under the plan, not the plan, as the event that moves the case from "turning up" to "reinforcing".

D-01

How congruent are the answers to the earlier sets?

C64

Question asked How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

The judgement. Highly congruent on the structure of the case and on the direction of every main line, with one factual error shared by both sets that changes the shape of the catalyst staircase, one behavioural assumption about the sell side that the record contradicts, three basis traps that the memo must not fall into, and a set of model-summary gaps that are explained by basis rather than by error. Where the second set refined the first (a lower net drop-through, a timing haircut on FY27, no working-capital release in FY28), the second set's refinement rests on measured record and should be preferred. Nothing in the two sets contradicts itself on the question that matters, which is that the operating margin is set by the utilisation of a largely fixed cost base against programme-shaped revenue the company does not time.

Where the sets align, in one paragraph. Both sets model the business from programme timing and utilisation rather than from a growth rate and a margin; both put the recurring FY26 base at about £131m and the FY27 reported figure flat to down; both hold gross margin in a 33% to 34% band as the reliable line and operating margin as the output; both treat financial leverage as immaterial and improving; both expect the next two reporting periods to show an optical decline that the external forces neither cause nor cure; both carry the FY25 deferral as the downside template (EPS 6p to 8p) and the sovereign-platform re-rating as the upside archetype; both regard the sell side as thin, aligned with management by construction and at a post-upgrade level with zero revision momentum; and both find the multiple at a trough with estimates unrebased. The AlphaSense reports written for this set corroborate every one of these readings from primary sources (the Zeus notes of 15 November 2024, 11 March 2025, 29 June 2026 and 13 October 2026; the FY26 annual report of 6 July 2026; the Companies House accounts of 31 July 2026).

Where they jar, in order of importance.

(1) The Defence Investment Plan has been published. The first set (C-01, C-04, C-10, C-22, C-37) and the second set (P-10) both describe the plan as "still unpublished as of October 2026" and make its publication one of the two dated events on which the next six months turn. The evidence assembled for this set says otherwise: the plan was published on 30 June 2026 (GOV.UK, 29 June 2026, "The Defence Investment Plan"; Flight Global, 30 June 2026, "Lucky DIP"; Morgan Stanley, 30 June 2026, "DIP Release Brings Relief"; HM Treasury minutes, 28 August 2026; Gowling WLG, 11 August 2026). It commits £298bn over four years, adds £15bn, targets 2.7% of GDP by 2029, names Projects CAIN and EVE together as leading-edge tactical radio networks, and leaves a £28bn gap between the Ministry of Defence's requirement and the Treasury's envelope (Jefferies, 17 May 2026; Deutsche Bank, 1 July 2026). The company's results statement of 29 June 2026, written the day before publication, is the source of the "delays to publication" language that both sets repeated. One report in this set (the H1 FY27 preview) still calls the release date unspecified, so the memo should state the fact with its source; the weight of evidence is that it is published. Three consequences follow. First, the "clearing event" has cleared nothing visible: the share has fallen from about 199p at the June results to 162.5p since the plan appeared, with estimates unchanged, so the market has read the document and found no contract in it. Second, the risk has migrated from "will the plan be published" to "will the Treasury fund the plan's conventional lines on time": press reports of 6 October 2026 say the government is considering delaying the spending review to autumn 2027 and slowing the roadmap to 3% of GDP, the Competition and Markets Authority announced in September 2026 that it will screen Ministry of Defence procurement data for collusion (half of the Ministry's FY25 contracts were directly awarded), and the United States is on a continuing resolution to 11 December 2026 that freezes new programme starts, which bears on Custom Power's United States order conversion. Third, the lag is now measurable: Jefferies (17 May 2026) puts three to six months between publication and revenue reaching suppliers, which points to contracting in October to December 2026 and shipments in the fourth quarter of FY27, exactly where the second set probability-weighted its £5m tranche; and the Segmented Acquisition Model introduced in April 2026 sets a one-year lane for tactical communications upgrades, which is a structural support for conversion that neither earlier set had. The positive (a published plan naming CAIN, a faster procurement lane, a small-business spend target of an extra £2.5bn by 2028) and the negative (a £28bn gap, a possible spending-review delay, a United States stopgap) offset, so I leave the FY27 inputs where the second set put them; but the catalyst list in the first set's C-22 should be rewritten with the plan removed and "the first contract let under it" put in its place.

(2) How the sell side behaves in a deferral. The first set (C-74) and the second set (P-06, P-10) reason that revenue which slips out of FY27 "lands in FY28", which is right for the business and wrong for the published numbers. In November 2024 Zeus removed about £20m from both FY25 and FY26 and cut EPS by 60% and 46% in one note; it did not move the revenue to the following year, and it re-added the revenue only when the order was reinstated in March 2025 (+20% to FY26 EPS). The fundamentals shift forward; the estimates fall in both years and are rebuilt on signature. This changes nothing in the model and a great deal in I-02 and in the share-price path of the deferral case: the first set's deferral trough of 85p to 105p is consistent with estimates being cut across two years, not one.

(3) The identity and meaning of the 17% customer. The first set reads the £26.7m customer as the government behind the NATO Support and Procurement Agency programme, which Zeus (29 June 2026) supports; the cost-recovery report in this set ties the same customer to Project CAIN and the Ministry of Defence. The sources conflict and the memo should not resolve them. More useful is the fact that FY24 also had a 20% customer (£33.4m; FY24 annual report), so concentration above 15% is the recurring signature of every programme-delivery year rather than a new fragility; the first set's "sharpest edge" stands, with the qualification that the edge appears whenever the thesis is working.

(4) Three basis traps. (a) The price-earnings multiple is quoted on at least four bases: Zeus's "about 15x forward" and "17.2x FY27" were struck at about 199p; FactSet's 24-month-forward multiple is 11.07x at the first percentile of five years; the dashboard's current multiple is 11.5x; the model's two-year-forward multiple is 12.0x at 171p; at 162.5p the share is on 14.0x FY27, 12.4x FY28 and 10.2x FY29 (one broker). All are consistent once dated; the memo should use the dashboard and FactSet readings and name them. (b) Zeus's "administration costs" of £38.1m for FY27 are not the second set's adjusted overhead of £42.2m; Zeus's line excludes distribution costs, and both models produce the same operating margin (6.3% and 6.0%). The reader should not conclude that Zeus models a leaner cost base. (c) The model summary's "EPS growth +45.3%" for FY27 compares a forecast on the adjusted basis (10.8p) with an FY26 base on FactSet's reported or Viewforth basis (about 7.5p to 8p); the like-for-like FY27 change is -2% on 11.0p, and the memo must not quote the model's growth row for FY27.

(5) The model summary's cross-check gaps, each explained. EPS: the Analyst's and the model's figures agree in every year, so the profit-and-loss inputs are reproduced exactly. Free cash flow: the model is £0.5m above the Analyst in every year (2.9 against 2.4, 6.4 against 5.9, and so on); the whole gap is the £0.5m a year of recurring reorganisation cost that the Analyst deducts in his strict definition and the model's adjusted EBITDA, by construction, excludes. Net cash: the model's year-end figures (-8.5, -4.2, +1.8, +8.7, +16.4) sit £4.8m, £4.2m, £3.8m, £3.4m and £3.0m below the Analyst's (-3.7, nil, +5.6, +12.1, +19.4); the model starts from FactSet's lease-inclusive March 2026 net debt of £9.5m (the Analyst's £4.2m excludes £5.9m of leases, and FactSet's lease figure differs by about £0.6m), and the gap then narrows by exactly the £0.5m a year of reorganisation cost. The model's lease-inclusive path agrees with Zeus's lease-inclusive path (£8.0m at March 2027, £5.7m at March 2028) to within £1.5m. None of this shows an input to be wrong, so I change no input; the memo should quote net debt on one basis, named, and should read the model's free cash flow as before about £0.5m a year of reorganisation cost that has recurred for three years.

(6) The second set's own two probability sets (P-06: 55% mode, 30% partial slip, 15% deferral for FY27 EPS; P-10: 55% base, 25% award-led, 20% deferral for paths) partition different things and are not contradictory, but the memo should use one, and P-10's path probabilities are the ones the model's cases correspond to.

(7) Minor unreconciled items the memo should carry as unreconciled: Berenberg's target (150p in one source, 240p in another); the chief executive's shareholding (546,207 or 150,053); the facility (£15m plus £10m accordion, or £30m).

Why congruence matters here. The earlier sets converge because the business has one moving part that matters, and the case's predictability is therefore the predictability of that part. The correction on the Defence Investment Plan slightly improves the foundation (the policy document exists and names the programme) and slightly worsens the timing read (the money behind it is contested), which is why the net effect on the inputs is nil and the effect on the catalyst staircase is large.

D-02

To what extent does past and present delivery make the expected change plausible, and how far does the thesis rely on strengths the business demonstrably has?

C55

Question asked To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

The judgement. For the 18-month forecast, about four-fifths of the expected change rests on strengths the business has demonstrated and on orders it already holds; the remaining fifth is a customer's calendar, which is not a strength the business could acquire. For the four-year path the balance shifts: roughly half of the margin recovery from 6.0% to 8.6% rests on demonstrated strengths (accredited incumbency, pass-through discipline, Power's restructured United States operation, own-brand mix) and half on capabilities that are asserted but not yet shown (Tier 2 sub-system delivery at scale from Ashchurch, pricing of a through-life support tail, United States antenna manufacturing, an engineering bench that has stayed at about 80 people while administrative headcount rose 71%). The precedents say that fixed-cost electronics makers loading new capacity with government serial volume do reach the margin (Astra Microwave, XP Power's Vietnam site, TT Electronics' Cleveland after its failure) but take longer than planned and slip at least once; Solid State's own record (beats of 3% to 9% in four of five years, one 60% miss when the customer's calendar moved) is that pattern exactly.

The 18-month change, tested item by item against the record.

(a) Replacing the £23.3m programme with CAIN's £8.0m initial order, Power's order intake and the restock: demonstrated. The CAIN order shipped in the quarter to June 2026 against memory constraints; the order book of £102.4m at 31 May 2026 was £18m higher underlying and most of it ships within 18 months, so about two-thirds of the base case's FY27 revenue was in hand before the year began; Custom Power's United States operation "outperformed previous recovery models" (annual report, 6 July 2026) with its open order book up 13%; Components grew 8.1% in FY26 after a 14.6% fall in H1 FY25. The record of converting an order book of this cover into revenue is good in every year except the one in which a government paused.

(b) Passing memory inflation through at a lag: demonstrated, and better than at peers. Through the 2021 to 2023 shortage, with lead times at 50 weeks, the then finance director said in December 2023 that component shortages had "practically zero impact" on gross margins and some margins rose, because customers were engaged from 2020 and supplier increases were passed on immediately; XP Power's gross margin fell 360 basis points in 2022, TT Electronics' Power and Connectivity margin fell from 8.2% to 5.6% on recovery lags, and Mercury Systems lost 640 basis points on fixed-price development contracts. The 2026 squeeze is more severe (DRAM contract prices rose about 60% in the second quarter of 2026 and 13% to 18% in the third; DDR4, the grade qualified in older defence configurations, is tightest), so the demonstrated strength is tested harder, and the company's own statement that some programmes could become "temporarily unviable" means the cost of the strength is timing rather than margin, as the second set modelled.

(c) The H2 FY27 tranche: not a strength, a dependency. The demonstrated strength behind it is incumbency: over 2,000 MPU5 radios fielded with the Royal Marines by October 2025 under Project EVE, the CAIN award for 16 Air Assault Brigade (the Army's largest brigade, over 6,000 personnel, held at very high readiness) following directly from it, and the plan of 30 June 2026 naming both. The precedents for follow-on timing on UK land communications are poor (General Dynamics expected a Bowman 5.7 contract "within six months" of September 2025; Morpheus has been extended repeatedly) and the one favourable structural change, the Segmented Acquisition Model's one-year lane, is untested since April 2026. The thesis does not need the business to acquire anything here; it needs the customer to act.

(d) Ashchurch covering its standing costs in FY27: partly demonstrated. Management said in December 2025 that the site would cover its basic operational costs in the current financial year; the DSTL cabinet contract (£1.65m to £1.7m) is the only named award from it; the £15m of excess capacity and the £3m of overhead are disclosed (interim presentation, December 2025; FY25 results). Covering costs is plausible on the CAIN and DSTL work; loading beyond that is the FY28 question.

(e) Holding overhead growth to 4% to 5%: not demonstrated under the new chief executive. FY26 added £8.7m of overhead and reinvested about 70% of the gross-profit gain; the record before that (FY21 to FY24, overhead £15.6m to £38.1m, partly acquired) is of a cost base that grows with ambition. The second set's assumption is reasonable because the FY26 step was tied to one-off events (succession, Ashchurch, the Power separation), but the first evidence will be the FY27 overhead against £42.2m, and this is the single largest assumption in the 18-month forecast that the record does not yet support.

(f) Power from 5.3% to 7.0%: demonstrated in direction. The division went from 1.0% to 5.3% in a year by exiting low-value work, and the restructured United States site is ahead of plan; Ultralife's 2025 operating loss shows the segment punishes poor manufacturing control, and the step from 7% to 10% needs volume at Fountain Valley that has not yet been produced.

The four-year change, and which strengths it needs. The base case takes Systems to 15% on £65m (FY29), Power to 10% on £49m (FY30) and Components to 6% on £70m (FY30). Systems at 15% needs £15m to £20m a year of programme revenue from FY28 and the pricing of training, support and spiral upgrades; the support tail is inferred (the feedback-loop work puts it at 15% to 20% of hardware value over five years; Persistent Systems' WaRTAK precedent, $14.6m in 2020 then a $7m upgrade in 2026, supports the shape), but Steatite's share of it, and whether Persistent's channel terms let it keep a software or support margin, are undisclosed; a former Silvus Technologies chief operating officer (June 2026) put tactical radio margins as "incredibly high" and integration services at 30% to 40% gross, which is consistent with Systems' 38.7% gross margin and says that Steatite earns the integrator's margin, not the radio maker's. Power at 10% needs United States execution at a site that produced a £2.7m impairment three years ago; the recovery since is the demonstrated part, the scale-up is not. Components at 6% rests on own-brand mix, where the record cuts both ways: own-brand sales were strong in FY26 and the divisional margin still fell ten basis points, which the annual report attributes to price-sensitive distribution volume diluting the gain. The engineering bench is the capability the four-year path needs most and shows least: technical specialists flat at about 80 while management and administrative heads rose from 91 to 156.

The precedents, and what distinguished success. Astra Microwave moved from sub-systems to build-to-specification radar and lifted gross margin from 30% (FY22) to 51% (FY26) by loading a dedicated unit with serial defence production after a long qualification phase; XP Power's Vietnam site diluted margins for two years, then sequential shipments rose 70% in late 2026 as orders arrived; TT Electronics' Cleveland site failed under complexity in 2024 and 2025 (a £35m to £41m North American write-down, a chief executive departure) and returned to profit in H1 2026 after process control and headcount cuts; Volex consolidated sites and pulled $10m of customer deliveries forward to protect supply; discoverIE raised its margin from 3.4% to 13.8% over twelve years by mix and acquisition, not by loading a facility. The common thread of the successes is serial production of a qualified product for a programme customer, which is what CAIN becomes if it progresses from one brigade to several; the common thread of the failures is complexity added faster than process control. Solid State's Ashchurch is small by these standards (£15m of capacity on a £154m group), which limits both the upside and the damage.

What the company's own markers say. The long-term incentive plan granted in May 2026 vests between FY28 EPS of 11.5p and 16.0p; the threshold is 0.5p above FY26, and the quiet withdrawal of the 12% group margin target from the annual report in favour of "margins broadly stable" says management itself does not regard the two-year path as reliable, while the 2030 ambition (20p, £250m) says it regards the direction as settled. The historical average margin of 9.2% (FY21 to FY24) was earned on £38m of overhead; on today's £42m plus 4% to 5% a year, the same margin needs about £180m to £200m of revenue, which is why "returning to normalised execution" (Zeus's framing) is necessary but not sufficient.

Judgement in one line for the memo. The 18-month numbers are a delivery forecast on orders held, by a management that delivers; the four-year numbers are a loading forecast on orders not yet placed, by a management that has not yet loaded a facility of this kind; the probability that the loading slips at least once in four years is high, and the second set's 30% to 40% chance that the slip is FY27 is the right order.

D-03

How diffuse or concentrated are the drivers that generate the differences between the forecasts and the last four to six years?

❌ D42

Question asked In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

The judgement. The drivers of the level of the forecast are more diffuse than the narrative implies, and the drivers of the variance around it are more concentrated than the narrative implies; the memo should hold both. Decomposed by division, two-thirds of the base case's increase in operating profit from FY26 to FY29 comes from Power and Components, not from Systems; but the whole of the cone around any single year comes from one line, Systems programme revenue, because the £15m to £20m a year of follow-on tranches that the base case assumes from FY28 is worth more than the entire group profit increase if it does not arrive. Against the last four to six years the drivers have changed in kind: FY20 to FY26 revenue growth of about 15% a year was half acquired (£33m of acquisitions, £26.8m of equity in FY23) and the earnings path was set by one programme swinging revenue by £20m to £38m a year; FY27 to FY31 growth of 5% to 6% a year is organic and its earnings path is set by utilisation of a cost base already built. The concentration has moved from the balance sheet (acquisitions) to the order book (one customer's tranches), and the second is less controllable than the first.

The decomposition, which the earlier sets did not do. Group adjusted operating profit of £9.6m in FY26 was Systems £8.9m (62.5 at 14.3%), Power £1.7m (31.8 at 5.3%), Components £2.4m (59.8 at 4.0%), central costs £(3.4)m. The base case's FY29 figure of £14.3m is Systems £9.75m (65.0 at 15.0%), Power £4.3m (45.0 at 9.5%), Components £3.8m (68.5 at 5.5%), central £(3.5)m. The £4.7m increase is therefore Power +£2.6m (55%), Components +£1.4m (30%), Systems +£0.8m (17%), central -£0.1m. Systems contributes little to the increase because FY26 already contained £23.3m of programme revenue at a 14.3% divisional margin; the base case has Systems replacing that programme with follow-ons and loading Ashchurch, which lifts the margin only from 14.3% to 15.0%. The FY27 step down (£9.6m to £9.2m) is the mirror image: Systems falls £1.8m (54.0 at 13.2%) while Power adds £0.9m and Components £0.4m. The FY28 step up (£9.2m to £11.5m) is about half Systems (+£1.2m), with Power +£0.7m and Components +£0.5m.

What that means for predictability. The three divisions' drivers are different clocks (the first set's C-01) and they are not correlated in level: the Power autonomy orders ($20m in four months, $7.4m of Tier 1 wins) and the industrial restock (discoverIE orders +22% in H1 FY27, XP Power book-to-bill 1.67) do not depend on the Ministry of Defence. So the base case's growth is supported by three independent sources, which is more diffuse than "a lumpy defence supplier" suggests and is the strongest argument for the level of the FY28 and FY29 numbers. But the variance is not diffuse. If no tranche ships in FY28 and FY29, Systems revenue is about £45m to £50m rather than £59m to £65m, the Ashchurch overhead of about £3m stays unabsorbed and the divisional margin falls toward 10% to 11%; Systems profit is then about £5m against £9.75m, a swing of nearly £5m, which equals the whole of the group's planned increase. No plausible miss in Power (a £5m revenue shortfall costs about £0.5m) or Components (a lost restock year costs about £0.3m to £0.5m) is of that order. The second-order drivers (memory inflation at about 0.5p of EPS, sterling at about 0.4p per 4% move, the overhead growth assumption at about 1p for each two points of growth) are each worth a tenth of the programme swing.

Measured against the company's own history. The FY24 to FY25 fall (revenue -£38.2m, operating profit -£11.0m) and the FY25 to FY26 recovery (+£29.1m, +£3.6m) were both the same single driver, a £20m to £24m communications programme moving across a year-end; Components' 14.6% fall in H1 FY25 and 8.1% recovery in FY26 and Power's 1.0% to 5.3% margin step were real but second-order. The forecast period repeats the structure at smaller amplitude: the programme block is £13m in FY27 and £20m to £30m a year from FY28 against a recurring base of £131m growing 5% to 6%, so programme revenue is about 10% to 15% of the total, down from 15% in FY26 and about 20% in FY24; the amplitude of the swing therefore falls over time, but it remains the only driver large enough to move EPS by a third.

Peers, for the comparison the question asks. discoverIE's dispersion through 2024 to 2026 was narrow (margin +1.2 points in a year of 7% organic decline) because its drivers are thousands of design-ins across 30 acquired businesses; Cohort's are concentrated (the Ministry of Defence was 48% of revenue and 58% of order intake in 2024, falling to 40% of revenue after the €140.7m Polish sonar award); Chemring's Roke sensors business sat at 54% revenue cover against 98% in countermeasures; Gooch & Housego carries 59% of EBIT in the second half; Mercury Systems' margin fell 1,000 basis points on a 40:60 development-to-production mix; TT Electronics' dispersion came from two North American sites. Solid State's concentration is of the Cohort and Chemring kind (one sovereign customer's cadence) layered on the Gooch & Housego kind (second-half weighting), with a smaller cushion than any of them because its fixed base is 24% of revenue against a break-even of about £110m to £115m: there is about £20m of recurring revenue between the current run-rate and the point at which operating profit disappears, which is the size of one tranche.

The steer for the memo. Describe the forecast as three-engine growth with a single-engine risk. The level of FY28 and FY29 can be defended on Power and Components alone; the cone around any year must be drawn from Systems alone; and the test of the case is not whether the three engines run but whether the one that sets the variance fires on time. For topic #9 of the memo, this is the simplicity and the fragility in one sentence.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

❌ D38

The judgement. The earnings cone is wide, about ±35% around the FY27 base and ±25% around FY28, and it is skewed downward on FY27 earnings and upward on FY28 earnings; the share-price cone over the same 18 months is wider still in the tails (about -45% to +50%) but skewed upward in expectation, because the multiple sits at the first percentile of its own history and has less room to fall than earnings have to fall. Predictability over 18 months is therefore low at the level of any single reporting period and moderate at the level of the two-year total, because what one period loses the next period usually recovers (FY25 and FY26 are the proof). The practical reading: the probability of a surprise at one of the next three events is high; the probability that the two-year outcome lands outside 11p to 17p of FY28 EPS is lower than the single-period volatility suggests.

The earnings cone, in numbers the memo can use. FY27 adjusted EPS: base 10.8p; award-led 11.9p (a £9m to £12m tranche ships in H2); deferral 7.0p (no tranche, the FY25 template); a memory-driven programme deferral or a sterling move of 4% to 5% adds or removes about 0.5p around any of these. FY28: base 14.0p; award-led 17.5p; deferral 11.0p, which is, not by coincidence, the long-term incentive threshold. The second set's path probabilities (55%, 25%, 20%) give probability-weighted EPS of about 10.3p for FY27 and 14.2p for FY28; the cone is 7p to 12p for FY27 and 11p to 17.5p for FY28, with the consensus (11.6p, 13.1p) sitting at the top of the FY27 range and in the lower half of the FY28 range.

Why the FY27 skew is negative and the FY28 skew positive. FY27 is decided by its second half, and the second half contains a tranche that is probability-weighted at 55%; the upside (the tranche is larger or ships earlier) is capped by the Ministry's cadence and the Segmented Acquisition Model's one-year lane, while the downside (it slips three months into April 2027) removes £5m to £9m of revenue and £1.5m to £2.7m of operating profit from a year with no offset. FY28 then receives whatever FY27 loses, on top of its own growth, so its distribution has a fat right tail. The company's own severe-downside sensitivity (revenue 6% below base over 18 months, EBITDA -22%, covenants intact) and Zeus's sensitivities (50 basis points of margin equals 0.9p of EPS; 2% of volume equals 0.24p) bracket the arithmetic; the FY25 record (revenue -23%, operating profit -65%) and the March 2025 reinstatement (+20% to EPS in one note) are the realised tails.

What narrows the cone, and by how much. (1) The order book: £102.4m at 31 May 2026, most shipping within 18 months, covers about two-thirds of the base case's FY27 revenue, and the CAIN initial order shipped in the first quarter, so H1 FY27 is largely known to management already; the November trading update is the first datum. (2) Contract terms: non-cancellable orders, deposits (contract liabilities £6.3m) and milestone billing protect the margin on programmes already won. (3) The plan of 30 June 2026 names CAIN; the Segmented Acquisition Model gives tactical communications a one-year lane; the single-source regulations were amended in June 2026 to raise the maximum incentive adjustment from 2% to 10%, which rewards on-time delivery. (4) Power's order intake is in hand and its customers pulled orders forward in early FY26, so its FY27 revenue is the best-covered line in the group. (5) Below operating profit nothing moves: interest about £0.9m, tax 25%, 57.8m shares.

What widens it. (1) The Treasury: a £28bn gap in the plan, press reports (6 October 2026) of a possible delay to the spending review to autumn 2027 and a slower path to 3% of GDP; the November 2024 precedent shows a review can remove 60% of a year's EPS in a month. (2) Memory: DRAM contract prices up about 60% in the second quarter of 2026 and 13% to 18% in the third, DDR4 tightest, and the company's own warning that some programmes could become temporarily unviable; the cost is deferred revenue, not margin, which puts it in the revenue cone. (3) The United States continuing resolution to 11 December 2026, which freezes new programme starts and bears on Custom Power's United States order conversion. (4) Sterling, unhedged on about 40% of purchases, worth about ±0.4p of EPS for the range of broker forecasts. (5) Working capital and liquidity are not in the cone (the balance sheet carries an FY25-type year with room to spare), but the optics of the H1 FY27 print are: revenue down about 12%, operating margin about 5.6% against 7.0%, in a market that has already sold the stock on timing.

The peer evidence on 18-month dispersion. Comparable UK defence and electronics small caps show the same shape. Cohort's results depend on Ministry of Defence timing (48% of revenue in 2024) and were stabilised only by international awards; Chemring's shorter-cycle Roke business had 54% revenue cover; Gooch & Housego's five-year average second-half EBIT weighting is 59%, and a fourth-quarter slip moves the year; TT Electronics cut FY25 EBITA guidance by 16% and Jefferies cut EPS by up to 39% when two North American sites failed; XP Power's margin went from 4.3% to 7.9% in a year on utilisation; Mercury Systems' margin fell 1,000 basis points on programme mix and is now rising 2.5 times faster than revenue as programmes move to production. The cohort's range of 18-month EPS outcomes around forecasts that rested on one or two awards is roughly -40% to +25%, and the awards were late more often than early; Solid State's own FY25 and FY26 are one of the wider pairs in that set. Forecasts that rested on one award were met on time in a minority of the cases the research found.

The share-price cone, which is what Richard will feel. Deferral: EPS toward 7p, estimates cut across two years as in November 2024, the multiple applied to depressed earnings expands rather than contracts, and the share trades at 85p to 105p (-35% to -48%); the first set's C-83 and this set's co-movement work agree. Base: EPS 10.8p then 14.0p, the multiple recovers toward the guideline 12.7x on the 18-month column, and the model's value per share is 247p (+44% from 171p; about +52% from 162.5p), of which 37 points is the multiple and 4 points earnings. Award-led: FY28 EPS 17.5p at 13x to 14x is 230p to 245p before any re-rating toward the cohort's 17x to 18x, so 230p to 300p. Weighted at 55:25:20 the expected 18-month return is about +30% to +35% from 162.5p, with a one-in-five chance of -40%. The expectation is favourable and the distribution is bimodal, which is the correct description of a timing-led case: the reader should not confuse a positive expected return with a predictable one.

So how predictable is the case? Over any one reporting period: low, because one line decides it and the company does not set its date. Over the 18-month total: moderate, because the fundamentals carry forward what a period loses, the balance sheet absorbs the shock, and the customer has demonstrated (March 2025) that a deferred order is a deferred order and not a lost one. The cone narrows materially on one event, a tranche award or a contract notice under the plan, and widens on one event, a Treasury pause; the case is predictable in direction and unpredictable in date, which is what the second set's staircase shape already says.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and the key drivers for monitoring the case over about the next six months?

C50

Question asked How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

The judgement. Helpful in cadence and honest in content, thin in the quantities that decide the thesis, and about to be tested by the first Capital Markets Day. The company gives the Consumer four dated readings in the next six months (a trading update in early November, the Capital Markets Day on 25 November, the interim results on 15 December, the pre-close in April 2027) and publishes the three figures that matter most at each (the order book with the one-off separated, divisional revenue and margins, net debt), which is enough to monitor whether the base case is on track. It does not publish the two figures that would let the Consumer monitor the mechanism rather than the outcome (Ashchurch utilisation and the programme or support share of Systems revenue), nor supplier concentration, nor a numeric guide; the Capital Markets Day is the first scheduled opportunity for those, and the absence of them from it would itself be a reading. Peers' disclosure runs ahead in frequency (discoverIE and XP Power report quarterly orders) and in content (discoverIE's design-win bank; Volex's utilisation KPI; TT's divisional margin targets with dates), so the sector's read-across is at least as useful a monitor as the company's own statements.

What the company discloses, and how to use each item in the next six months.

(1) The November trading update (4 November 2025 and 6 November 2024 in the last two years). It gives the order book at the end of October and a sentence on trading against expectations. Readings: an order book at or above £100m with the one-off separated (it was £96.6m at 31 October 2025 and £102.4m at 31 May 2026) says the base case holds; below £95m says the pipeline is not converting; any mention of a follow-on tranche or a second-half weighting moves the probability on the FY27 tranche directly. The November 2024 update was the warning; the November 2025 update held.

(2) The Capital Markets Day (25 November 2026), the first under the new chief executive and chair and the first since the three-division structure; the company says it will present the "3Cs" strategy and growth ambitions. Readings, in order of value: a utilisation figure or revenue capacity for Ashchurch and the antenna chamber; a dated bridge from 6.2% to the divisional targets, or the fate of the 12% group figure; the share of Systems revenue that is programme, product and through-life support; a capital-allocation framework (a leverage range would quantify the balance sheet's unused option); any FY27 phasing comment. The peer precedents are instructive: Volex's April 2026 event set numeric medium-term targets ($2bn, 12%) and Jefferies raised FY27 and FY28 EBITA by 4% and 6% within days; discoverIE's September 2024 event set a 15% FY28 margin target and the stock still de-rated to 15.6x on the destocking cycle; TT's April 2024 event set a 12% target that operational failure then overwhelmed. A Capital Markets Day that supplies numbers converts the company's disclosure from "specific in structure, vague in arithmetic" (the first set's C-72) to monitorable; one that supplies only frameworks leaves the Consumer monitoring outcomes.

(3) The interim results (15 December 2026). Disclosed: revenue, gross margin and adjusted operating margin by division, order book, net debt, inventory, work in progress is annual only, the interim dividend. The base case's readings are: revenue about £75.5m (down about 12% on £85.7m); gross margin at or above 33.0% (below says the memory lag is biting; above 34% says price is being kept); adjusted operating margin at or above 5.5% (the first set's thesis-at-risk line; the base case has 5.6%); Systems revenue at or above £24m (the recurring run-rate plus CAIN's £8m), with anything above £30m meaning a tranche shipped; Power margin at or above 6% against 5.3%; Components gross margin at or above 26.8%; net debt modestly above £4.2m on inventory and the final dividend, with anything above £8m excluding leases a working-capital warning; an interim dividend increase in the 8% to 12% range (it was 11% last year). Management's commentary on second-half weighting, memory availability and the Defence Investment Plan's conversion is the qualitative read; the company has used language ("may affect the pace of order-book growth") that the Consumer should take literally.

(4) The April 2027 pre-close, the company's habitual upgrade point (it raised FY24 in March 2024 and FY26 in April 2026, and reported "slightly ahead" in April 2025). By then the second-half tranche has either shipped or not, so this is the event at which the FY27 outcome is known and the FY28 number moves.

(5) Between events: regulatory announcements of contracts (the company announced the $10.8m CAIN order and Custom Power's $7.4m of wins; it does not announce every order), Persistent Systems' own press releases (it announced the CAIN selection and the Royal Marines fielding before or alongside the company), Ministry of Defence contract notices under the plan, United States appropriations by 11 December 2026, DRAM contract pricing, and peers' December updates (discoverIE's H1 FY27 results in early December; TT, XP Power and Volex in the same window).

What is not disclosed, and what the Consumer cannot therefore monitor. Ashchurch utilisation (the £15m headroom and £3m overhead are known from presentations, not reported); the programme, product and support split of Systems revenue; the order book by division; supplier concentration and the terms of the Persistent Systems channel; any customer churn or design-win measure; the company's own numeric expectation (expectations are the average of Cavendish, Berenberg and Zeus, which the company names and publishes, so the Consumer monitors the brokers rather than the company); and the identity or contract term of the 17% customer. The company has also used a results platform that allows filtering of live questions, which the first set recorded; the Capital Markets Day's question session will show whether that practice continues.

The helpfulness of the disclosure for the two things that matter. For the H1 FY27 optics the disclosure is good: the comparator is published, the one-off is separated, the H2 weighting has been flagged, and Zeus has modelled a flat year, so an informed reader will not be surprised by a revenue decline. For the FY27 tranche the disclosure is adequate only after the fact: the company will say when a tranche is awarded, and nothing it publishes beforehand (order book, pipeline commentary) distinguishes a tranche that is three months away from one that is nine months away. The Consumer should therefore monitor the Ministry and Persistent Systems for the award and the company for the delivery.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into operational drivers, onto revenue, cost and margin lines, and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to EPS?

C52

Question asked How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

The judgement. The internal chain is clear in structure from the first link to the last and breaks at exactly one point, the quantity of Systems revenue that loads Ashchurch and the price at which it is sold, which is the link the company neither guides nor discloses. Below operating profit the chain is fully traceable and nearly deterministic. The external chain is identifiable at every stage (budget, plan, procurement lane, prime or agency, channel partner, Steatite, Systems revenue, Ashchurch absorption, group margin, EPS) and has two links whose timing is set outside the company and one whose economics are undisclosed (the Persistent Systems channel margin). The memo can therefore show Richard a complete transmission diagram with one box labelled "date set by the Ministry" and one labelled "margin set by the partner", and nothing else hidden.

The internal chain, link by link.

What the company says: "margins broadly stable in FY27 as investment continues ahead of revenue"; "high confidence to exceed next-year market expectations"; divisional medium-term margin targets of 12.5% to 15.0% (Systems), 10.0% to 14.0% (Power), 7.0% to 9.0% (Components); a 2030 ambition of at least 20p of adjusted EPS and £250m of revenue; a long-term incentive band of 11.5p to 16.0p for FY28; capital expenditure over £7.5m across FY27 and FY28; further strategic inventory possible; the "3Ps" (products, projects, programme partnerships) and "3Cs" frameworks; Ashchurch to cover its basic costs in FY27.

Onto drivers: "programmes at scale" and "programme partnerships" mean more Systems revenue in multi-year tranches with a support tail; "products" means own-brand share in Components (Gateway at 40% to 43% gross margin, Durakool, Pacer) and standard product lines in Systems that shorten design cycles; "capabilities with discipline" means Ashchurch, the antenna chamber, Fountain Valley, Power's test rooms; "Made in USA" means Power and antenna revenue through Custom Power and Q-Par USA.

Onto lines: Systems revenue is the recurring base (about £39m) plus programme tranches; each £10m of Systems revenue is worth about £3.9m of gross profit and, with Ashchurch's standing costs already in the base, about £3m of operating profit, or about 190 basis points of group margin; Power revenue at a 36% gross margin and a divisional margin rising from 5.3% toward 10% as Fountain Valley loads; Components at 27% gross margin and 4% to 6% divisional margin, moved by mix not volume; group gross margin 33.5% to 34.2% on mix; adjusted overhead £42.0m growing 4% to 5%; so group operating margin 6.0%, 7.0%, 8.0% on £153m, £165.5m, £178.5m. "Broadly stable" traces to about 6.2% on consensus revenue of £156m and about 6.0% on the base case's £153m, both giving EPS of 10.8p to 11.6p; the divisional targets, if reached on the base case's revenue, trace to a group margin of about 10% to 11% and EPS of about 24p to 26p on £200m, which is why the 20p ambition is reachable at 8% to 9% on £200m and does not need the targets.

Through to EPS: operating profit less net financial items of about £0.9m falling to £0.2m (interest at 2.3% to 2.6% over SONIA on falling drawings, lease interest £0.3m), taxed at 25%, over 57.8m to 58.7m diluted shares, less about 0.1p a year of dilution; reported EPS about 3.6p below adjusted (acquired-intangible amortisation £1.7m, reorganisation £0.5m, share-based payments £0.4m, largely non-deductible). This part of the chain has been forecast accurately for five years and nothing in the next 18 months changes it.

Where the chain breaks: the company gives no utilisation figure, no programme or support share, no order book by division and no numeric guide, so the one quantity that sets the Systems line and therefore the group margin is inferred from the order book, the pipeline language and the Ministry's actions. The chain from "20p by 2030" back to the drivers also lacks a dated bridge (the 12% group figure was dropped from the annual report), which is the Capital Markets Day's job.

The external chain, and where its timing and economics sit. Budget: UK 2.5% of GDP by 2027 and 2.7% by 2029 under the plan of 30 June 2026 (£298bn over four years), NATO members moving to 2.5% to 3.0%, the Strategic Defence Review's 20/40/40 doctrine; cash outlays lag enacted budgets by 12 to 24 months (Jefferies, 2020). Plan to contract: three to six months (Jefferies, May 2026), through the Land Environment Tactical Communication and Information Systems initiative under which CAIN is funded, now with the Segmented Acquisition Model's one-year lane for tactical communications and a National Armaments Director Group that can engage mid-tier suppliers directly; the £28bn gap and the possible spending-review delay sit here. Contract to channel: the Ministry or NSPA selects Persistent Systems' Wave Relay (as it did for CAIN in March 2026), and Steatite as the exclusive UK partner supplies, integrates, trains and supports; Persistent's channel terms, the split of hardware and software value and the support margin are undisclosed, and a direct relationship between Persistent and a ministry would remove the link. Channel to lines: Systems revenue at about 38.7% gross margin (an integrator's margin; the radio maker keeps the product margin), programme tranches of £8m to £23m, follow-on upgrades (the WaRTAK precedent: $14.6m then $7m six years later). Lines to EPS: as above. The parallel chains for Power (autonomy doctrine and United States appropriations to Tier 1 primes to Custom Power to Fountain Valley utilisation) and Components (industrial PMIs and distributor inventories to Solsta to a 4% to 6% margin) are shorter and better dated but smaller.

The cost side of the external chain is also clear: AI data-centre demand to memory and mature-node allocation to DRAM contract prices (up about 60% then 13% to 18% in successive quarters of 2026) to Steatite's and Solsta's bills of materials, recovered through back-to-back pricing and non-cancellable orders with a one to two quarter lag, at the cost of deferred programmes where customers refuse the new price; and sterling to about 40% of purchases, unhedged, at ±£0.7m to £0.8m of pre-tax profit per 10%.

The clarity, summarised for the memo. The company's words trace to the numbers at every step except the quantity and date of Systems programme revenue; the value chain's dynamics trace to EPS at every step except the Ministry's date and the partner's margin; the memo can present the transmission as one diagram with two undated boxes and should say that the November and December events can fill the first box (a utilisation figure, a bridge) but not the second (the award), which the Ministry fills.

D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically, and so how far is the multiple company-driven?

C60

Question asked To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

The judgement. Almost entirely idiosyncratic, and more so now than at any point in two years. Over the latest six-month window (27 April to 22 October 2026) the share's daily returns have a correlation of 0.10 with the equal-weighted basket of its fifteen dashboard peers, a beta of 0.21 and an R-squared of 1%: the basket explains one percent of the stock's variance and the company's own news, or the absence of it, explains the other 99%. Over the 24 month-end readings the R-squared never exceeded 12% (the November 2025 and April 2026 windows), and in the windows ending November 2024 to March 2025 the beta was negative (-0.7 to -1.1) because the share collapsed on the deferral while the basket rose. The multiple tells the same story from the other side: the stock's 24-month-forward price-earnings ratio is 11.07x at the first percentile of its own five-year range (median 15.7x), while the cohort's ratings have risen with the restock and AI narratives (discoverIE 17.3x at the 62nd percentile of its ten-year history, Volex 14.5x at the 92nd, AQ Group 22.3x at the 76th, TT Electronics 10.0x at the 53rd, Huber+Suhner 17.2x at the 13th). The sector re-rated and Solid State de-rated, with estimates flat; the de-rating is the company's, and it is made of two things, the timing fear after the November 2024 precedent and the illiquidity of an AIM share trading about £0.3m a day.

What the co-movement record says about the mechanism. The six-month windows with the highest co-movement (September 2025 to April 2026, correlation 0.23 to 0.35) coincide with the period in which Solid State's news was positive and sector-aligned (the CAIN award in October 2025, strong interims in December, the pre-close upgrade in April 2026, and the cohort's recovery); the windows with the lowest or negative co-movement coincide with company-specific shocks in either direction (the November 2024 deferral; the three months to October 2026 in which the share fell 12.2% while the basket rose 7.9%). Within the basket no single peer explains anything: the highest pairwise correlations are SFS Group (0.17) and Senior (0.14), neither of which shares an end market with Solid State, and the closest business comparators (discoverIE 0.07, TT Electronics 0.02, Volex 0.07) are as unrelated as the rest. The event work in this set confirms the same pattern from the price history: the November 2024 cut (share to 210p then 123p), the March 2025 reinstatement (175p, the multiple falling from 33x to 18x on raised numbers), the October 2025 CAIN award (no estimate change, a 15% discount to peers maintained), the April 2026 pre-close (+18% to 165p in a day), the June 2026 results (to about 199p) and the autumn 2026 pullback (to 162.5p on no estimate change) each moved the share by 15% to 40% with no corresponding move in the basket. The October 2026 rotation out of AI and hardware names that took Volex and discoverIE down 6% in a day did not register.

What this means for the multiple, and for the fair multiple. The multiple is company-driven. Three consequences follow. First, a sector re-rating gives Solid State no lift and a sector de-rating does it little harm; the memo's peer multiples (17x to 29x for the quality names, 18.2x for the UK defence and industrial technology average that Zeus cites) are a ceiling the stock has never reached in five years and not a gravitational pull. Second, the stock's own history is the right frame for the fair multiple: the ten-year bands (10.9x crash, 13.1x conservative, 14.1x prudent, 16.2x defendable, 18.4x mania) and the five-year range (10.5x to 23.3x); the guideline fair multiple of 12.7x sits between crash and conservative, which is the correct phase for a stock whose next two events can cut or confirm estimates, and the model's 18-month return of +44% is 37 points of multiple recovery from the first percentile to below the 35th and only 4 points of earnings. Third, because the multiple responds to company events and to nothing else, the two dated events (the Capital Markets Day and the interim) and the one undated event (the award) will move the multiple directly and by the full amount of the information they carry; there is no sector tide to float the stock while it waits, and no sector ebb to blame if it falls.

The liquidity point. With about 117,000 shares (about £0.2m to £0.3m) traded a day and one visible broker on FactSet, moves of 10% to 20% on no news are normal for this register, and the three-month fall of 12.2% with estimates unchanged fits the pattern of small-cap outflows the first set's C-82 described. This technical idiosyncrasy is part of the 99%, and it cuts both ways: it is why the multiple is at the first percentile and why a confirmed tranche could move the price 20% to 30% on little volume. The consolidation of accredited electronics niches (discoverIE's Trival and 3Gmetalworx at about 11x to 12x EBITA, Diploma's CDM, the recommended cash offer for Gooch & Housego at 25.9x adjusted operating profit and a 41% premium) puts a floor under the multiple that the co-movement data cannot see, because it acts on the company's value rather than on its daily price.

For predictability. An idiosyncratic price is a double-edged finding. It means the forecasts translate into the price without sector noise, so if the model is right the return follows; and it means the price will carry the full weight of any forecast error, so the 18-month cone of D-04 is the price cone. For the fair-multiple topic the finding is favourable: the multiple is low for company-specific reasons that company-specific events can reverse, and the room to the stock's own median is 40%.

Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

The Investment Analyst's heading The fourth set: Assessing the extent to which our assessment is reflected in current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative

The fourth set as a whole C53 written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The gap between our assessment and what guidance, the sell side and the narrative already reflect is small and unfavourable for the next six months and larger and favourable for the twelve months after that. Guidance, which is the three-house average the company adopts, has FY27 at 11.6p where our probability-weighted figure is 10.3p and our base path 10.8p; the sell side has not modelled the second-half dependence, the deferral case or the catch-up, and revises only at four points a year and only on signed orders, so the first revision after the interim is more likely down than up and the first after the April 2027 pre-close more likely up than down; and the narrative, which is one broker's, articulates the comparator and the discount and leaves unarticulated the arithmetic that makes the case (leverage both ways, the tranche probability, the support tail, net cash) and the arithmetic that threatens it (the dollar, working capital, the Treasury's gap behind a published plan). The medium-term markers (the 2030 ambition, the divisional targets, the LTIP band) are known in headline and unexplained in path, and the Capital Markets Day is the first scheduled chance to explain them; the peer precedents say a numeric event (Volex) moves consensus within days and a framework event (discoverIE, TT) leaves the shares to the cycle and to execution. For the memo: topic #4 should say guidance is honest, conservative in level and silent on timing; topic #7 should say the scope for upgrades is in FY28 and FY29, not FY27, and depends on one contract and one presentation; topic #8 should say momentum in guidance, estimates, narrative and price is currently nil or negative by construction and that the peers' momentum (discoverIE, Volex, XP Power) is in a narrative Solid State does not share; and the summary should say that the market has priced the fear of a slip without having been told the size of the prize if there is no slip, which is the configuration in which a confirmed tranche produces both an estimate upgrade and a re-rating at once.

Guidance

1 question
I-0150
I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events, and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, the same for the 18-month to three-year markers.

C50

Question asked How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

The judgement. The company issues no numeric guidance; "market expectations" is the average of Cavendish, Berenberg and Zeus, which the company names and speaks to, so "guidance" here means the company's statement at each event that trading is ahead of, in line with or below that average. On that definition, over the eight events of the next 18 months, the FY27 expectation (revenue about £156m, adjusted pre-tax profit £8.8m to £9.0m, EPS 11.6p) is more likely to be reduced than raised: I put the probabilities at raised 20%, met or maintained 40%, reduced 40%, with the most likely reduction a modest phasing cut at the December interim or the April pre-close rather than an FY25-type collapse, and the most likely raise at the April 2027 pre-close after a second-half tranche has shipped. The FY28 expectation (revenue £159m to £162m, EPS 12.7p to 13.1p) is more likely to be raised than reduced (raised 50%, met 25%, reduced 25%), because it sits below the input forces, below the base case (14.0p) and below management's own incentive midpoint (13.75p), and because the Capital Markets Day is the natural point for the company to put a number on FY28 or FY29 for the first time. Secondarily, the 2030 ambition (20p, £250m) is most likely to be restated unchanged at the Capital Markets Day (65%), with a one-in-four chance that it is reframed around revenue and programmes without a margin path (which the market would read as a reduction), and a one-in-ten chance that a dated interim target is added (a raise in effect); the group 12% margin figure has already been withdrawn and its replacement by the divisional targets is the reduction that has happened; the long-term incentive band (11.5p to 16.0p for FY28) will not change.

The events, in order, with what drives each outcome.

(1) Trading update, early November 2026. Met (60%): an order book around £100m with the one-off separated and "in line with expectations"; this is what the November 2025 update did. Reduced (25%): a statement that second-half weighting has increased or that a programme has been deferred on component cost or Ministry timing; the November 2024 precedent, and the 6 October 2026 reports that the Treasury may delay the spending review, are the drivers. Raised (15%): a follow-on tranche or an NSPA framework order named in the update; the company announces orders of this size by regulatory news, so the update is more likely to confirm one already announced than to reveal one.

(2) Capital Markets Day, 25 November 2026. This event does not move FY27 but it sets the medium-term marker. Restated (65%): the 2030 ambition, the divisional targets and a qualitative bridge; Zeus's view after its October site visit ("the opportunity to expand operating margins is material") is the house broker's framing of it. Reframed (25%): a revenue-led 2030 without a margin, or the divisional targets re-dated beyond 2030; the driver is the same caution that dropped the 12% figure from the annual report. Added (10%): a dated FY28 or FY29 group margin, a utilisation target for Ashchurch, or a capital-allocation framework with a leverage range; the Volex precedent (April 2026, $2bn and 12%, followed by broker upgrades within days) is the template management is most likely to have studied, and the chair's background at Thales UK argues for numbers rather than frameworks.

(3) Interim results, 15 December 2026. Met (50%): revenue about £75m to £77m, operating margin 5.5% to 6%, order book near £100m, "full-year expectations unchanged, second-half weighted"; this is what the December 2024 and December 2025 interims did. Reduced (35%): an operating margin below 5.5% on under-absorption, or management saying the second-half tranche is now expected in FY28; the sell side would then cut FY27 and FY28 together, as in November 2024. Raised (15%): a tranche already shipped or contracted for the second half with a stated value.

(4) Pre-close, April 2027. This is the event at which FY27 is known. Raised (35%): the company's habit (March 2024, April 2026) when the second half delivers; the base case's £77.5m second half with a weighted tranche is consistent with "ahead of expectations" if the tranche is larger than £5m. Met (35%). Reduced (30%): the tranche slipped into FY28 and the company says so; management rebases honestly (it did in November 2024 and then beat the rebased figure).

(5) FY27 results, late June 2027: the company's upgrade point for the following year (June 2026 lifted FY27 by 12.5%). Raised for FY28 (50%), on utilisation evidence and the first NSPA or CAIN follow-ons; met (30%); reduced (20%).

(6) AGM statement, September 2027; (7) trading update, November 2027; (8) interim results, December 2027: the FY28 first half will carry whatever slipped from FY27, so these events skew to met or raised unless a second deferral occurs.

What drives the probabilities, stated plainly. Toward "reduced" in the next six months: the second-half dependence on a tranche whose contracting began only with the plan's publication on 30 June 2026 and whose lag is three to six months; the Treasury's £28bn gap and the possible spending-review delay; memory inflation that can make a programme "temporarily unviable"; the United States continuing resolution to 11 December 2026 for Custom Power; a fixed base that converts a £5m revenue slip into £1.5m of profit. Toward "met": an order book that covers two-thirds of the year, the CAIN order already shipped, Power's intake in hand, a management that sets a low bar and has never issued a number it did not then meet except once. Toward "raised" in months six to 18: the Segmented Acquisition Model's one-year lane, the plan naming CAIN, Zeus's own description of its FY28 growth of 2.6% as "prudently modelled with scope for upgrade", the LTIP midpoint above consensus, and the habit of raising at the pre-close and the results.

Where our assessment sits against guidance. Below it for FY27 (10.8p against 11.6p, a probability-weighted 10.3p), above it for FY28 and FY29 (14.0p and 17.7p against 13.1p and 16.0p), and in line with the 2030 ambition in direction but a year late in date. The gap is unfavourable for the next two events and favourable from the April 2027 pre-close onward, which is why the score sits at the midpoint: guidance is neither sandbagged enough to make the near term safe nor stretched enough to make the medium term risky.
